\section{The invariant catalogue}

These are the properties the design is willing to be judged on. Where an invariant has a
mechanism, the mechanism is named; where it is only prose, that is stated, because the project's
own doctrine is \emph{"prefer a mechanism to a sentence — a rule stated only in prose has no
reader."}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.0cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{9.2cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.5cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Invariant} & \textbf{Statement} & \textbf{Enforced by} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Invariant} & \textbf{Statement} & \textbf{Enforced by} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Order-inde\-pendence &
  Everything derived is a pure function of declared protocol facts, never of arrival order.
  "Protocol, not trace." &
  Permutation/interleave/dup proptests over the whole observable end state
  (\file{tests/tests/deter\-minism.rs}). \\
\addlinespace
MISS = FAULT &
  Tables are forward-populated only; a lookup that misses is a loud fault, never a guess.
  \textbf{Refined:} a \textasciitilde28-site audit found the absolute had a second, correct
  answer — the real rule is decided \emph{per site}: \emph{not yet knowable $\Rightarrow$
  DEFER; never knowable $\Rightarrow$ FAULT}. A mapping arriving before its PDB bind defers; a
  handle resolving to the wrong \emph{kind} faults. Getting it wrong is asymmetric: fault-when-
  it-should-defer hangs the guest, defer-when-it-should-fault is a security question. &
  \code{AddressTable} has no reverse-resolve path to call.
  \file{tests/tests/miss\_taxonomy.rs} makes the taxonomy executable.
  \emph{No shipped site needed a behaviour change; three \textbf{doc} claims did, two of them
  stating the literal opposite of their own code.} \\
\addlinespace
Per-target keying &
  \code{Pdb} and \code{VChid} are per-GPU namespaces. Cross-GPU identical ids are legal;
  same-target duplicates are loud collisions. &
  \code{project()}; \file{tests/tests/multi\_gpu.rs}. \\
\addlinespace
Per-\code{Proc} isolation (I1) &
  Identical guest VAs and handles in two processes reach disjoint arenas, disjoint host address
  spaces and disjoint isolates \emph{by construction}. &
  Types (\code{GpaSpace::release} by value; \code{HostHandle} carries its \code{IsolateId}) plus
  a proptest against an injective oracle. \\
\addlinespace
One gated ring path &
  A doorbell can only reach the host after the \#14 working-set gate. &
  \textbf{Now structural, not a call-graph claim.} \code{VerbPlan::Doorbell} is
  \code{\#[non\_exhaustive]}, so its only constructor is \code{VerbPlan::gated\_doorbell}, which
  runs the gate; an ungated one is compile error E0639, pinned by a \code{trybuild} row. The
  cardinality claim corrected in \S\ref{sec:corrections} is what this replaced. \\
\addlinespace
\textbf{GSP-S1} &
  \textbf{We may never emit an element run wider than the guest's staging buffer.} At driver 580
  the guest reads our \code{elemCount} field directly into an unbounded \code{portMemCopy} loop
  whose staging area is exactly \code{queueElementSizeMax / queueElementSizeMin} elements — 16 on
  the bench — with the live \code{msgq} metadata as the very next byte. An \code{elemCount} of 17
  overwrites the metadata the guest is actively using; at the ring's reachable maximum it writes
  188\,416 bytes past a \code{portMemAllocNonPaged} allocation. This is a \emph{guest kernel heap}
  corruption reachable from a value we choose. &
  \textbf{★ PAID OFF AT THIS REVISION} (\code{250b78c}), and it was the item the previous revision
  named as this project's highest-stakes prose-only rule. \code{max\_elements} is derived from the
  layout, \code{encode\_message} refuses \code{GspFault::\allowbreak ElementCountOutOfRange} before
  building a run, \emph{unconditionally} rather than gated on the layout's field, and the receive side
  range-checks the guest's declared count and cross-checks it against the derivation
  (\code{ElementCountMismatch}), leaving the cursor unmoved on refusal. \emph{With one honest
  footnote:} the originally briefed bite could not fire, because at a dividing element size the
  length bound already implies the count bound — the count bound is independently reachable only
  at a non-dividing \code{msgSize}, which is real guest-chosen input, and the test moved there. \\
\addlinespace
Completion integrity (I2) &
  A completion fires only from a genuinely-armed source in the \emph{owning} process, at most
  once; the system forge path is typed so it cannot reach a user process's queue. &
  Type-level (\code{Traffic::System}) plus a differential against an independent reference
  fence model. \\
\addlinespace
Refcount soundness (I3) &
  A resource is alive $\iff$ it has a live handle or map reference. No premature destroy, no
  leak, dup survives origin free. &
  Proptest over deep trees with cross-client dups and interior frees. \\
\addlinespace
DoS containment (I4) &
  \code{Gpu::apply} is transactional; every guest-growable table is capacity-bounded with a loud
  refusal, never OOM. &
  \textbf{Corrected, and the correction is in the design doc:} "no $O(n^2)$ path on hostile
  floods" is \textbf{false and was measured}. The caps bound \emph{memory}, not \emph{time}
  — see \S\ref{sec:discussion}. \\
\addlinespace
\textbf{R1} &
  No blocking host verb — indeed no potentially-blocking syscall — may be issued while any ranked
  lock is held. Blocking calls take \textbf{zero} ranked locks. &
  \textbf{★★★ This is the invariant that was BROKEN on the production path, and the suite could
  not see it} (\S\ref{sec:r1}). \code{assert\_lock\_free} guards the backend doors, and a
  \code{Drop} is a call site — one violation was exactly an \code{Arc} ownership issue in a
  \code{Drop} (\#57). But the spawn of an isolate is also a blocking call, it ran under rank 0,
  and \textbf{1\,975 green tests could not reach the assert} because the suite spawns through a
  mock. The repair moved the assert to \code{IsolateBox::new} — the one door every plane shares —
  which turns the whole mock-driven suite into a live guard. \\
\addlinespace
R3 &
  Locks are acquired in strictly increasing rank, at most one per rank. &
  \code{check\_acquire}, always-on, panics before the OS acquire. \\
\addlinespace
R5 &
  After re-acquiring, re-validate everything the plan assumed. &
  The four \code{commit\_*} functions; bounded re-plan at
  \code{MAX\_COMMIT\_RETRIES = 8}. \\
\addlinespace
F1 &
  Three unrelated things are called F1 in this project: a fixed threat-model bug, the per-GPU
  collision guard, and the reactor's bounded-poll rule. &
  All three real; the overloading is a documentation hazard, not a code one. \\
\addlinespace
GL1--GL13 &
  The guest-memory-lock invariants. &
  \textbf{Documentation only. Zero occurrences in any \code{.rs} file.} They have no port to be
  enforced at, because capability group 8 was removed from the \code{Vmm} trait. Treat as a
  designed, unimplemented subsystem. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}}

\subsection{The two-layer trust model}
\label{sec:trust}

One normative rule deserves its own space, because it decides designs and because it is the
cleanest statement the project has produced:

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{Layer 1 (trap + lock) gives SECURITY against a guest that ignores the protocol.
Layer 2 (NVIDIA's own synchronisation points) gives CORRECTNESS for a guest that follows it.
Shared tables are NEVER authoritative for an immediate decision.}
\hfill \src{docs/design/core\_security\_threat\_model.md \S2.1}
\end{claimbox}

Neither layer is a weaker version of the other. Layer 2 alone is not security: a hostile guest
can update a shared table \emph{after} validation and \emph{before} we act, and it can fake the
handshake entirely. Layer 1 alone is not correctness: a trap says the bytes cannot change while
you look; it does not say they are \emph{finished}. A cooperating guest halfway through
publishing a page-directory update has written something internally inconsistent and perfectly
stable, and reading it under a perfect lock yields a perfectly-locked wrong answer.

The consequence — \emph{a shared table is evidence, never authority} — is why the address table
is forward-populated and why a miss is a fault.

\textbf{And the C's measurement sharpened it in a way worth carrying.} The Layer-2 edge is
\emph{whichever} edge the protocol commits at, not a favourite one. On the kernel/UVM/RM paths
that edge is the TLB invalidate. On the GSP-emulated compute path it is \emph{not}: round 6 of
the \#14 investigation measured \textbf{zero} occurrences of both invalidate transports there.
A design that had encoded "read at the invalidate" as \emph{the} Layer-2 rule would have had no
synchronisation point at all on the path that matters most. The general rule survived; one of
its instances did not.

\textbf{What the C artifact does not have is Layer 1.} There is no lock of any kind between QEMU
and an isolate in a system that runs 22 real GPU applications at host parity. It ships a
copy-once snapshot instead, and the reasoning is written out in its source. The working system
to date has been running on Layer 2 alone — which is correct for a cooperating guest and is not
a security property.

\textbf{And the boundary points both ways, which building the GSP made concrete.} Everything
above is about not trusting the guest. \textbf{GSP-S1} is the mirror: the guest kernel is inside
the boundary we are defending, and the faked GSP is a device that can corrupt guest kernel memory
from a width \emph{we} choose. The mechanism is fully traced in
\file{mode2\_gsp\_port\_plan.md} \S4.6 and reduces to one sentence — at driver 580 the guest reads
our \code{elemCount} field into a copy loop bounded by nothing but the ring, and the byte after
its staging area is the \code{msgq} metadata it is actively using, so the corruption is
immediately self-amplifying. It is the same defect class as the C artifact's unguarded
\code{\% s->q\_msgcount} \code{SIGFPE}, pointed at the guest instead of at QEMU. The obvious
objection — "\emph{the guest is the victim, so it is the guest's bug}" — is answered in the plan
and the answer is that it is both. \textbf{At the previous revision this invariant was written
down and not enforced, and this paper called that its most security-relevant sentence-rather-than-
mechanism. It is now a mechanism} (\code{250b78c}; see the GSP-S1 row above and
\S\ref{sec:instruments} for why the bite that proved it is not the bite that was predicted). The
general point survives the specific repair: the boundary points both ways, and a faked device is
a device.

\textbf{★ "The suite runs in about 20 seconds" has been deleted rather than updated.} It was

(\file{scripts/run\_full\_suite.sh}), whose mutation phase alone ran 33\,374\,s on its last
outing. The OS-free property the 20-second figure was really claiming — no GPU, no OS
dependency, no wall clock — does survive, and is still what makes the adversarial suites
affordable on every change.

\subsection{The CI gates --- and the gate that GitHub cannot be}

There is one workflow file with six jobs, and the \code{stable} job grew from fourteen steps to
\textbf{twenty-four}. Seven of the nine new ones are \emph{reached-count} floors for oracle
families that only exist on a box with the vendored NVIDIA trees, plus an
\emph{ABI-quarantine} gate, an \emph{ogkm-version-tag} gate (every NVIDIA citation names its
tree) and \textbf{the claim-ledger gate} described below.

\textbf{The bridge-exclusivity gate fired on its first run}, on \code{kayfabe-gsp}'s own stale
doc paragraph claiming the bridge would live in \code{kayfabe-fwd}. That is worth more than the
gate itself: a gate that has never been observed to fire is not evidence, and this one earned its
keep before it was five minutes old.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ GitHub CI is opportunistic convenience. It is not the definition of green}, and the
project says so in an owner ruling rather than leaving it implied: \emph{"this project cannot
rely on gh for iterating for free hardware \dots\ ensure whole suite can still be run if you have
real gpu."} The authoritative run is \file{scripts/run\_full\_suite.sh} on real hardware, whose
exit 0 means \emph{everything this box can run, ran}.

\smallskip
\textbf{The failure it is written against is this paper's own founding doctrine.} \textbf{A test
that is COUNTED but never PASSES is worse than a missing test.} There are two live instances and
both are honest about it: the 51 \file{/dev/kvm} tests and the 10 VBIOS-oracle tests are counted
on GitHub and never run there, because \code{ubuntu-latest} has neither the device nor the
vendored trees. Both print a marker on the \emph{skipping} arm, straight to stderr, deliberately
bypassing libtest's capture — because capture swallows output on the \textbf{passing} path, which
is the arm that matters. ★ And the runner does not know those families by name: it
\textbf{derives} them from the markers a run emits, so on a box whose profile promised the
capability a single \code{SKIPPED} is a red run. \emph{A gate quantified over a hand-list is a
smaller true statement.}
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{The claim ledger --- a gate on the paper's own vocabulary}
\label{sec:ledger}

The most unusual instrument in the repository, and the one this document is itself held to.
\file{scripts/claim\_ledger.py} sweeps every tracked Rust source and design document, finds every
prose \emph{block} making an epistemic claim (\emph{measured}, \emph{observed}, \emph{verified},
\emph{confirmed}, \emph{proven}), and asks one question of each: \textbf{does the block name what
is behind the claim?} It classifies into \measured{} / \inferred{} / \assumed{} /
\textsc{unattributed}, and enforces three bars: \textbf{381 unattributed (a ceiling), 66 conflated
(exact), 17 bare-hardware (exact)}.

\textbf{The middle class is the named failure mode.} \textsc{conflated} means a block says
\emph{measured} or \emph{proven} and the only thing behind it is a \emph{citation into source} —
\code{ogkm}, nvproxy, or the C artifact. \emph{Reading the open kernel modules tells you what the
driver does. Only a live boot with real work tells you what happens.} The bar is exact in both
directions, because a gate that only catches growth leaves slack a later claim can be added into.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★ Four things this gate says about itself, and they are the reason to trust it.}
\textbf{(i)} \textbf{The universe is derived, never listed} — \code{git ls-files} plus
untracked-but-not-ignored files, after a measured incident where a pre-commit run read 383 with
three new modules unstaged and 385 the moment they were added. \textbf{(ii)} \textbf{It cannot
check that the evidence exists}, that it says what the claim says, or that it was ever run: a
block citing an imaginary capture passes. \emph{Adjudicating a citation is human work} — and
\S\ref{sec:oraclelimits} is the worked example of a citation gate being satisfied by a source
that said nothing. \textbf{(iii)} \textbf{The unattributed bar is a ceiling and is deliberately
weaker than the other two}, "a worse gate honestly described, not a better one described
optimistically", because an exact pin across a tree several agents write into at once turns an
unrelated doc commit red — \emph{which is how a ratchet decays into a formality.}
\textbf{(iv)} \textbf{Two of its four firings were false positives and both moved the pattern,
not the bar.} One site cited \code{ogkm} and then said, in the same block, \emph{"No Ada card was
measured for this number"} — the exact honesty the gate exists to encourage, scored as the defect
it exists to catch. ⊘ \emph{Absorbing that site into the bar would have been the cheaper edit and
the wrong one: a gate that penalises the behaviour it is for teaches people to stop writing it
down.}
\end{claimbox}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ And at this pin the gate is RED, which the previous revision could report as green and
this one cannot.} \measured{} here, \code{scripts/claim\_ledger.py --gate} at \code{5c367a38},
\textbf{exit 1}:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrl@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{class} & \textbf{count} & \textbf{bar} & \\
\midrule
\textsc{unattributed} & 1\,166 & 381 & ceiling — \textbf{3.1$\times$ over} \\
\quad\textsc{conflated} & 90 & 66 & exact \\
\quad\textsc{bare-hardware} & 36 & 17 & exact \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{@{}l@{}}{\emph{for scale}: \measured{} 2\,167 \quad \inferred{} 857 \quad
\assumed{} 136} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\smallskip
\textbf{The bars have not been touched since \code{1227babe}, 2026-07-31}, while the tree tripled
and roughly forty directories of committed bench artifacts (\file{traces/**/*.md}) entered the
gate's universe — which is derived from \code{git ls-files}, so committing a trace README
containing the word \emph{measured} moves the number with no source change at all.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{Two readings, and this paper will not choose between them without a measurement it did
not take.} Either the tree really has accumulated 785 new unattributed claims, or the gate's
universe grew in a way its bar was never re-derived for. \textbf{Both are failures of the same
kind} — a ratchet whose denominator moves is not a ratchet — and the project's own doctrine names
the second one exactly: \emph{"a gate quantified over a hand-list is a smaller true statement"},
here inverted into \emph{a gate quantified over a growing derived set is a bar that means
something different every week}. What is \emph{not} in doubt is that the authoritative local gate
run (\file{scripts/ci\_gates.sh --all}) cannot currently reach a clean exit.
\end{warnbox}

⊘ \textbf{And one honest note that has not changed}: \code{.tex} is not in the gate's universe —
it sweeps \code{*.rs} and \code{*.md} — so \textbf{the whitepaper is the one artifact in this
repository the claim ledger does not police}, which is worth knowing before trusting any
\emph{measured} in it more than the design document it came from.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.5cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Job} & \textbf{When} & \textbf{What it asserts} \\
\midrule
\code{stable} & every push + PR &
  \textbf{Twenty-four steps}: build; \textbf{test with KVM forced absent}; \emph{six}
  reached-count floors (KVM, sandbox, VBIOS-oracle, GMMU-oracle, token-oracle,
  pushbuffer-oracle, USERD-chid-oracle); clippy \code{-D warnings}; \code{fmt --check}; the
  hexagonal-boundary grep; the VMM-vocabulary grep; \textbf{the generation-name grep} (no
  concrete chip or driver version in the logic crates); \textbf{the ABI-quarantine gate} (a C
  layout is quarantined, or provably own-wire); the \code{unsafe}-surface \code{ls} gate; the
  host-pointer gate; the GPA-accessor gate; the bridge-exclusivity gate; \textbf{the
  ogkm-version-tag gate} (every NVIDIA citation names its tree, ratcheted at 161 untagged);
  \textbf{the claim-ledger gate}; three unsafe-containment asserts including the
  \textbf{two-crate ratchet at 91 and 49}; and \code{fmt --check} for the fuzz workspace. \\
\code{aarch64} & every push + PR & \code{cargo check --workspace --target
  aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu} \\
\code{nightly-fuzz} & every push + PR & the fuzz harness \emph{compiles} \\
\code{slow} & nightly & the full suite with \code{KAYFABE\_SLOW=1} \\
\code{tsan} & nightly & ThreadSanitizer over four threaded targets, \code{-Zbuild-std} so std's
  own synchronisation is instrumented \\
\code{mutants} & weekly & \code{cargo mutants} over every production crate \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★ Two of the steps in that table are RED on \code{master} and have been for at least a
week, and the previous revision of this paper listed them as things CI asserts.}
\code{cargo fmt --all --check} fails on \textbf{40 files} (\measured{} here) and
\code{cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings} fails with \textbf{5 errors}
(recorded at \code{72f902f}, not re-run here). The consequence is stated in the project's own
words: \emph{"\code{ci\_gates.sh --all} runs both, so the authoritative local gate run cannot
currently reach a clean exit for a reason that has nothing to do with any rung's change."}

\smallskip
★ \textbf{And the \code{fmt} red is not attributable, which is the more interesting half.}
\file{rust-toolchain.toml} pins \code{channel = "stable"} — a \emph{moving} channel, not a version
— so what "formatted" means changes underneath the repository whenever the toolchain updates, and
the bench notes record this exact toolchain \emph{"reformatting 62 files of untouched master"}.
⊘ This paper therefore cannot say whether 40 files drifted or \code{rustfmt} did. \textbf{A gate
that can go red with no commit is a gate whose red carries no information}, and it is the same
species as everything in \S\ref{sec:drift}: not a wrong statement, a statement whose subject
moved.
\end{warnbox}


\subsection{Exercised versus merely present --- the project's own core doctrine}

This distinction is the most useful thing in the repository, and it is worth reproducing the
incident that generated it, because it is the template for every criticism in
\S\ref{sec:discussion}.

\begin{warnbox}\small
The conservation ledger's first census over the mean run reported \textbf{0 leaked objects} —
and that was a \textbf{true negative, not a pass}. A design document claimed the workload
"exercises T0 thousands of times per run"; the churn it actually drives adds and removes
\emph{RPC} bindings, which own nothing host-side, and the script's two real subset-frees targeted
channels that phase 0 deliberately leaves virgin. \textbf{The path had never been reached.}
Reaching it took a new test phase. Once reached, the honest baseline was \textbf{24 objects, 6
mappings and 24\,576 GPA bytes leaking, linear in frees.}
\hfill \src{docs/design/testing\_doctrine.md \S1}
\end{warnbox}

Three rules follow, and the project applies them: a reclamation test that does not first
allocate proves nothing; every instrument needs a non-vacuity assertion (a case where it is
known to read non-zero); and \textbf{bite-check the instrument, not only the fix} — reverting the
fix must move the number, and if it does not, the number is not measuring the fix.

Bite-checking is real practice rather than aspiration, and the evidence is that the repository
records bite-checks that \emph{did not bite}: at least six such notes are written into the
source at the site they concern, each explaining what the neutering failed to move and what was
added as a result. One has been promoted into a permanent test that plants a gap and a repeat in
a trace and requires the order checker to fire. A commit message from this week reads, in part,
\emph{"the bite harness itself lied twice."}

The doctrine also produces two mechanisms directly. Slow tests are gated by
\code{KAYFABE\_SLOW}, and when unset they print \code{SKIPPED (slow): \dots} \textbf{straight to
stderr}, bypassing libtest's capture; nothing in the suite is \code{\#[ignore]}d, on the argument
that \code{\#[ignore]} is invisible in a summary and impossible to count. And the KVM gate prints
on \emph{both} arms — \code{KVM-GATE: RAN} or \code{KVM-GATE: SKIPPED} — with CI asserting a
floor on the sum, because the reverse failure (all 36 gated tests silently vanishing while CI
stays green) is equally silent.

\subsection{The mean test, and composed versus isolated runs}

\file{tests/tests/l1\_mean.rs} is the largest single test file (35 tests) and is designated
\emph{the arbiter}. Its standing is: \emph{pass = the design survived contact; fail = the doc
changes, not the assert.} The rule it exists to enforce is an owner directive with four
obligations — drive realistic multi-process $\times$ multi-thread $\times$ multi-GPU traffic end
to end through the mocks and assert the observable end state; make it mean rather than happy-path
(free mid-flight, race a re-declaration against a still-publishing generation, kill a worker
partway, run both lock modes); wire every new milestone into it; and \textbf{never narrow a test
to make it pass}.

The justification is measured, not asserted. One identity round landed 7 bite-checks; \textbf{none
of the 363 pre-existing tests caught any of them}. Every real defect that day came from a composed
run or a newly sharpened criterion. In the doctrine's words: \emph{"the happy path found nothing,
all day."}

A representative finding: a test named
\code{worker\_death\_retires\_the\_proc\_loudly\_and\_never\_resurrects} was green — and green
\emph{only because it never issued an apply after the HUP}. The composed run put a worker hangup
and an alloc/map-heavy workload in the same run and found that an out-of-band retire is undone by
the next refresh: a guest able to crash its isolate worker got a \textbf{clean new isolate} on its
next RM event. The first fix for the related leak class was a use-after-free of its own making.

\subsection{The conservation ledger}

The instrument all eight teardown paths are measured by. \code{HostLedger} replays the recorded
verb log and demands every acquisition matched by exactly one release and nothing released that
was never acquired — objects \emph{and} mappings. A test may declare a \code{ResidueClaim} for
what it knowingly leaves outstanding, and the counts are compared \textbf{for equality, not as a
bound}: \emph{less} residue than declared also fails, so a fix that closes a leak must delete the
claim that excused it. There is deliberately no \code{allow\_leaks} escape hatch.

Its stated limit, recorded in the source: the ledger increments only on \emph{success}, so it
says nothing about failed verbs. That is why a separate \code{VerbFailure\{err, orphans\}} type
exists to carry handles out of a chain that died partway.

\subsection{The mutation gate --- \texorpdfstring{\textbf{five}}{five} lies deep, and the fifth is the best one}
\label{sec:mutgate}

The gate mutates the code and measures whether the suite notices. It has now been the most
valuable instrument in the project \emph{and} silently wrong \textbf{five} separate times, every
one of them wrong \emph{upward}. That asymmetry is not coincidence and it is the single most
important thing to understand about this instrument: every way an instrument like this fails
makes the code look better than it is, because a mutant that is not run, not built or not counted
is a mutant that never survives.

\textbf{Lie 1 — it tested nothing.} \code{kayfabe-fwd} and \code{kayfabe-rt} have no unit tests
of their own; without \code{--test-workspace true}, cargo-mutants ran an empty suite and reported
everything missed. (This one is the exception that proves the rule — it failed \emph{downward},
and was therefore noticed immediately.)

\textbf{Lie 2 — compiler crashes vanished from the denominator.} cargo-mutants rewrites one file
per mutant in a copied tree that reuses one incremental dep-graph cache, and that cache does not
survive the churn — rustc ICEs. cargo-mutants cannot tell a compiler crash from a type error, so
it files the mutant \emph{unviable} and \textbf{silently removes it from the denominator}.
Measured: \textbf{136 of 293 mutant builds ICE'd}, turning a real 88.95\% into a reported 67\%. CI
now fails on any ICE in a mutant log, and on a zero viable count, \emph{before} the threshold is
read.

\textbf{Lies 3 and 4} — the configuration file was never read, and the gate could report 100\%
from a broken suite — are covered in \S\ref{sec:instruments}.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ Lie 5, measured 2026-08-03, and it is the sharpest of the five because the score was
\emph{exactly} 100\%.} A full-suite run on a rented GPU box printed:

\begin{center}\code{mutation score 100\% (killed 1936 / viable 1936), threshold 91\%\quad ok
(33374s)}\end{center}

\noindent
and, \emph{in the same phase}, fifteen \code{No space left on device} errors plus \code{Error:
write outcomes.json}. It had found \textbf{8\,738} mutants and scored \textbf{1\,936}.
\textbf{6\,802 mutants — 78\% — were unaccounted for, with zero \code{MISSED} and zero
\code{TIMEOUT} lines. They did not survive and get counted; they vanished.}

\smallskip
\textbf{The mechanism was already understood \emph{in that function}.} The ICE guard directly
above it says a dropped mutant \emph{"removes it from the denominator and INFLATES the score"} —
and guards only the ICE trigger. ENOSPC does the same and worse: it stops the result files being
written. \code{missed.txt} was never created, the counting helper read its absence as \textbf{0},
and \code{viable = killed + 0} forces the score to exactly 100\%.

\smallskip
★★★ \textbf{This is the project's own FIFTH ORACLE LIMIT reproduced inside its own harness}
(\S\ref{sec:oraclelimits}): \emph{an empty capture is evidence of nothing, not evidence of
emptiness.} Absence read as zero, in a different repository, by a different mechanism, four days
after the rule was written down. ⊘ \textbf{And a perfect score is the alarm, not the
achievement}: 1\,936/1\,936 with no misses, after a run throwing ENOSPC, is the signature of a
universe that collapsed under the campaign.

\smallskip
\textbf{Four new refusals landed, each proven to fire}, plus a control arm proving a genuinely
clean campaign (8\,738 found / 8\,600 scored) still passes. ⚠ \textbf{And the harness was not
wholly blind}: its summary already printed \emph{"★ …and the disk is below the comfort bar"}. But
the \emph{phase verdict} contradicted the harness's own warning — \textbf{and the phase verdict
is what a reader believes.}
\end{warnbox}

The historical numbers, each true of the scope it was measured on: \textbf{99.2\%} (245/247) on
the pure L0 core; \textbf{92.44\%} (159/172) on the L1 threaded surface. Both were measured over
a population that \emph{included the instruments}, because the exclusion file was inert.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{Do not quote a mutation score from this project. There is still not a defensible one.}
The threshold in force is \code{91} (\code{MUTATION\_THRESHOLD\_PCT}), so the previous revision's
"there is currently no mutation bar in force at all" is superseded — \textbf{but a bar in force
is not a measurement, and the only recent campaign to complete is Lie 5's, which is void.} The
project's own rule is that the bar is derived from a measured baseline under the configuration CI
will actually use, and \textbf{no such run has completed}. Its number is not available to this
document.

\smallskip
\textbf{The campaign that \emph{has} completed, labelled honestly.} 2026-07-28, 2\,898 mutants,
5\,h\,49\,m: 2\,305 caught, 112 missed, 3 timeout, 478 unviable. Product-only, with scaffolding
removed from the denominator, that computes to \textbf{95.52\%} (\textbf{95.38\%} if a
\code{TIMEOUT} is not counted as a kill, which is a real choice — cargo-mutants scores a timeout
as \emph{killed}). ⚠ \textbf{That run's tree was modified underneath it while it ran}, by a
concurrently dispatched task, and the number is therefore reported as an indication and not as a
measurement. It also does not describe the population CI will now test: with the configuration
finally being read, \code{--list} yields 2\,667 targets, not 2\,898.

\smallskip
\textbf{What that campaign found, which is worth more than its score.} \code{kayfabe-gsp} is the
floor at \textbf{92.71\%} and holds 36 of the survivors — exactly as expected for a crate no
campaign had ever covered, and the direct answer to the previous revision's "no mutation campaign
has ever run over them". \code{kayfabe-abi}'s encode side, previously a named gap, is
\textbf{closed}: 195 mutants, zero survivors. Of the 115 survivors, \textbf{8} are provably
equivalent (each proven by its sibling mutant being caught at the same site), \textbf{22} are dead
public API or \code{Debug} diagnostics with no callers anywhere, \textbf{17} are scaffolding, and
the remaining \textbf{68 are real untested logic, individually named}. A debt that is enumerated
to 68 named items is a different object from a percentage.
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{And one methodological result from closing some of them, which cuts against the usual
reading of a surviving mutant.} \code{b612a91} closed twelve \code{kayfabe-gsp} survivors and
found that one of them — a \code{+}\,$\rightarrow$\,\code{*} in \code{RegionMap::load} — was not
guarding correct code. It was \emph{masking wrong} code: the function reported \code{pages.len()
+ i} as the index of a misaligned page-table entry while \code{pages.push} ran in the same loop,
so the reported index was $N + 2i$ and correct only for the first entry of each table page. A
guest-facing diagnostic naming the wrong entry of a hostile table. \textbf{"This mutant survived"
is therefore not automatically "write a test"; it is sometimes "read the function".}

Three of the targeted survivors were \emph{deliberately not closed}: \code{RegionMap::is\_empty}
and \code{page\_size} have zero callers repo-wide, and writing tests for them would be exactly the
score-gaming the instrument exists to detect. \code{is\_empty} cannot simply be deleted
(\code{clippy::len\_without\_is\_empty} requires it once \code{len} exists) and deleting dead
public API is an owner call. That distinction — kill what a real readback naturally asserts,
refuse to invent a probe for what nothing calls — is the whole difference between a mutation score
and a coverage number.

\subsection{The OS-free configuration, and why it exists}

\code{KAYFABE\_NO\_KVM=1} forces the KVM probe to report the device absent, so the pure logic
core plus mocks — no OS layer at all — runs on purpose on any box. CI runs \emph{this} as its
default test configuration.

The reason it was made explicit is the doctrine applied to itself. GitHub runners have no
\file{/dev/kvm}, so CI was already running the OS-free configuration — \textbf{by luck of what
the runner image happens to lack}. It was unreproducible on any developer box, and it would have
lapsed silently the day a runner image started shipping the device. In the commit's own words:
\emph{"a guarantee that holds by luck is the same shape as a green instrument on an unexercised
path."}

The owner's question that prompted it is worth repeating because it is the right question:
\emph{"I hope you didn't delete the mocks so the core can still be tested without OS layer? and
that must pass as well so we don't lock in architectures/os semantics/qemu and later regret."}

\subsection{What the suite does not prove}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{It does not prove anything about NVIDIA.} Every test runs against
  \code{MockArch}, whose encodings are deliberately \emph{not} NVIDIA's. That is the right
  design for proving the seam, and it means the suite cannot catch a wrong belief about the real
  protocol. Only the C artifact and the bench can do that.
\item \textbf{The with-KVM configuration has no automated gate.} CI runs \emph{only} the
  KVM-absent configuration. The 36 tests that use a real \file{/dev/kvm} — including the entire
  real memory plane and the vCPU/MMIO dispatch — run on developer boxes and the bench, and
  nowhere else. This is the largest hole in the CI story, and the workflow says so in its own
  text rather than hiding it.
\item \textbf{The fuzzer is compiled, not run, by CI.} One target, over the one place raw guest
  bytes reach the core. Its two historical findings were minimised into deterministic in-tree
  regressions, which is the stronger form. The corpus is git-ignored, so a fresh clone starts
  from empty.
\item \textbf{Single-threaded logic testing of a concurrent system.} The core is tested
  deterministically because it is a pure state machine; the concurrency is tested separately by
  the threaded suites plus TSan. The argument is sound, but it depends on the core genuinely
  having no interior mutability — which is asserted at compile time, and is the sort of property
  worth re-checking if you are looking for holes.
\item \textbf{The page-size axis is designed and not built.} The testing doctrine cites
  \code{[4 KiB, 16 KiB, 64 KiB]} as an application of its "both modes ship from day one" rule.
  The environment variable that would select it does not exist in any \code{.rs} file — only in
  forward-looking comments. The \code{LockMode} half of that same rule \emph{is} built and
  exercised.
\end{itemize}

% ===========================================================================
\section{Discussion: the good, the bad, and the risky}
\label{sec:discussion}

\subsection{What is genuinely good}

\textbf{The purity constraint is real and it is mechanised.} A "pure core" claim is usually
aspirational. Here it is three CI greps, a manifest-level \code{forbid}, a naming rule that makes
\code{ls} the audit tool, and a ratchet on the exact number of \code{unsafe} relaxations. The
external dependency surface is three crates, two of them dev-only.

\textbf{The failure ledger is unusually honest and it is load-bearing.} Two design documents
carry "contact logs" — \file{l1\_concurrency.md} \S12 and \file{l1\_os\_shell.md} \S14 — recording
every place building the code proved the design wrong. They are long. They contain entries whose
titles are things like "the criterion was wrong", "boundary-1 broken", "two sections of \S3
contradict each other", "the design was wrong about its own severest claim". A project that
writes these down is a project whose other claims are worth more.

\textbf{The adversarial suites found real bugs before hardware existed.} 15 at L0, more since at
L1, including two control-plane \emph{wedge} bugs reachable by a hostile guest process — a parked
\code{SET\_PAGE\_DIRECTORY} and a parked \code{MAP\_MEMORY\_DMA}, each of which could be aimed
via a dup alias to make a benign third party's allocations fail forever. Both were found by a
property test, not by inspection. \emph{The same parked-fact machinery produced this revision's
worst core defect too} (\S\ref{sec:oracle}), which is a fair reading of where the difficulty in
this design actually lives.

\textbf{The bridge was designed on paper before it was built, and the design was then reported
against.} \file{gsp\_core\_\allowbreak bridge.md} was written first, in full, including the finding
that changed the roadmap; the four implementation commits then recorded roughly two dozen places where
that design met the compiler and lost, \emph{by section number}, including three of its own test
obligations turning out to be unbuildable and one turning out to be wrong. A design document that
is graded by the code that implements it is worth more than one that is merely followed.

\textbf{Both lock modes and both fallbacks ship as first-class tested modes.} The argument is
sharp: the slow path is the path the system takes \emph{when something has already gone wrong},
and a fallback exercised only by the fast path declining has test coverage proportional to how
often the fast path fails — which is the quantity the optimisation exists to drive to zero.

\textbf{The ABI oracle design.} Pinning generated layouts against three independent readings,
with a test that documents exactly how they disagree, is better than what most projects do, and
it caught the C artifact's own parity test being weaker than the code it guarded.

\textbf{★★ Compiling NVIDIA's own code as the oracle, five times.} The strongest testing idea in
the project is newer than any of the above and does not appear in its doctrine document. Where a
wire encoding had to be settled — the doorbell token, the GMMU page-table format, the pushbuffer
codec, the USERD channel-id decode, the VBIOS layout — the answer was not transcribed from the
headers and asserted. \textbf{NVIDIA's own generator, reader and recombination logic were
compiled and run as the oracle}, and our implementation differentially tested against them. A
transcription cannot detect a shared misreading; a compiled oracle can, and each of these caught
one. Five such families now have their own CI reached-count floor, so a box that cannot run them
says so instead of quietly counting them as passes.

\textbf{★ Corrections are struck, not deleted — and the practice survived contact with being
wrong in public.} \file{boot\_measured\_2026\_08\_01.md} carries sections marked \textbf{REFUTED}
at their heads, kept in full, with the observation preserved and only the inference withdrawn:
\emph{"it is kept, not deleted, because the observation below is accurate and only the inference
was wrong — and because this is the clearest example in the file of the failure mode the whole
document exists to resist: a chain of assertions read as a chain of causes."} A project that
writes that down about itself is a project whose other claims are worth more.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★ The execution plane was the wall --- and it was crossed}{The execution plane was the wall --- and it was crossed}}
\label{sec:execplane}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{This section replaced every previous revision's headline, and then was itself falsified.}
The previous revision's version read: \emph{"the critical path was always the plane where a real
engine has to move real bytes, and no amount of protocol work reaches it"}, with a specific,
well-cited prediction of where the boot would keep dying. \textbf{It was right about the kind of
problem and wrong about its finality.} The plane was crossed in eleven days, and this section is
now the account of \emph{how}, which is more useful than the wall was.
\end{claimbox}

\subsubsection*{What the wall was, and why it could not be answered with a protocol}

Partway through initialisation the guest driver stops asking questions and runs an experiment.
\code{memmgr\allowbreak InitCeUtils\_IMPL} calls \code{memmgr\allowbreak TestCeUtils}
unconditionally: it
writes \code{0xAABBCCDD} to framebuffer, issues \code{memmgr\allowbreak MemCopy} with
\code{TRANSFER\_\allowbreak FLAGS\_\allowbreak PREFER\_CE}, reads it back, and asserts
\code{sysmemData == vidmemData}.
\textbf{A functional end-to-end test of the copy engine with a read-back comparison. There is no
control-shaped answer to it.} Its status returns through \code{NV\_ASSERT\_OK\_OR\_RETURN} into a
\code{kernel\_fifo.c} arm that is \code{NV\_ASSERT(0)}, fatal for any non-\code{NV\_OK} in this
phase, so the pre-init sweep's \emph{"\code{NV\_ERR\_NOT\_SUPPORTED} = amputate and carry on"}
rule does not apply.

Every escape hatch was closed by citation, and one structurally: the Blackwell-only skip takes the
\code{else} arm on GA106; \code{bDisableGlobalCeUtils} requires modifying the guest, which is
outside "stock, unpatched"; the \code{IS\_VIRTUAL\_WITHOUT\_SRIOV} arm makes the interrupt
self-test permanently unpassable; and \code{!IS\_SILICON} \textbf{does not exist} — the three flags
behind it are declared in the driver's own headers and \emph{assigned nowhere in the tree}, so it
is constant true. \emph{This is not a lie we decline to tell; it is a lie we cannot tell.}

\textbf{★★★ That analysis was correct, and the conclusion drawn from it was wrong in an
instructive way.} The reading was \emph{"the driver demands a real copy engine, therefore the
remaining problem is the copy engine"}. The actual remaining problem was that the driver demands
\textbf{a real GPU behind every plane it touches} — and the way through was not to satisfy this
one test but to stop treating the planes as separable. The engine executes; we do not answer for
it. \S\ref{sec:ladder} is what that took.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★★★ The ladder to first compute --- \code{cup2} $\to$ \code{cup3} $\to$ \code{cup8}}{The ladder to first compute}}
\label{sec:ladder}

Every rung below is a boot on a real RTX 3060 (GA106) with host driver \textbf{580.159.04 open}, a
\textbf{stock, unpatched} guest NVIDIA driver, QEMU 10.2.4 with the QOM shim, and a named source
revision embedded in the binary and checked by a stamp gate before the run is graded.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.0cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Rung} & \textbf{Metric} & \textbf{What it does and does not establish} \\
\midrule
\code{cup2} & \code{CUP2\_RC=0} &
  A CUDA \emph{copy-engine} round trip: \code{cuCtxCreate}, \code{cuMemAlloc},
  \code{cuMemcpyHtoD}/\code{DtoH}. ⊘ \textbf{Not compute}, and the project renamed it to say so.
  Its 4-byte transfer does not even use a copy engine on real hardware — it is the compute class's
  I2M unit with the payload as a \emph{pushbuffer literal}, so a passthrough is correct by
  construction and proves nothing about an engine. \\
\addlinespace
\textbf{\code{cup3}} & \textbf{\code{CUP3\_VAL=43}} &
  \textbf{★★★★★ FIRST COMPUTE.} \measured{} 2026-08-14, rev \code{0655e6aa},
  \file{traces/w297\_cup3/}. A JITed PTX kernel computes \code{out = in*3 + 1} on \code{in = 14}.
  \textbf{43 is unforgeable in this stack}: a copy engine copies and memsets, our emulator has no
  arithmetic, and a forged completion carries a payload \emph{we} chose. ⇒ the host GA106
  \textbf{GR} engine executed the guest's shader. Reproduced \textbf{6/6}. \\
\addlinespace
\textbf{w299} & \textbf{\code{CUP3A\_VAL=43}} \newline \textbf{\code{CUP3B\_VAL=43}} &
  \textbf{★★★★★ TWO CONCURRENT GUEST PROCESSES BOTH COMPUTED.} \measured{} 2026-08-14, rev
  \code{f459cffa}. The device saw three isolates materialized, three live, zero refusing, with
  distinct \code{proc=} ids. \textbf{The C artifact's \#14 shape did not reproduce} — including on
  a staggered arm where the second process started while the first was \emph{demonstrably inside}
  \code{cuCtxCreate}, gated on the first's own print rather than a sleep. \\
\addlinespace
\textbf{w367} & \textbf{\code{TORCH\_OK} $\times 3$} \newline \code{8/8, 8/8, 8/8} &
  \textbf{★★★★★ THREE SEQUENTIAL GUEST PROCESSES PASSED IN ONE BOOT.} \measured{} 2026-08-20,
  rev \code{cca2eb4b}, 600\,s step bounds. A first torch process, a second with no
  \code{nvidia-smi} between it and the first, and a third after \code{nvidia-smi} --- all
  \code{avail=True sum=4.0}, all \textbf{8/8} completions observed, all ringing
  \emph{identically} 104 \code{Ce} + 403 \code{GrCompute} doorbells; host \code{dmesg} 0
  \code{NVRM} 0 \code{Xid}. Two boots earlier every process after the first rang exactly eight
  doorbells and stalled forever. ⊘ \textbf{A \emph{fourth} process still fails}, above the
  \code{ioctl} boundary, cause unknown; $n = 1$ boot. \S\ref{sec:seqfix}. \\
\addlinespace
\code{cup8} & \code{bad=0 maxerr=0} &
  \textbf{Byte-exact matmul at scale.} \code{N=2048}, 16\,MiB per matrix, 48\,MiB device,
  \code{grid=(128,128) block=(16,16)}, \file{traces/w308\_cup8/}; and again at \textbf{N=3072}
  (36\,MiB operands, 108\,MiB device) over 12 verified iterations, \file{traces/w327\_cliff/}.
  ⊘ \code{cup8} allocates its buffers once and keeps them, so it never performs the
  allocate\,$\to$\,free\,$\to$\,allocate-elsewhere sequence that is the real reclamation defect:
  \emph{evidence the size is fine, not evidence the defect is gone.} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{⊘ Four things the ladder does not establish, each recorded by the run that produced it
rather than found by a critic.}

\smallskip
\textbf{(1) Every green is a RELAXED green, the relaxations are named, and the shipped default is
not among them.} Eleven arms are forced on by the bench runner. \textbf{Nine were single-ablated}
and \textbf{five are measured load-bearing}: \code{VAS\_PUBLISH=drain} (its alternative produces
an \code{Xid 31 FAULT\_PDE}), \code{GR\_ROUTE=passthrough}, \code{GUEST\_RING=ring},
\code{CE\_EXECUTOR=host} and \code{PT\_WITNESS\_EXEC=on}. ★★★ \textbf{\code{KAYFABE\_GR\_ROUTE}
still defaults to \code{refuse} in the shipped code}, so the configuration that computes is
\emph{not} the configuration that ships. ★ The arming was verified from the device's own emissions
rather than from the environment, \emph{because a boot happening is not an arm running}.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{And the ablation census was itself wrong and corrected within the day.} Two arms
recorded INERT for \code{cup3} were later measured to \emph{each alone kill} a second workload (a
raw copy-engine client with no \code{libcuda}), and were restored. \emph{"Inert for \code{cup3}"
had been read as "inert".} The project's response was a mechanism rather than a note: an
inertness claim must now clear \textbf{both} workloads before it counts.

\smallskip
\textbf{(2) \code{n} is small and this project has measured its own false-negative rate.} A
single-boot \code{43} is \textbf{wrong 1 time in 5} on these boxes — 4/5 on the branch \emph{and}
4/5 on same-hour clean master, both reds identical field-for-field. \emph{So a single green boot
is not a grade}, and the cause of the intermittent is known: a \textbf{drain-budget truncation}
whose walk ascends VA order, so it always drops the top of the address space — exactly where the
completion semaphore lives.

\smallskip
\textbf{(3) It is one shape of work on one part.} \code{cup3} is a \textbf{1$\times$1$\times$1
launch of a six-instruction shader}: it says nothing about occupancy, multi-block dispatch, shared
memory, barriers or throughput. Everything is GA106 and \textbf{no host driver other than
580.159.04 has ever been run against this code.}

\smallskip
\textbf{(4) The bench is nested and that now matters.} The rented box is itself a VM, so the
Mode-2 guest is L2. The nested-virt tax is bounded at $\leq 2.2$\,ms/launch — which was 2.6\% of
an 85\,ms doorbell trap and is \textbf{up to 55\% of a 4\,ms one}. ⇒ \emph{Bare metal matters more
now precisely because the doorbell path got fast.}
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{★★ How the boot got through, in the terms a reader can check}

The mechanism is not one fix; it is one invariant, and the C artifact stated it in its own source
before the Rust port re-derived it the hard way: \emph{"a mapping is always backed before the
engine that uses it runs."} The port's version has \textbf{three co-equal population sources},
which is one more than either project's design documents claimed for most of the campaign:

\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.3ex]
\item \textbf{An RPC capture.} \code{NV2080\_CTRL\_CMD\_GPU\_PROMOTE\_CTX} (\code{0x2080012b}) is
  snooped in flight and its \code{\{gpuPhysAddr, gpuVirtAddr, size, physAttr\}} entries folded into
  the address table. This is the source both trees' documents kept omitting.
\item \textbf{A doorbell-time sweep of the guest's GR page tables}, re-swept when a tracked
  page-table page is written.
\item \textbf{The observed CE page-table write}, decoded and latched at the completion-semaphore
  release — the commit point that replaces the TLB invalidate, \emph{which on this path does not
  occur} (\S\ref{sec:trust}).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{★ And one difference between the two implementations turned out to be load-bearing, at the
granularity of a single line.} The C rounds every promote-derived mapping \emph{up to 64\,KiB}
before mapping it (\code{asize = (size + 0xffff) \& ~0xffffull}). The Rust port originally bound at
the \emph{declared} length, which produced non-page-aligned rows and a \textbf{sub-page hole} —
2\,560 bytes the port's own \code{resolve} answered \code{Miss} for, inside a page the guest had
mapped. \textbf{The C could not have that hole; the port had it by construction.} The C also
treats \code{NV\_ERR\_NO\_MEMORY} on a \code{FIXED} map as \emph{success} — it means the VA is
already mapped in the host VAS — a semantic the port had to match or it would refuse exactly the
context buffers the host RM had already placed.

\subsubsection*{★ And a whole class of walls turned out to be one wall}

Six months of this campaign chased faults one at a time: pushbuffer VAs, the semaphore page, the
CE operand, its extent, the GR write. \textbf{Each is one instance of the same missing invariant},
and the C artifact went green \emph{without servicing or forwarding a single GPU fault} — there is
no fault-buffer emulation anywhere in it — because publishing the whole working set before the
engine runs makes that class of fault \textbf{impossible} rather than handled.

⊘ \textbf{The honest cost of that design is stated in \S\ref{sec:perf}}: publishing the working set
before every submission is what makes our doorbell handler the dominant term in a kernel launch.
\emph{The correctness argument and the performance problem are the same mechanism.}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★ Performance: the binding constraint moved twice, and every number here needs a scope}{Performance: the binding constraint moved twice}}
\label{sec:perf}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★★★ READ THIS BEFORE QUOTING ANY NUMBER IN THIS SECTION.} \measured{} 2026-08-19: the
guest's \code{submit\_ms} for the \emph{same workload, same binary, same box} was \textbf{85.32,
9.41 and 64.39\,ms} on three consecutive boots — \textbf{a 9.1$\times$ spread with the middle boot
fastest}. Within any one boot the seven iterations agree to $\pm 2\%$, so \textbf{the value is
decided at boot and then holds}. Across twelve boots that day it spanned \textbf{6.3 to 216\,ms}.
The same twelve boots held \code{sync\_ms} at \textbf{10.23--10.40\,ms, $\pm 0.8\%$}.

\smallskip
⇒ \textbf{Any submit-side grade at $n=1$ or $n=2$ per arm is uninterpretable}, and the project
says so about its own published tables. ⊘ The obvious explanation — a session ramp as host state
accumulates — was \textbf{refuted in ten minutes}: the sequence is non-monotone, \emph{and} free
memory improved after the slow boot while submit stayed slow. \textbf{What varies per boot is
still unknown.}

\smallskip
★ \textbf{The surviving half is the useful one}: a \emph{null} on \code{sync\_ms} is strong
evidence precisely because \code{sync\_ms} is the $\pm 0.8\%$ statistic, where the identical null
on \code{submit\_ms} would be worth nothing. \emph{Two numbers printed side by side in the same
table can have completely different evidential weight.}
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{Where the time goes, and the two answers that were both right}

\textbf{★★ The launch floor was our own doorbell handler}, and the measurement closes.
\measured{} 2026-08-14 (w315, \file{traces/w315\_floor/}, $n=2$ boots, $N=512$): a guest
\code{cuLaunchKernel} takes 90.9\,ms, of which \textbf{86.7\,ms is spent inside a single MMIO
doorbell trap} with the vCPU halted. Inside that trap:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{segment} & \textbf{ms} & \textbf{\% of the 86.7\,ms trap} \\
\midrule
\code{vas\_publish} & 48.3 & 55.7 \\
\code{pt\_decode} & 22.3 & 25.7 \\
\code{pt\_sweep} & 6.6 & 7.6 \\
\code{pt\_vascensus} & 2.1 & 2.4 \\
\midrule
\textbf{page-table + publication} & \textbf{79.4} & \textbf{91.5} \\
\textbf{the real host RM forward} & \textbf{3.6} & \textbf{4.1} \\
our own logging & 0.2 & 0.2 \\
\midrule
\textsc{unmarked} & 0.009 & \textbf{0.01} \quad ⇒ \emph{the breakdown closes} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

⚠ \textbf{Two denominators, both correct, and mixing them is a documented error in this project.}
91.5\% is \emph{of the trap}; the same family is \textbf{69.9\% of the 113.6\,ms end-to-end
launch}, and the host forward is 4.1\% of the trap but 3.2\% of the launch.

\textbf{★ And 95.6\% of the trap is classified \code{shape=work}} — it costs the same on bare
metal. ⊘ \textbf{That classification is \measured{}; the inference from it is not}: the rung's own
"what is not here" section says \emph{no bare-metal control was run}, and the bench is nested, so
the guest is at L2.

\subsubsection*{★★ The fix, and why one statistic could not grade it}

Two changes were built, both default-off for weeks, and \textbf{they act on different
statistics}:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{arm} (\code{mmio\_doorbell total\_us}) & \textbf{median} & \textbf{p90} & \textbf{max} \\
\midrule
control ($\times 2$) & 18\,741 / 19\,286 & 86\,104 / 82\,488 & 2\,753\,760 / 2\,726\,516 \\
\textbf{dirty gate} & \textbf{2\,197} \;(8.5$\times$) & \textbf{4\,431} \;(19.4$\times$) &
  2\,815\,676 \;⊘ \emph{unchanged} \\
\textbf{coalescer} & 30\,311 \;⊘ \emph{1.6$\times$ WORSE} & 76\,670 & \textbf{217\,190}
  \;(12.7$\times$) \\
both ($\times 2$) & 9\,016 / 11\,759 & 10\,712 / 14\,807 & 249\,118 / 394\,035 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

⇒ \textbf{Graded on the median alone the coalescer reads as a regression; graded on the max alone
the gate does nothing.} Only both statistics separate them, because they remove different work:
the gate removes the per-doorbell page-table pass (the \emph{typical} trap), the coalescer removes
the guest-RAM pin drain (the \emph{worst} trap). ⚠ $n=1$ per arm for the two single-flag rows, so
by the lottery above \textbf{those two ratios are owed a re-grade} and the project's own scoping
note says so.

★ \textbf{The strongest row in that table is a cross-machine reproduction}: the gate's p90 going
$86.1 \to 4.43$\,ms independently reproduces an earlier campaign's $85.248 \to 4.078$\,ms on
different hardware, a different driver build and a different guest kernel. \emph{A reproduced
ratio is worth more than a repeated arm.}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ And the reason the handler was expensive was not the reason everyone assumed.} The
hypothesis was that the per-doorbell scan is $O(\text{VAS size})$. \textbf{From the control arm's
own log: walking 16\,425 rows costs 0\,ms.} The 40\,ms appears only when the scan finds
\textbf{eight} candidates, and it is \textbf{eight host round trips at ${\sim}5$\,ms each, every
one of them refused \emph{"already joined"} and released} — repeated 87 times per boot for the
same eight leaves. ⇒ \emph{Making the check incremental would have saved 0\,ms.} The fix was to
arm a dirty flag at the \textbf{sink} — inside the two write sites of the table itself — rather
than at an enumeration of boundary events, which makes a missed invalidation
\emph{unrepresentable} rather than merely tested for.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{And the arithmetic that pointed the wrong way looked like corroboration.}
$48.3\,\text{ms} \div 16\,425\ \text{rows} \approx 2.9\,\mu\text{s/row}$ — a completely plausible
per-row cost, arriving before anything was measured, for a scan that actually costs zero.
\textbf{This project has now recorded four such \emph{pre-corroborated} answers}, each a magnitude
that agreed with a hypothesis before the hypothesis was tested, and each wrong. \emph{A quantity
that agrees in advance is the weakest evidence that feels like the strongest.}
\end{claimbox}

\subsubsection*{★★★ Then the binding constraint moved, and the campaign's headline number expired}

An earlier rung fitted $\text{guest} = C + k \cdot \text{native}$ and got \textbf{$C \approx
115$--$132$\,ms fixed and $k \approx 22$--$27\times$}, concluding that batching cannot help
because the floor is per-\emph{submit}, not per-\emph{sync}. \textbf{That fit is now stale by its
own tree's instruction}: the successor rung records, in writing, that \emph{"the arithmetic must
be re-derived against 4\,ms, not against 115--132\,ms"} — and \textbf{the re-derived multiplier is
printed nowhere.} ⊘ This paper therefore does not quote one.

\textbf{What replaced it is a cleaner and more uncomfortable result.}

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule
$N$ & \textbf{submit (ms)} & \textbf{sync (ms)} & \textbf{sync off-CPU} & \textbf{nvcsw} \\
\midrule
128 & 3.483 & 0.971 & $-0.003$ & 0 \\
512 & 3.699 & 22.734 & $-0.010$ & 0 \\
1024 & 4.103 & 59.786 & $-0.002$ & 0 \\
2048 & \textbf{4.373} & \textbf{908.966} & $+0.189$ & 0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Submit is flat across a 4096$\times$ range of arithmetic while sync moves 936$\times$, and
the guest spins on-CPU for 100\% of the wait, blocking zero times, at every size.}
⇒ \code{cuCtxSynchronize} is not a floor to be optimised; \textbf{it is the kernel running}.
\textbf{The submit path this whole campaign drove down is 0.5\% of an $N=2048$ launch}, so driving
it to zero would improve that launch by half a percent.

\smallskip
\textbf{★★ Two separable problems that bind at different kernel sizes, and conflating them is the
error to avoid.} At $N=128$ submit is \textbf{78\%} of the launch — the LLM shape, many small
kernels. At $N=2048$ it is \textbf{0.5\%} and the compute itself is \textbf{22--81$\times$ off
native}.

\subsubsection*{★★ Where the 22--81$\times$ lives: the operands are in the wrong memory}

\measured{} 2026-08-14, PCIe gen3 $\times 16$ confirmed from the link itself, all three numbers
taken with the same kernel on the same GPU:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrl@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{placement} & \textbf{GB/s} & \\
\midrule
host VRAM & \textbf{313.5} & $n=3$, $\pm 0.04\%$ \\
host pinned sysmem, 2\,MiB pages & \textbf{12.33} & $n=3$, $\pm 0.2\%$ — \emph{the link,
  saturated} \\
\textbf{our guest's operands, read by the kernel} & \textbf{2.51} & $n=3$ boots, $\pm 1.5\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

⇒ \textbf{125$\times$ below VRAM and 4.9$\times$ below the link.} The overall factor decomposes as
\textbf{placement $14.80\times$ $\times$ aperture quality $2.76\times = 40.85\times$}, which is the
same-hour measured ratio at $N=2048$ — \textbf{and neither factor is the submit path.} ⚠ $n=1$ per
placement for the decomposition.

⚠⚠ \textbf{Do not conflate 2.51\,GB/s with the copy path.} 2.51\,GB/s is the GPU kernel reading
operands during the matmul. \textbf{\code{cuMemcpyHtoD} runs at ${\sim}9$--$11$\,MiB/s} — two
independent rungs agree ($\approx 9.25$ and $\approx 10.8$\,MiB/s). \emph{These differ by
${\sim}250\times$; they are different planes.}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{⊘⊘⊘ And the most instructive result in the whole performance campaign is a correction to
a favourable one.} A rung had reported that our guest was \emph{faster} than natively pinned host
memory at $N \geq 1024$. \textbf{The control was the anomaly.} Its one non-VRAM baseline measured
\textbf{3.697 / 9.456 / 11.886 / 10.887 / 9.613\,GB/s over five identical runs} — a $3.2\times$
spread — while the three other placements reproduced to \textbf{0.2\%}. Re-run against an honest
baseline, "2.2$\times$ faster" became \textbf{1.37$\times$ slower} and "1.6$\times$ faster" became
\textbf{2.76$\times$ slower}.

\smallskip
⇒ \textbf{A control is a measurement and needs its own $n$ and its own scatter.} A baseline is
usually reported as a single number precisely because nobody expects it to be the interesting
one — which is exactly why an unstable one is invisible.
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{⊘ Tokens per second: there is no number, and the ones in circulation are not measurements}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ The Rust port has never measured tokens/second. $n = 0$. No LLM has ever run in this
guest} — the CUDA runtime now initialises, and the model still raises \emph{"CUDA unknown
error"} before any token (\S\ref{sec:llmwall}). The one figure that circulates, \textbf{0.04\,tok/s}, is \emph{arithmetic}: an \assumed{} 250
launches/token multiplied by a per-launch floor that has since fallen by ${\sim}20\times$. It is
re-quoted in two design documents as though it were a fact. \textbf{Treat it as neither a
measurement nor current.}

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{And the C artifact's headline figure is not what it appears either.} \emph{"20 $\to$ 60
tok/s"} is \textbf{the title of a plan}, not a result, and its 60 was borrowed from \emph{Mode 1},
a different architecture with no doorbell trap. What the C actually measured with logs is
\textbf{19.5--26.6\,tok/s at $\pm 40\%$ run-to-run}. A separate bare-metal figure of \textbf{49.9
vs 47.5\,tok/s host-native} exists and is credible, \emph{but its commit is documentation-only —
no log and no trace was committed}, unlike the matmul result beside it. \textbf{Treat 49.9 as
plausible and uncorroborated; treat 20--38 as measured.}

\smallskip
★ \textbf{One external comparison was also refuted, and the refutation is the transferable part.}
A sibling Mode-1 project's $0.82\times$ eager-mode result was cited here as \emph{"the external,
measured bound on what a doorbell trap can ever cost"}. \textbf{That project has no doorbell
interception anywhere in its codebase} and still loses 18\%. ⇒ \textbf{exit count and per-exit
work are independent axes.} The C's own slow case was root-caused to a \emph{busy-polled register
read} — one register accounting for ${\sim}99\%$ of all BAR0 reads, 1--3\,k vmexits per token —
which is a read, not a doorbell. \emph{Our shape is the opposite}: few exits, expensive ones, and
the expense is our own work.
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★★★ The LLM wall: a ladder of controls, a fatal PAIR, and a milestone graded on a proxy}{The LLM wall: a ladder of controls, a fatal PAIR, and a milestone graded on a proxy}}
\label{sec:llmwall}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{This section was rewritten three times while this paper was being written}, as five
commits landed in one afternoon. That is not an apology — it is the measurement
(\S\ref{sec:drift}), and each version was wrong in an instructive way. The sequence was:
\emph{"the cause is unknown and five hypotheses are refuted"} $\to$ \emph{"the cause is one
control we refuse"} $\to$ \emph{"the wall is broken"} $\to$ \textbf{the version below, which is
that the initialisation wall is genuinely broken and \emph{the LLM still does not run}.}

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{AND A FOURTH TIME, 2026-08-20 15:39 --- three minutes after this document was pinned,
and it is the one that changes what a reader should do with this section.} The version below ends
by naming \emph{the object graph} as the next wall. \textbf{That wall does not exist}
(\code{129dd316}, \code{dbc6c953}), and the fold-in sits above it rather than beside it. ⚠ Read
the whole of this section as \measured{} at \code{3887be37}: \textbf{the LLM workload has not
been re-run since}, and in particular not across the multi-process fixes of \S\ref{sec:seqfix}.
Its current status is \unver{}, not known-failing.
\end{claimbox}

\subsubsection*{The symptom as it stood, and why every conventional instrument said "healthy"}

In one guest process: \code{libcuda} — the CUDA \textbf{driver} API — answered every call
correctly (\code{cuInit} = 0, device count 1, name correct, 12\,540\,182\,528 bytes,
compute capability 8.6, a primary context retained, managed allocation and
\code{cuCtxSynchronize} = 0). \code{libcudart} — the \textbf{runtime} API — returned \textbf{3},
\code{cudaErrorInitializationError}, from its first call. Host \code{Xid} \textbf{0}; guest
\code{NVRM} line count identical to a fully green boot; \textbf{every \code{ioctl(2)} returned 0
on both sides.}

\begin{warnbox}\small
⊘ \textbf{\code{strace} is structurally blind to this entire class.} \textbf{RM returns its
verdict \emph{inside} the parameter struct}, not in the syscall's return value: a control that
fails with \code{NV\_ERR\_NOT\_SUPPORTED} is an \code{ioctl} that returned \textbf{0}. The
standard reflex — \emph{"strace it and find the failing syscall"} — yields a clean trace over a
broken system. The project's name for this is \emph{"\code{failed=0} is not \emph{nothing
refused}"}, and it cost several rungs.
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{★★ The instrument, and the four ways it was wrong before it worked}

The oracle is a \textbf{host$\leftrightarrow$guest differential}: the same one-call program,
recorded through the same \code{LD\_PRELOAD} shim, on the bare-metal GA106 and inside our guest
on the same box in the same hour, capturing the parameter buffer on \textbf{both sides} of every
\code{ioctl}. Traces at \file{traces/cudart\_ga106/}. Host \textbf{105} records, guest
\textbf{104}; 98 identical; \textbf{2} status divergences; \textbf{27} body divergences at equal
status.

\textbf{Four instrument defects, each of which produced a confident wrong answer first}, listed
because they are the transferable part:

\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.35ex]
\item \textbf{The first differ compared \code{(dev, nr, cmd, status)} and threw away the
  bodies} — \emph{the only place the defect could have been}. The recorder had captured them all
  along.
\item \textbf{A per-id refusal bisection reported all six candidate controls innocent, and was
  right about every one of them}, because it can only ever find \emph{individually} sufficient
  causes. \textbf{It is structurally incapable of finding a fatal \emph{set}.}
\item \textbf{A word count taken from the hex string length inspected exactly half of every
  body}, silently under-reporting non-zero words. Take it from \code{paramsSize}.
\item \textbf{Two silent truncations} — a rule array capped at 64 entries and, separately, a parse
  buffer capped at 1024 bytes against a 3158-byte rule set — made the substitution experiment
  report a confident answer derived from an arbitrary nine-rule subset. The shim now prints rules
  \emph{parsed} and rules \emph{applied} as two numbers. \emph{One count cannot detect its own
  truncation; two can.}
\end{enumerate}

★ \textbf{And the substitution injector was proven before use}, which is what made its negatives
mean anything: corrupting one reply word of \code{0x20809009} from \code{0xd} to \code{0} turns
the working host from \textbf{0} into \textbf{3}. So a wrong value at \code{status = 0} \emph{can}
kill \code{cudart}, and the shim \emph{can} produce one.

\subsubsection*{★★★★★ The answer: it is a ladder of controls, and the minimal fatal set is a PAIR}

Two findings, and each one invalidated the experiment that had preceded it.

\textbf{(1) The wall is a ladder, not a control.} Serve one, the guest advances one step, and the
\emph{next} refusal becomes the wall: \code{0x2080a084} $\to$ \code{0x2080a026} $\to$
\code{0x2080a001} $\to$ \code{0x2080200b}. ⊘ \textbf{Fixing these one per boot is the wrong
shape}, and four boots were spent discovering that.

\textbf{(2) The minimal fatal set is a \emph{pair}, and no one-at-a-time experiment can find it.}
The full subset lattice over three of them, measured on the bare-metal host by refusing each
subset in band:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{\hskip 2.5em}lc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{refused subset} & \textbf{host verdict} & \textbf{refused subset} & \textbf{host verdict} \\
\midrule
$\varnothing$ & 0 & \code{a084}, \code{a026} & 0 \\
\code{a084} & 0 & \code{a084}, \code{200b} & 0 \\
\code{a026} & 0 & \textbf{\code{a026}, \code{200b}} & \textbf{3} \quad ← \textbf{minimal} \\
\code{200b} & 0 & \code{a084}, \code{a026}, \code{200b} & 3 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

⇒ \textbf{Three individually-innocent controls, one fatal pair.} That is why the per-id bisection
was right six times and useless, and it is why the lattice — not the bisection — is the
instrument. \textbf{★★ It also produced the real deliverable: a zero-boot reproduction.} Refusing
\code{\{a026, 200b\}} on a bare-metal host reproduces the guest's exact failure \emph{in seconds,
with no VM}.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★★★ And one member of the ladder was invisible to every experiment driven by the
reference capture.} \code{0x2080a001} appears \textbf{zero times} in an unmodified host trace —
\textbf{a healthy host never needs it.} It is the \emph{fallback} the guest takes only after the
two above it are refused. So neither the refusal bisection nor the 136-word substitution sweep
could ever have covered it; both were sound and both were \emph{structurally incapable}.

\smallskip
\textbf{⇒ A control that exists only inside the failure mode is invisible to a reference capture
taken outside it. To see it you must drive the reference INTO the failure and re-capture.}

\smallskip
★ That also dissolved a residue that had been sitting unexplained in the differential:
\textbf{105 records versus 104 was never a missing call. It is a different branch.}
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{★★★ The initialisation wall broke --- and the milestone did not}

\measured{} 2026-08-20, boot \code{w354cud1}, real GA106. The pre-registered falsifier was
\textbf{met}:

\begin{center}\small
\code{RT\_cudaGetDeviceCount = 0} \quad \code{RT\_cudaSetDevice = 0} \quad
\code{RT\_cudaMalloc = 0} \quad \code{RT\_cudaDeviceSync = 0} \\[2pt]
\code{TORCH\_INIT\_OK} \quad \textbf{\code{TORCH\_AVAIL = True}} — \emph{False for the whole
campaign until then}
\end{center}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ And then the milestone test said no, and the correction is the most useful thing in
this section.} \measured{} the same afternoon, boot \code{w355llm1}, running the \emph{actual
model} rather than the probe:

\begin{center}\small
\code{TORCH\_CUDA\_AVAILABLE = True} \quad \code{TORCH\_DEV\_COUNT = 1} \quad
\code{nvidia-smi} prints a real header \\[2pt]
\code{torch.\_C.\_cuda\_init()} $\rightarrow$ \textbf{\code{RuntimeError: CUDA unknown error}}
\\[2pt]
\textbf{\code{LLM\_OK=0} \quad \code{LLM\_TOKENS=0} \quad \code{LLM\_RC=1} \quad
\code{LLM\_OUTCOME=FAILED\_rc1}}
\end{center}

\smallskip
\textbf{⊘ The milestone had been graded on a PROXY.} \code{is\_available()} calls
\code{cudaGetDeviceCount} — the exact thing the probe measured — while the milestone is
\emph{tokens}. \textbf{They disagreed, and the proxy was the optimistic one.} The project has paid
for this lesson before, under a different name: an earlier rung declared "first compute" on a
copy-engine round trip that was not compute.

\smallskip
\textbf{What is true, stated at the width the evidence supports}: the \code{cudart}
\emph{initialisation} wall is fixed, now confirmed \textbf{$n = 2$ across two different
workloads}; \textbf{the failure moved one rung}, from
\code{cudaErrorInitializationError} to \emph{"CUDA unknown error"} inside torch's full CUDA
init, which walks the primary-context path; and the control-serving plane is genuinely clear —
the refusal counter on that path fired \textbf{zero} times.
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{⊘⊘⊘ REFUTED: "the next wall is the object graph" --- there is no object-graph wall}{REFUTED: there is no object-graph wall}}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{⊘⊘⊘ EVERYTHING BELOW THIS BOX IS REFUTED, AND IT WAS REFUTED THREE MINUTES AFTER THIS
DOCUMENT WAS PINNED.} It is kept, not deleted, because the \emph{way} it was wrong is the
section's most useful content. \measured{} \code{129dd316}, 2026-08-20: the same refusal census
was run against the boot that \textbf{passes} --- \code{w354cud1} (\code{cudart} green) versus
\code{w355llm1} (LLM failing), \textbf{same revision \code{3887be37}, same binary}:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{refusal} & \textbf{passes} & \textbf{fails} \\
\midrule
\code{PromoteFault::UnknownContextObject} & 4 & 3 \\
\code{RmGraphError::FreeUnknown} & 14 & 10 \\
\code{BridgeRefusal::UnmappedAllocClass} & 6 & 4 \\
\code{wrong-kind(Device)} census lines & 2 & 2 \\

\textbf{★★★★★ Every element of the "wall" is MORE NUMEROUS in the boot that works.} It is

the \emph{difference} is.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{And the sharpest line was the most wrong.} \code{tsg = mid-miss(h0xa,
wrong-kind(Device))} is \textbf{not a defect} (\code{dbc6c953}): a channel's parent is
legitimately either a TSG or a Device --- grouped channels have the first, bare channels the
second --- and the route that does not apply says so by name. The same line appears
\emph{byte-identical} in a boot from 2026-08-09 with the same count. \textbf{"The sharpest line
on the board" was an eleven-day-old baseline line.} Two of the three refusals were separately
already ruled: \code{PromoteFault} is the guest kernel RM's \emph{RC watchdog} channel, refused
deliberately since 2026-08-09; \code{FreeUnknown} is faithful RM behaviour, decided 2026-07-28.

\smallskip
★ \textbf{The process failure is the transferable part, and it is this project's own written
rule.} \file{memory/a\_refusal\_needs\_a\_negative\_control.md} says \emph{"the failing run is
not the experiment; the DIFFERENCE is"} --- and the census below was read as a wall
\textbf{without running the control, which was two log files already on the bench and one
\code{grep} away}. ⇒ \textbf{Before naming any refusal a wall, run the control. Mechanically,
first, always.} ⚠ A second-order cost: the refuted claim lived in a \emph{commit message}, and a
research agent later returned it as its top-ranked candidate \emph{with a hash attached to lend it
weight}. Speculation in a commit message becomes somebody's premise.

\smallskip
\textbf{⇒ What replaces it: nothing yet.} What fails inside \code{\_cuda\_init()}'s ${\sim}479$-ioctl
primary-context graph is \textbf{unidentified}. Eliminated by measurement so far: the reply-body
plane, the C artifact's CE-caps precedent (both of its controls are never issued here),
\code{pdb=N} (by design since 2026-08-09), \code{wrong-kind} (a route that legitimately does not
apply), and now the entire refusal census. The recorded method that has worked twice is to trace
host versus guest at the failing layer and \textbf{diff the bodies} --- not to read a census.
\end{warnbox}

\smallskip
\emph{The refuted text follows, verbatim.}

\code{Xid} is \textbf{0} and guest \code{NVRM} sits at the green baseline, so this is a
\emph{refusal}, not a fault. The boot names it:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{refusal} & \textbf{count} \\
\midrule
\code{PromoteFault::UnknownContextObject} & 3 \\
\code{RmGraphError::FreeUnknown} & 10 \\
\code{BridgeRefusal::UnmappedAllocClass} (\code{0x70}, \code{0x208f}) & 4 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{★ And one line is sharper than the counts.} The census for the first refusal reads
\code{tsg = mid-miss(h0xa, wrong-kind(Device))} — \textbf{a handle that resolves, but to the wrong
kind of object.} That is a named, specific defect in the context-promotion path, and it is
\emph{not} the class of thing the previous six rungs were about. \emph{The campaign has moved from
"which control does the driver want" to "is our object model right", which is a different kind of
problem and squarely inside our own code.}

\smallskip
\emph{End of the refuted text.} ⊘ \textbf{The caveats below are about the \code{cudart} control
ladder --- the fix that \emph{did} land --- and they stand.}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{⊘ Four caveats on the fix, none of them small, all from the rung that landed it.}
\textbf{(1)} \textbf{$n = 1$} on the \code{cudart} green, against this project's own measured
rule that a single-boot green is wrong \textbf{1 time in 5}. \textbf{(2)} The fix is
\textbf{capture-derived and therefore expiring}: it is pinned to GA106 and 580.159.04, and two of
its rows were captured from \textbf{a host driven into the failure state}, because a healthy host
never asks those controls. \textbf{(3)} Two members of the ladder are \emph{still refused} —
correct per the lattice, since breaking either member of the fatal pair suffices, but narrower
than \emph{"serve what \code{cudart} asks"}. \textbf{(4)} One row had to be \textbf{spliced rather
than substituted}: the guest's request carries real content, so returning a constant body would
have clobbered the guest's own words — the under-filled-body defect pointing the other way. The
implementation keeps the guest's buffer and overwrites exactly the nine words a real GA106
overwrites.
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★ A trace of a boot that SUCCEEDS --- and the ladder, enumerated}{A trace of a boot that SUCCEEDS --- and the ladder, enumerated}}
\label{sec:recorder}

\begin{claimbox}\small
⚠ \textbf{Scope note, first, because it is a claim about where code lives.} The recorder and the
