demand lists described here are on branch \code{replay-conformance}; they are \textbf{not on
\code{master}}, which is where this document lives. Everything below is real, committed and
dated, and a reader checking out \code{master} will not find the paths. This paper would rather
say that than either omit the work or cite a path that resolves to nothing.
\end{claimbox}

Until now every trace this project owned was of a boot that \textbf{fails} — the C's
\code{cap1} ends where the C's emulator stopped, and our own boots end where ours does. A trace
of a failing boot has an ambiguity that cannot be argued away: \textbf{every absent control is
indistinguishable between "the driver never wants it" and "the driver died before asking."}

A recorder now exists inside CPU-RM itself. Two capture points in NVIDIA's own
\code{message\_queue\_cpu.c} — one per direction — recording the \emph{complete} message-queue
element, header and body, into a \code{vmalloc} ring drained through \code{/proc}. \textbf{Two
call sites. That is the whole instrumentation surface.} Its one invariant is stated as such:
\emph{a record has its bytes, or it does not exist} — fill-and-stop, never overwrite, and a
non-zero drop count makes the trace unreplayable and the decoder rejects it.

\textbf{What it captured, 2026-08-03, on a real GA106:} 1\,076 records (535 out, 541 in), 1.17\,MiB
of a 64\,MiB ring, \textbf{zero dropped}, 14 distinct RPC functions, 620 control elements
resolving to 310 request/reply pairs and \textbf{104 distinct controls} — and, the line that
matters most, \textbf{replies declaring params with no bytes present: 0}. Two independent GSP
bring-ups in the trace (sequence numbers restart), with \emph{zero} retransmits inside either,
which makes it a repeatability check as well as a capture. \textbf{The boot succeeds}:
\code{nvidia-smi} reports the card, and the capture script fails if it does not.

\textbf{★ Six of the C oracle's empty rows now have bodies}, including the one that cost four
rungs: \code{0x20802a08} answers 20\,480. \emph{A genuinely zero-length reply is now
distinguishable from an unmeasured one} — which is the whole of \S\ref{sec:oraclelimits}'s fifth
limit, repaired at the source rather than worked around.

\paragraph{The ladder, enumerated rather than discovered one boot at a time.} A successful boot
demands \textbf{104} distinct controls. The failing \code{cap1} capture reaches \textbf{53}.
\textbf{53 are demanded by the successful boot and never asked for by \code{cap1}}, and two go
the other way. ⚠ \emph{The two 53s are a coincidence of this capture and the source doc goes out
of its way to deny a relationship between them}; this paper repeats the denial. And the ladder's
\emph{serve} obligation is at most \textbf{93}, not 104, for the reason in the next paragraph.

Each control is bucketed \textbf{DATA} (the reply is the whole answer; servable from a table),
\textbf{ACT} (params all \code{[in]}; the reply only acknowledges something that \emph{happened}),
\textbf{MIXED}, or \textbf{UNKNOWN} — statically, out of the driver source, with a citation to
both the params struct and the code that decides direction. \textbf{81 of 106 (76.4\%) are
bucketed with a citation}; 79 are DATA or ACT; \textbf{25 are UNKNOWN}. ⊘ \emph{The
classification is a reading, not a measurement}, and the docs keep the two in different
sentences.

\textbf{★ The 25 unknowns are not a tail — they are one wall.} Three are a binary-API class with
no export, no flags and no struct on the CPU side. The other 22 are GSS-legacy commands that RM
forwards verbatim to GSP with a comment saying, in NVIDIA's words, that it no longer supports
them and is forwarding them so GSP can decide. \textbf{None of the 22 appears in either vendored
driver tree}; the versions do not disagree, they are both silent. \emph{The ladder is not 53 hard
problems; it is 31 easy ones and one closed-firmware wall.}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ And the finding that bears hardest on our own design: a real GSP refuses 13 controls
on the boot that works.} Eleven come back \code{NV\_ERR\_NOT\_SUPPORTED} unconditionally; two are
conditional. \textbf{\code{NV\_ERR\_NOT\_SUPPORTED} is \code{0x56} — the same status this port
emits when nobody claims a command.}

\smallskip
\textbf{And CPU-RM anticipates 7 of the 7 it can name.} Six are on a list RM keeps of commands
whose unsupported status it declines even to \emph{log}; the seventh has an explicit tolerance
arm at its call site that converts the refusal into a normal branch. The four it does not
anticipate are exactly the four GSS-legacy ones, \textbf{which the source cannot name}. ⊘ Scope,
honestly: the list RM keeps is written about a vGPU guest, so it evidences that RM has a named
class of legitimately-unsupported controls containing these ids — not by itself a statement about
the bare-metal path. \textbf{What makes the pairing strong is that the refusal was \measured{} on
a real GA106 while the anticipation was \inferred{} from source. Two instruments, one answer,
neither doing the other's job.}

\smallskip
⇒ \textbf{Refusal is ordinary protocol behaviour, not merely our posture.} That is a real
strengthening of this project's refusal-first design: for eleven controls, refusing is
demonstrably what the real device does on a boot that then works.
\end{claimbox}

\textbf{⊘ But refusal is not a free pass, and the correction here is worth more than the rule.}
An earlier document concluded from the two conditional refusals that \emph{"a static policy
cannot express it."} \textbf{That was retracted as too strong.} A policy keyed on the
\emph{command id} cannot; a policy keyed on the \emph{params} expresses one of them exactly — the
status is a deterministic function of the argument, re-measured identically across both
independent bring-ups in the trace — and is the only shape that could ever express the other.
\textbf{The surviving distinction is cmd-keyed versus params-keyed, not static versus dynamic.}
One row cannot be served in any form: two identical requests return different live PCIe byte
counters.

\textbf{And two facts a replay needs are independent.} A control can be \textbf{DATA and still be
refused by hardware}: two controls bucketed DATA off a clean reading of the driver get
\code{NV\_ERR\_NOT\_SUPPORTED} from a real GA106 on a boot that then works. \emph{The bucket says
what the reply means; it never said the reply exists.}

\paragraph{What the recorder cannot see, stated by its own author.} It answers \emph{what a
well-behaved boot demands}, and nothing else. It is a strong \emph{positive} oracle and a weak
negative one — it says nothing about what the driver does when refused, when something arrives
out of order, or late, so our refusal-first cases must be \emph{authored}, not recorded. It
covers one part, one kernel, one driver, and only the open driver — the closed one cannot be
instrumented and the correspondence is \assumed{}. Nothing in \code{crates/} reads the format.
And ⊘ \textbf{observational neutrality is not proven}: the hooks add a \code{memcpy} of up to
64\,KiB under a spinlock on the RPC path, the 88/88 cross-validation is against a
\emph{differently instrumented} build of the same driver, and \textbf{no measurement in that task
establishes what an uninstrumented driver issues}. ⚠ For the same reason this paper does not call
it a stock-driver capture: it is real GA106 silicon and real GSP firmware, with the CPU-side
driver rebuilt from stock \emph{source} plus two hooks.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★ The bridge, and the two NVIDIA findings that survived being right}{The bridge, and the two NVIDIA findings that survived being right}}
\label{sec:bridge}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{This section has been the paper's headline twice and been falsified twice.} Revision two:
\emph{"the critical path is the bridge, not the crate."} Revision four: \emph{"the bridge exists
and it does \emph{not} unlock compute."} \textbf{Compute is unlocked} (\S\ref{sec:ladder}), so the
second is retired with the first.

\smallskip
★ \textbf{What is worth keeping is the part that was a claim about NVIDIA rather than about our
schedule}, and both such claims held up. They are stated below unchanged, because \emph{they are
the reason the address plane had to be built out of three unusual sources instead of the obvious
one} — and a reader who wants to know why this system's address table looks the way it does will
find the answer here rather than in the architecture section. If you read one supporting document,
read \S2.7 of \file{docs/design/gsp\_core\_bridge.md}.
\end{claimbox}

\subsubsection*{What was built}

\code{translate(\&DriverAbiTable, \&RpcCommand)} is a stateless free function; \code{GraphPolicy}
is the \code{CommandPolicy} the boot FSM calls, doing translate\,$\rightarrow$\,\code{Gpu::apply}
\,$\rightarrow$\,reply; \code{kayfabe-abi} gained the per-class alloc table and four params
decoders; and the control arm (function 76) decodes what it can model and \textbf{refuses the rest
by name}. The refusal set is fifteen variants wide and every one of them carries the number that
caused it, because the census is the stage's real output: it says which decoder is missing, not
merely that one is.

\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{★★ \code{MAP\_MEMORY\_DMA} is not on the wire, so the bridge cannot deliver compute}{MAP\_MEMORY\_DMA is not on the wire, so the bridge cannot deliver compute}}

This is the finding to carry away, and it is a roadmap fact rather than a code fact.

\begin{warnbox}\small
On a GSP-client GA106, \code{rpcMapMemoryDma} (function 14) and \code{rpcUnmapMemoryDma}
(function 15) are \textbf{\code{\_STUB} through the entire HAL chain} — \code{\_GA106}
$\rightarrow$ \code{\_GA102} $\rightarrow$ \code{\_GA100} — and the stub returns
\code{RPC\_UNKNOWN\_FUNCTION}. The real implementation is installed only by the \emph{vGPU}
IP-version table. \textbf{So \code{RmEvent::MapMemoryDma} has no producer on this path, and no
stage of this bridge builds an \code{NVOS46} decoder.}
\end{warnbox}

Three independent oracles agree, which is why this is stated flatly: the open kernel modules'
own generated HAL tables; the C artifact, which booted a real GA106 guest to CUDA and has
\textbf{no function-14 hook at all} in its complete snoop set (47/76/21/103/10/65/70/72/73); and
two C-era design documents that say it in words — \emph{"there is no per-map GSP-RPC carrying
VA$\leftrightarrow$phys"}.

\textbf{★★ The consequence, and it is the one prediction in this paper that was both correct and
acted on.} If no RPC carries VA$\leftrightarrow$phys, then the address table cannot be populated
from the object model at all, and \emph{finishing the bridge would leave the address plane empty}.
That is what the previous revision said, and it was right: the compute path was never one bridge
stage away, it was a different plane. \textbf{So the different plane was built}, out of the three
sources \S\ref{sec:ladder} enumerates — the \code{GPU\_PROMOTE\_CTX} snoop, the doorbell-time GR
page-table sweep, and the CE page-table write latched at the release semaphore. None of them is an
\code{RmEvent}; \code{GPU\_PROMOTE\_CTX} in particular declares a \emph{set} of
\code{(gpuPhysAddr, gpuVirtAddr, size, bufferId)} entries, which is an address-table population
rather than an object-model edge, and it was deliberately kept out of this seam for that reason.
\emph{The seam discipline held: a fact that is not object-model shaped did not get forced through
the object-model door.}

\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{★ \code{SET\_PAGE\_DIRECTORY} is not how an ordinary address space declares its root}{SET_PAGE_DIRECTORY is not how an ordinary address space declares its root}}

B4 was built to decode the control that carries a page-directory base. It settled the question
\emph{against} the design that asked it.

On a bare-metal GSP client, \code{SET\_PAGE\_DIRECTORY} reaches the wire \textbf{only} for an
address space marked \code{SHARED\_\allowbreak MANAGEMENT} or \code{IS\_\allowbreak EXTERNALLY\_OWNED} —
\code{gvaspaceExternalRootDirCommit\_IMPL} asserts on exactly that, and its only callers are the
UVM / gpu-ops path. Every \emph{ordinary} RM-managed VAS declares its root through
\code{NV90F1\_CTRL\_CMD\_\allowbreak VASPACE\_\allowbreak COPY\_\allowbreak SERVER\_\allowbreak RESERVED\_PDES}
(\code{0x90f10106}) at construct time, as \code{levels[0].physAddress}, on a path that is on by
default for any GSP client. There is a third:
\code{NV2080\_CTRL\_CMD\_\allowbreak INTERNAL\_\allowbreak GMMU\_\allowbreak COPY\_\allowbreak RESERVED\_\allowbreak SPLIT\_\allowbreak GVASPACE\_\allowbreak PDES\_TO\_SERVER}.

The design's arm was therefore \textbf{necessary and not sufficient}. All three commands are
refused as a \emph{distinct} variant, \code{PageDirControlNotModelled\{cmd\}}, rather than folded
into the generic unknown-control refusal — and the reason is the sharpest small argument in the
bridge: \textbf{a dropped page-directory declaration is invisible}. The VAS never routes, every
channel defers at its first doorbell forever, and nothing anywhere says why. A separate refusal
variant converts a silent hang into a counted, named record of exactly which decoder the address
plane is waiting on.

Two smaller results from the same arm are worth keeping because they are the kind of thing a
version table does not catch. \code{hVASpace == 0} is \textbf{not} "unspecified": NVIDIA's own
header says verbatim, in both vendored trees, that zero means \emph{the implicit VA space
associated with the client/device pair} — a real object the RPC does not name and the graph has
no node for, so passing it through would park a PDB on a node the guest never declared. It is
refused. And note the deliberate asymmetry: on the \emph{alloc} arm, a zero handle means "nothing
declared" and maps to \code{None}. Same zero, opposite meaning, documented differently by NVIDIA
— so neither may be inferred from the other.

\subsubsection*{Where the design met the compiler}

Roughly two dozen items are recorded across the four commit messages, and the pattern in them is
more useful than any single one: the design's \emph{taxonomy} claims survived and its
\emph{buildability} claims did not. "Known-and-inert versus unknown, there is no third state" was
contradicted by the first stage's own row — there are at least four states. Three separate
non-vacuity arms the test plan specified turned out to be unbuildable at the stage that specified
them, one of them because it was also \emph{wrong}: refusing a second, different PDB for one
VASpace requires remembering the first, which a stateless bridge cannot do and which
\code{RmGraph} had already decided the other way, with an argument (re-binding is protocol-legal;
refusing would hang a conforming guest).

One item is a finding the design does not have and could not have had: \textbf{functions 72 and
73 never reach the policy at all.} The FSM returns on a no-reply disposition \emph{before} calling
\code{respond}, so those arms are unreachable through this adapter. Harmless today, because
neither carries object-model content — and load-bearing to know, because a future no-reply
function that \emph{did} carry a fact would be dropped silently.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★ A live-memory-free defect --- and the independent oracle had the identical bug}{A live-memory-free defect --- and the independent oracle had the identical bug}}
\label{sec:oracle}

This is the most serious defect the project has found in its own core, and the part that matters
is not the defect.

\textbf{The defect, one line wide.} \code{RmGraph::apply} drained its three parked-fact tables
from the \code{Alloc} arm only, on the reading that a parked fact waits for its target's
\emph{allocation}. It waits for its target's \textbf{handle} — and \code{Alloc} and \code{Dup}
are the two events that mint one. So a dup-of-a-dup whose middle handle was minted by a
\code{Dup} stayed parked \emph{forever} while being fully resolvable.

\textbf{The measured consequence.} \code{project()} follows parked dups through
\code{origin\_of}, so the process grouping saw the alias; the refcount never did. The undercount
takes references to zero, the resource is destroyed, the component vanishes, the process is
vacated — and \textbf{the reaper emits \code{Free} for memory a live kernel alias still
references.} That is the project's own "never free host memory RM says is live" rule, reached
through a door nobody was watching. It was three defects, not one: the same missing drain also
left \code{Resource::pdb} unset for a parked page-directory declaration and never installed a
parked mapping, giving a fault on a \emph{legal} guest.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★ And the independent oracle carried the identical bug.} The property suite's
\code{RefTracker} — the deliberately separate reference-count model whose entire purpose is to
disagree with the implementation — also promoted parked facts only in its \code{Alloc} arm. Model
and implementation were wrong \emph{the same way}, so the property stayed green over a
three-defect bug for as long as both existed. The correction is proven to be a strengthening
rather than an assertion: with the model fixed and the implementation's drain removed again, the
property fails on its own.
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{Why this is the most instructive entry in the paper.} An independent oracle is this
project's strongest single testing idea — it is what \code{kayfabe-abi}'s three transcriptions
are, what \code{gspworld} is, and what the bridge's hand-hex fixtures are. This is the failure
mode of that idea, in its purest form: \emph{independently written} is not \emph{independently
reasoned}. The same author, holding the same wrong sentence about what a parked fact waits for,
wrote it into both. Nothing about the shape of the test caught it; what caught it was a different
invariant, derived from the other side of the graph, disagreeing.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★ The quality instruments were wrong --- three of them, in one day}{The quality instruments were wrong --- three of them, in one day}}
\label{sec:instruments}

This is the real theme of the work since the last pin. The code got better; what got \emph{much}
better is the confidence one is entitled to have in the instruments that say so. Each of the
three below was a live, silent, one-directional error in the machinery this document quotes.

\paragraph{1. The mutation configuration was never read.}
\code{mutants.toml} sat at the workspace root. cargo-mutants reads
\file{.cargo/mutants.toml}. \textbf{The file was never opened, and every exclusion argued inside
it was inert} — including the exclusion of \code{kayfabe-mocks} and \file{tests/src}, which is the
one this paper has repeatedly relied on when saying "excluding scaffolding".

It was proven rather than inferred, which is the right standard for a claim of this shape:
appending a bogus key to the root file changed nothing and produced no parse error; appending the
same key to \file{.cargo/mutants.toml} fails loudly. \code{cargo mutants --list} goes from
\textbf{3\,760} targets to \textbf{2\,667}, and scaffolding mutants listed goes from \textbf{361}
to \textbf{0}.

\textbf{The consequence for every number this document has ever quoted.} Every mutation figure
recorded before 2026-07-28 was measured over a population that \emph{included the instruments},
and the "$\approx$94.9\% excluding scaffolding" figure — which this paper printed at the previous
revision — was a \textbf{hand computation}, not something the tool ever did. That is exactly why
it read as a parenthetical rather than as the gate: it was never the gate.

\paragraph{2. The gate could report 100\% from a broken suite.}
cargo-mutants baselines with \code{--package=} naming only the mutated packages, while the
mutants themselves run \code{--workspace}. Measured: the baseline runs about 200 tests across 39
binaries and
\textbf{omits \code{kayfabe-tests} entirely} — the conformance suite that catches most mutants. A
red conformance suite would therefore pass the baseline and then make \emph{every} mutant look
caught. Fixed with \code{needs: stable}, which makes the real suite a precondition of the gate
rather than an assumption of it.

The same job's \code{--timeout 240} sat at or below honest completion (the workspace suite alone
is 201\,s on a 25-core box, and a 4-vCPU runner at \code{-j 2} is slower). \textbf{cargo-mutants
scores a \code{TIMEOUT} as \emph{killed}}, so mass timeouts would have reported near-100\% and the
gate would never have fired. Raised to an absolute 1\,800\,s — and deliberately \emph{not} to a
\code{--timeout-multiplier}, because the multiplier is computed off the same baseline that omits
the suite, and would reintroduce the identical inflation through a different door.

\paragraph{3. Thirteen test park-points hung forever instead of failing, producing zero output.}
The shape is a \code{thread::scope} whose cleanup is reached only by statements \emph{after} the
assertions: any failed assert unwinds past them, and the scope blocks forever joining threads that
were never told to stop.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{It had already happened, for twenty-three hours.} Three wedged test binaries were found
alive on the box, three threads each, all parked in \code{futex\_do\_wait}, at $\sim$83\,000\,s.
And the failure mode is worse than "buried": under CI-realistic output capture \textbf{the panic
message is never emitted at all} — the log stops at \code{running 1 test} and stays empty
forever. One site hung \emph{spinning}, burning a full core, and outlived the \code{cargo} process
that spawned it.
\end{warnbox}

Every one of the thirteen was \textbf{proven vulnerable before and proven fixed after by inducing
a real failure at each site}, not by reading the code — which is the same bite-check discipline
the project applies to its assertions, applied to its harness. The fixes are \code{Drop} guards
armed as the first statement inside each scope closure, plus a barrier type that panics instead of
blocking when its rendezvous breaks. Each guard carries an in-comment R1 argument naming its
callee, because \emph{a \code{Drop} is a call site} and R1 does not guard \code{Drop}.

\paragraph{And a fourth, smaller, which is the same species.} The test count itself was wrong:
551 came from \textbf{stale prebuilt binaries} in \file{target/}. Two test files had 16 and 32
\code{\#[test]}s at HEAD while the inherited cache ran 13 and 24. A related trap bit twice in one
day inside the bite harnesses: \code{cp -a} and \code{shutil.move} both preserve mtimes, so a
restored file keeps the previous mutation's compiled artifact and a whole bite table comes back
\emph{inflated}. Any harness that restores files must \code{touch} them.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{What these four have in common, and why they belong in a whitepaper rather than a
changelog.} Every one of them made the project look \emph{better} than it was, and every one was
invisible from the output. An inert config, an omitted baseline, a timeout scored as a kill, a
hang that prints nothing, and a stale artifact are all the same failure: \textbf{the instrument
reported a number that no longer described a measurement}. This is the project's own founding
doctrine — \emph{"a green instrument on an unexercised path is worse than no instrument"} — turned
around and pointed at the instruments themselves, and it is the strongest available evidence that
the doctrine is being applied rather than quoted.
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{kayfabe-gsp}: boot is modelled, reboot is modelled and never exercised, and no teardown has ever been run}{kayfabe-gsp: boot is modelled, reboot is modelled and never exercised}}
\label{sec:gsp}

\begin{claimbox}\small
⊘⊘⊘ \textbf{SUPERSEDED IN ITS TITLE, 2026-08-21 --- read \S\ref{sec:pcd7} first.} This
subsection is called \emph{"reboot is modelled and never exercised"}. \textbf{It has now been
exercised} --- eight consecutive device opens in one boot, driven by nothing more exotic than
\code{nvidia-smi} --- \textbf{and it broke on the fifth}, permanently, from a message-queue
sequence carried across a driver life. The prediction below that this was the untested seam was
\emph{correct}; what it could not say is that the first workload to touch it would find a defect
in the first five seconds. ★ The sharper form of the warning stands and is now evidenced:
\textbf{"modelled" and "exercised" are different words, and the gap between them was hiding a
bug that makes the product unusable after five invocations of its simplest command.}

\smallskip
\textbf{Two revisions ago this section said "34 lines, and it is the critical path". One revision
ago it said "3\,550 lines, and nothing calls it". Both are now superseded}: the crate is
\textbf{5\,497} lines across 8 modules, it has \emph{six} production consumers, a stock guest
driver boots against it to a working \code{nvidia-smi} and then computes, and the one invariant
this paper flagged as prose-only — GSP-S1 — has been paid off in code.

\smallskip
\textbf{What has changed below, and it is the subsection title.} Three of the plan's nine stages
are still unbuilt and all three need a GPU. \textbf{But "reboot is not modelled" was too strong} —
the boot FSM carries suspend and reload states and transitions. ★ \textbf{What is true is
sharper and worse}: they have \textbf{never been exercised against a real guest driver reload},
and the core has \textbf{no reset entry point of any kind}, so on a guest \code{modprobe -r} the
graph, every live process with its sandboxes, arenas and host handles, both routing maps and the
guest-physical window all survive into the next driver's life with nothing reclaimed. ⊘ The
project's own words: this limitation is \emph{"inherited here by omission rather than by decision,
which is worse, because nothing names it."} \textbf{The item that used to occupy this slot —
\S11-O7a — is resolved} (below). And the plan the crate was built from was written against the
wrong driver version, a fact that keeps producing new consequences rather than being absorbed
once.
\end{claimbox}

\subsubsection*{What is built, and what "built" is worth here}

The five seams the crate claims — GPU architecture, driver version, hypervisor, GPU lifetime, CPU
architecture — each have a test that pushes on them, which is the difference between a seam and a
decoration. Two are worth singling out because they are cheap to fake and were not faked. The
lifetime seam is exercised by running \emph{repeated} driver lives in one process: the C
artifact's measured second-life failure (\code{msgqRxLink} timeout, \code{-7}, 71\,064 retries)
has exactly one cause in the driver's own source, \code{rx.size != size}, and the FSM is shaped so
that cause cannot arise. The architecture seam is exercised by driving the same FSM through
\emph{two} deliberately-different register models, which is the same argument as \code{MockArch}
one layer down.

\textbf{The oracle situation has changed completely since the previous revision, and it is the
one unambiguously good piece of news in this section.} That revision said: \emph{"Those traces do
not exist: the capture patch is $\sim$60 lines against a C file this work does not own, and it has
not landed."} \textbf{It landed.} Five captures exist, two are committed into this repository
decompressed, and \code{kayfabe-crec} replays them against the real FSM in the default suite. ⊘
\textbf{And no replay has been closed} — see \S\ref{sec:oraclelimits}: \code{cap1} stopped on a
missing observation at transaction 978, \code{cap1b} carries it to 1028, and what stops it there
is a plane the C's recorder cannot witness at all. \emph{"Byte-equivalent with the C" is
therefore established up to a stated transaction and not beyond it}, which is a far better
position than untested and is not the same as tested.

\subsubsection*{Boot is modelled. Reboot and resume are not.}

The plan has nine stages. S0--S5 — the six that need no GPU — are the boot and steady-state
protocol, and they are built. \textbf{S6, S7 and S8 are the three that need a GPU, and none is
built.} The bench-offline blocker that also held S6 is gone (\S\ref{sec:bench}), so what remains
is work rather than an obstacle — but it has not been done, and the two open questions behind it
(\textbf{O3}, whether sequence numbers reset across a true \code{rmmod}/\code{insmod};
\textbf{O5}, which boot mode a post-teardown re-acquire uses) are still answerable \emph{only} by
a bench run. This matters more than the stage count suggests, because the owner's standing
requirement is that a GPU restart — idle, released, re-acquired — must work \emph{with no
bolt-on}, and restart is exactly the region S6--S8 covers. \textbf{And a boot that dies in
\code{RmInitAdapter} never reaches a teardown}, so nothing on the bench exercises this region
even incidentally. Boot is the part that could be modelled offline, so boot is the part that got
modelled.

\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{⊘ RESOLVED: the "sharpest open item" was closed the day after this paper last called it unresolvable}{RESOLVED: the sharpest open item was closed}}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ This subsection is kept, struck, because the correction is worth more than the item.}
The previous revision printed \S11-O7a as \emph{"the one item in the whole component with no
proposed resolution, because the evidence that would settle it is not in any open-source tree"},
and listed it in its risk register as the one thing that \emph{"is not yet a known quantity."}
\textbf{It was resolved on 2026-08-06} — before three of the four subsequent whitepaper
revisions were written — and this paper carried it forward as open for two more.

\smallskip
\textbf{The resolution, on both driver versions.} CPU-assist events are \code{emit-never}, and the
version split is \emph{not} "RPC versus local": on \textbf{both} 580 and 610 every route to
\code{CORE\_RESUME} begins with a \emph{GSP$\to$CPU} event and the CPU driver never starts one
itself. Only which event carries it differs. The opcode family exists so the GSP can borrow the
CPU \textbf{while real silicon is down}, and an emulated GSP has no down-silicon phase; nothing on
the driver side waits for one, because the boot wait is \code{rpcRecvPoll(GSP\_INIT\_DONE)}.
\measured{}: the C artifact contains \emph{zero} occurrences of the sequencer and a stock
580.159.04 driver nonetheless completed cold boot $\to$ \code{cuCtxCreate} $\to$ matmul $\to$
teardown $\to$ GSP reload.

\smallskip
★★ \textbf{And the premise underneath it — "the evidence is in closed firmware" — is dead too.}
\measured{} 2026-08-06 from a committed trace of a real GA106: a real GSP sends \textbf{exactly
one} \code{RUN\_CPU\_SEQUENCER} per bring-up, \code{rpc\_len = 6328}, roughly 200\,ms
\emph{before} \code{GSP\_INIT\_DONE}. It is ordinary cold boot and the buffer is byte-decodable.
⊘ Replay is still refused, on a different and better argument: it would be \emph{a too-capable
double}, obliging us to emulate falcon halt bits, SEC2 start, a scratch-register handoff, a
mailbox and ${\sim}104$ register polls each with its own timeout — binding the port to one chip's
boot script to serve a round trip whose purpose (reviving real silicon) has no referent here.

\smallskip
\textbf{⚠ What actually remains open is smaller and differently shaped}, and it is the residual
risk \code{emit-never} names for itself: \emph{a handler side effect we skip that something later
reads}. Two candidates are named rather than assumed away — an \code{appVersion} write and the
mailbox stores the GSP reads back after \code{CORE\_RESUME}. Both \emph{should} be vacuous because
we own producer and consumer, \textbf{so the stage that lands must assert it rather than assume
it.} That is a test obligation, not an unanswerable question.
\end{warnbox}

\smallskip
\textbf{The original item, struck, for the reader who wants to see the shape of the mistake.}
It read: this is the one item with \textbf{no proposed resolution}, because the evidence that
would settle it is not in any open-source tree.

The mechanism is present at both driver versions and reads identically: a core resume resets into
RISC-V, re-programs the LibOS boot-args address, starts SEC2 and waits on
\code{NV\_PGC6\_BSI\_SECURE\_\allowbreak SCRATCH\_14.\_BOOT\_STAGE\_3\_HANDOFF}. What differs
is \textbf{who initiates it}:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.6cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{580.159.04 (the bench)} & \textbf{610.43.02 (the vendored spec)} \\
\midrule
where the wait lives & inside the CPU sequencer executor, as opcode \code{CORE\_RESUME} &
  promoted to a first-class HAL, \code{kgspExecuteCoreResume\_TU102} \\
\addlinespace
how it is reached & \textbf{only} through \code{\_kgspRpcRunCpuSequencer} — i.e.\ only if
  \emph{we send} a \code{GSP\_RUN\_CPU\_SEQUENCER} event &
  locally, from two call sites in the driver. \textbf{Nothing is required of us.} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

Three things follow, and none of them is comfortable.

\textbf{It is not a layout difference, so a version table does not absorb it.} Every other
580-vs-610 divergence the project has found is an offset, a field count or a constant — one row in
a version-keyed table. This one is a difference in \emph{who initiates}, which means a faked GSP
that must emit sequencer buffers at 580 and must not at 610 has version-conditional
\emph{behaviour}. \file{docs/design/four\_axes\_of\_variation.md} \S5 rule 1 forbids exactly that:
\emph{"No \code{if guest\_version == \dots} in a logic crate. Transitions fire on observed protocol
facts, never on driver identity."} The two honest options named in the correction brief are a
version-keyed \emph{capability} on the FSM or a loud refusal, and the brief declines to choose
between them on the grounds that choosing requires an answer it does not have.

\textbf{The answer cannot be read out of the open source.} What the open tree shows is that the
path exists and how it is triggered. What it cannot show is whether the firmware ever triggers it
on a path we must support, because the \emph{contents} of a sequencer buffer come from GSP-RM
firmware, which is not open. Specifying an emitter now would be guessing, and the plan says so
rather than shipping a guess.

⊘ \textbf{And the conclusion that was drawn from it — struck.} It read: \emph{"the requirement is
genuinely at risk \dots\ 'GPU restart must work with no bolt-on' is not currently satisfiable by
the design as written."} \textbf{The next step named was correct} — \emph{"a reading task, not a
hardware task"} — and doing that reading is what dissolved the item. ★ The general form is the one
this paper keeps re-learning: \textbf{an item recorded as unresolvable because "the evidence is in
closed firmware" is worth one more read before it is believed}, because the boundary between open
and closed source is itself a claim, and this one was wrong.

One further sharpening worth carrying, because it cuts against the project's own instinct: the
relayed finding that started this said "580 has a resume handoff 610 never reads". \textbf{That
half was wrong} — 610 has the identical routine reading the identical register. The correction log
reports it as wrong rather than quietly fixing it, which is the behaviour a reviewer should be
checking for.

\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{★ The plan was written against the wrong driver, and one item was RESOLVED backwards}{The plan was written against the wrong driver, and one item was RESOLVED backwards}}

This is the most instructive thing that happened to this component, and it is not flattering.

The port plan took its NVIDIA citations from the vendored open kernel modules at
\textbf{610.43.02}. \textbf{The bench runs 580.159.04.} The two do not merely differ in offsets;
they differ behaviourally, and 3\,550 lines were built against the un-caveated plan before anyone
vendored the matching tree. Eight claims changed when \code{ogkm-580.159.04} was finally vendored
(\code{7af23cb}): the element count is \emph{read from a field} at 580 and \emph{derived from a
length} at 610; MCTP/NVDM transport headers do not exist at all on the 580 GSP path; the layout
break interval narrowed from \code{(570, 610]} to \code{(595.84, 610.43.02]}; the init RPCs are
queued \emph{before} bootstrap at 580 rather than inside it; the suspend sentinel is an exact
equality at 580 and a mask at 610; and the queue geometry is compile-time at 580 rather than
negotiated, which removes the "the guest declares its own geometry" argument the plan had
presented as its highest-leverage design choice.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★ And at this revision the same seam bit inside a range the project claims to support,
which is a strictly worse shape than "the plan cited the wrong tree".}
\code{NV\_CHANNEL\_ALLOC\_PARAMS} \textbf{diverges at offset +32}: 610 inserts
\code{hHandleVASpace} there, where 580 — \emph{the bench driver} — has \code{hUserdMemory[0]}. A
generated 610 mirror would mis-read \textbf{every field from +32 onward} for the guest we actually
run, \code{engineType} included, and would do so \emph{plausibly} rather than loudly.

\smallskip
The response is the one the four-axes rule implies and it costs something: \textbf{the struct is
deliberately not generated at all}, only the 32-byte agreed prefix is decoded, and a test named
\code{the\_channel\_alloc\_prefix\_stops\_where\_the\_two\_trees\_stop\_agreeing} asserts the
\emph{absence} of the generated struct so that nobody helpfully adds it later. What that costs is
concrete: \code{engineType} lives past the prefix, and \textbf{a CUDA process's GR and CE channels
are the same \code{hClass}} — so \code{Arch::classify} cannot answer which engine a channel is
for, and a CE channel only becomes one later, via its engine object and a refinement pass. A
version-keyed \emph{table} does not absorb this; only a decoder that declines to decode does.
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{The worst instance is \S11-O7, which had been marked RESOLVED with the answer that is
backwards for our bench.} It said \code{GSP\_RUN\_CPU\_SEQUENCER} "is not implemented, do not emit
it" — true at 610, where the executor was deleted; at 580 it is fully implemented, dispatched, and
first on the bootup-window allowlist, so an emitted sequencer buffer would execute register writes
and falcon resets inside the guest kernel rather than being harmlessly ignored. The RESOLVED
status has been withdrawn.

\textbf{The class, which is the part worth keeping.} A \emph{versioned} specification was cited as
if it were \emph{the} specification. The fix is mechanical and now normative: every citation
carries its tag (\code{ogkm-580:} / \code{ogkm-610:}), an untagged claim is unverified by
definition, and line numbers \emph{and paths} never cross tags — the whole \code{msgq} library
moved directories between the two.

\textbf{★ That fix is being applied to new code and the backlog is getting worse, which is the
honest and unflattering measurement.} At the previous pin the crates carried \textbf{121} bare,
untagged \code{ogkm:} citations and \textbf{zero} tagged ones. At this revision there are
\textbf{56 tagged} citations — the rule is real and new work follows it — and the bare count has
risen to \textbf{159}, because four crates' worth of new code was written faster than the backlog
was converted. \code{kayfabe-gsp}'s own share fell slightly, 96 to 92. \textbf{The CI grep that
would stop the class recurring is specified and still not written}, and it is now the only thing
standing between a normative rule and a growing pile of counter-examples. The task is filed
(correction-brief item B11) and was explicitly not reached.

The self-critical note in the correction log is the one to read: one claim in the same document
was marked \code{[inferred]} and turned out to be right, while O7 was marked RESOLVED and turned
out to be backwards — and the difference was not luck. \emph{The inferred claim knew it was
second-hand and the resolved one did not.}

\subsubsection*{What this does and does not change about the critical path}

\textbf{For three revisions this subsection said the gap had moved up one layer and had not
closed. It has closed, and the answer was not what any of those revisions predicted.} The L2
adapter exists, \code{GuestRam} has a production implementor, \code{GraphPolicy} is constructed
outside \code{tests/}, and \code{DriverAbiTable::gsp\_element} has a caller: the shipped register
plane selects a wire layout to answer a real guest through. The version-dispatch machinery
finally has something production-side asking it a question.

\textbf{And the critical path turned out never to have been any of the layers this paper kept
nominating.} It was not the crate, then the bridge, then the L2 adapter. \textbf{It is the
execution plane} (\S\ref{sec:execplane}) — a plane no amount of protocol modelling reaches,
because the guest driver's own acceptance test for it is a functional copy with a read-back
comparison. \emph{Read \S\ref{sec:bridge} with that in hand}: even a complete bridge fed by a
complete adapter produces a correct object model and an address table that only one of its two
sources fully populates.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★ Worth noticing about this document rather than about the code.} Three consecutive
revisions each identified "the critical path", each was invalidated within days by the work it
named, and \emph{each was invalidated in the same direction} — the named obstacle got built and
was not the obstacle. That is a pattern in the paper's own reasoning, not in the project's
execution: \textbf{a layer that is missing is easy to see, and a plane that cannot be faked is
not a layer.} It is recorded here because the fourth answer above should be read with exactly
that much suspicion.
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★★★ Sequential multi-process: the wall fell, three processes pass, and the fourth does not}{Sequential multi-process: the wall fell, three processes pass, and the fourth does not}}
\label{sec:seqfix}

\begin{warnbox}\small
⊘⊘ \textbf{FRAMING SUPERSEDED 2026-08-21 by \S\ref{sec:pcd7}: the ordinal is not over CUDA
processes, it is over DEVICE OPENS.} A bare \code{nvidia-smi} --- no CUDA, no context --- both
consumes the resource and dies of it, at \#5 of 8. Every measurement below is still correct as
measured; only the word \emph{"process"} in the reasoning is wrong, and it kept the search inside
CUDA for an evening. The framebuffer-join defect it names is real and was really fixed; it was
simply not the whole of what this workload was hitting.

\smallskip
\textbf{★★★★★ This subsection was added after the rest of the paper was written, and it falsifies
the paper's own headline blocker.} Everything in it is dated \code{w361}--\code{w367} and landed
in the five hours \emph{after} this document's pin (\code{5c367a38}, 15:36) and before its first
reading (\code{56dc01f3}, 20:32). The claim it replaces --- \emph{"a second CUDA context
sequentially in one boot still hangs"} --- is left standing wherever it appeared, corrected in
place rather than deleted, per this paper's own house style (\S\ref{sec:drift}).
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{What it was.} \measured{} boots \code{w361}, \code{w362}, \code{w363} (revs
\code{3887be37} and \code{ecb67fae}), real GA106, internally controlled inside each boot: the
\emph{first} guest CUDA process rang 403 \code{GrCompute} + 104 \code{Ce} doorbells and got
\textbf{8/8} completions; \emph{every later one} rang exactly \textbf{8 + 8} and got
\textbf{0/8}, then spun forever (\code{R} state, 100\% CPU, unbounded at a 600\,s step limit).
★ The effect was \textbf{ordinal, not predecessor-kind}: an \code{A2} arm --- a second torch
process with no \code{nvidia-smi} between --- failed identically, which retracted this project's
own earlier reading that a predecessor \emph{poisoned} its successor.

\textbf{The cause, named by our own refusal text.} Three GR context frames --- \code{0x400000}
(\code{SET\_VALID\_\allowbreak SPAN\_\allowbreak OVERFLOW\_AREA}), \code{0x600000} (\code{SET\_TEX\_HEADER\_POOL}),
\code{0x800000} (\code{SET\_TEX\_SAMPLER\_POOL}) --- were refused 48 times each with \emph{"that
framebuffer range is already joined; installing a second backing over it would give one leaf two
memories"}. The first process joined all three; the later ones got \emph{zero} joins, so their GR
context had no host backing, so nothing completed and \code{libcuda} waited forever. Everything
upstream was identical between the working and failing processes and everything downstream was
proportional to doorbell count; ⊘ \textbf{the vocabulary diff was empty both ways} --- only the
\emph{count} diff could see this.

⊘ \textbf{And the takeover mechanism that already existed could not fix it \emph{by
construction}.} \code{supersede\_\allowbreak joined\_\allowbreak fb\_leaf} routes by the \emph{caller's own}
\code{(gpu, pdb)} and searches only that address space, while the framebuffer store is
per-\emph{device} and keyed by physical address --- so a join left by a different, exited process
lives under another page-directory base and is unreachable to it. Arming it harder does nothing:
32 successful supersedes in the same boot and \textbf{zero} on these frames.

\textbf{The two fixes.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.4ex]
\item \textbf{Orphan reclaim} (\code{4d88723c}, \code{w366}). \measured{} \code{w365}: all 48
  refusals of each of the three frames, arriving from \emph{two independent} later processes,
  censused \code{live=0 retired=0}. \textbf{The framebuffer store held a join that no address
  table anywhere named} --- refusing it refused on behalf of nobody. The fix gives the store's
  join back at the \code{SUPERSEDE NO-ROW} branch when neither a live nor a retired row names the
  frame, recomputed at the decision point rather than read from the diagnostic's cache.
  ★ \textbf{The cross-process boundary is kept, and it is measured}: \code{live > 0} stays refused
  \emph{by name} --- \textbf{132} times in the passing boot. A live peer's backing is never taken.
\item \textbf{Re-key the takeover cap} (\code{cca2eb4b}, \code{w367}).
  \code{SUPERSEDE\_CAP\_PER\_FRAME = 4} was keyed by \emph{frame} and reset by nothing, so a
  device-lifetime budget spent by early actors left later leaves \emph{fabricated} --- with no
  host backing behind them. \textbf{Our own bound then raised a host \code{Xid}}: \code{w366}
  recorded \code{Xid 31 FAULT\_PDE} on \code{CE2} at \code{0x7e59\_c6000000}, traced to
  \code{fb\_phys=0x1e00000}, which appears \code{CAPPED} nine times in our own log. Re-keyed by
  \code{(phys, taking va)}, a ping-pong between one pair of aliases is still bounded --- at
  $2\times$\,CAP for that pair --- while a genuinely new owner arriving later gets its own budget.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{The result.} \measured{} \code{w367}, rev \code{cca2eb4b}, real GA106, 600\,s step bounds,
one boot:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{arm} & \textbf{what it is} & \textbf{at \code{w363}} & \textbf{at \code{w367}} \\
\midrule
\code{A}  & first torch process & \code{TORCH\_OK} & \code{TORCH\_OK} \\
\code{A2} & second, \emph{no} \code{nvidia-smi} between & \code{TORCH\_HUNG} at 600\,s & \textbf{\code{TORCH\_OK}} \\
\code{C}  & torch after \code{nvidia-smi} ran and exited & \code{TORCH\_HUNG} at 600\,s & \textbf{\code{TORCH\_OK}} \\
\code{D}  & a \emph{fourth} process & \code{TORCH\_FAIL} & \code{TORCH\_FAIL} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\code{avail=True sum=4.0} on all three passing arms; completions \textbf{8/8 OBSERVED} for
\code{proc=2}, \code{proc=4} and \code{proc=7}; doorbells \textbf{104 \code{Ce} + 403
\code{GrCompute}, identically for all three}; the three frames at \code{refused=0 joined=3} each;
reclaim \code{ORPHAN-RECLAIMED=192 NOT-AN-ORPHAN=132 NO-OP=0}; host \code{dmesg} \textbf{0 lines,
0 \code{NVRM}, 0 \code{Xid}} --- both of \code{w366}'s faults gone.

\textbf{★ The identical doorbell counts are the strongest single line}: the second and third
processes now do exactly the same amount of work as the first, where two boots earlier they rang
eight doorbells and stalled forever.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{⊘ What this is NOT, stated at the width the evidence supports.}

\smallskip
\textbf{(1) Three is not unbounded.} \code{D}, the \emph{fourth} process, still fails ---
\code{avail=False}, \emph{"CUDA unknown error"}. \measured{} \code{w367}: only \emph{three}
processes ever existed for four workloads, so \code{D} never reached the device at all, and the
guest kernel logged nothing while it ran --- guest \code{dmesg}'s last line is at 28.99\,s uptime.
\textbf{It fails above the \code{ioctl} boundary and the cause is unknown.} ⚠ The \code{w367}
run's logs are not committed to this tree; this row rests on the boot's commit message
(\code{56dc01f3}) and on its operator's reading of the log, not on an artifact a reader can open.

\smallskip
\textbf{(2) The LLM workload has not been re-run at this revision}, so nothing here says it
passes, and \S\ref{sec:llmwall} should be read as pinned to \code{3887be37}. ⚠ \code{D}'s error
string is \emph{verbatim} the LLM workload's, which makes the two plausibly one defect that bites
after $\geq 2$ processes --- \textbf{deliberately not welded}: the \code{w355}/\code{w360} LLM
boots died \emph{earlier}, with \code{COMPLETION-WATCH = 0}.

\smallskip
\textbf{(3) A host object is leaked per reclaimed orphan frame} --- deliberately, and the log says
so. With no table row there is no host virtual address and no memory to hand to the revoke path;
\emph{the missing row is exactly what would have carried them}. Bounded at one object per orphaned
frame, and a frame can be orphaned only once. The follow-up is named: plumb the backing that
\code{FbStore::release\_join} already returns out through \code{RegPlane::release\_fb\_join},
which currently discards it.

\smallskip
\textbf{(4) $n = 1$ boot}, against this project's own measured rule that a single-boot green is
wrong \textbf{1 time in 5}. \unver{} at any larger $n$.
\end{claimbox}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★★ And the mechanism prediction was wrong in sign, which is the transferable part.} The
rung pre-registered \emph{"\code{SUPERSEDE CAPPED} falls sharply"} as its mechanism clause. It went
\textbf{202 $\rightarrow$ 5\,291}, and the frame that raised the \code{Xid} still reports
\code{refused=482} --- while \code{joined} went to 21 and the \code{CE} \code{Xid} disappeared.
Re-keying creates far \emph{more} tracked pairs, so \emph{more} \code{CAPPED} lines is the
expected consequence of the fix, not evidence against it. ⇒ \textbf{The outcome clauses were met
and the mechanism clause was measuring the wrong quantity.} \emph{Pre-register outcome and
mechanism separately, and say in advance which one grades the rung.}
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★★★ The fifth device open wedges the GPU --- and the code predicted it, then filed it as a trade-off}{The fifth device open wedges the GPU --- and the code predicted it}}
\label{sec:pcd7}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★★★ Added 2026-08-21, and it supersedes \S\ref{sec:seqfix}'s framing and
\S\ref{sec:gsp}'s subsection title at the same time.} \S\ref{sec:seqfix} calls the defect
\emph{"sequential multi-process"} and reasons about CUDA processes throughout.
\measured{} \code{w370}: running \code{nvidia-smi} \textbf{eight times} in one boot --- no CUDA
anywhere, no context ever created --- \textbf{\#1--4 print the GPU name and \#5--8 print "No
devices were found"}, permanently. A bare \code{nvidia-smi} is both a consumer of the resource
and a victim of its exhaustion. \textbf{The ordinal was never over CUDA processes. It is over
device opens.}

\smallskip
And \S\ref{sec:gsp} is titled \emph{"reboot is modelled and never exercised"}. It has now been
exercised, eight times in a row, by the cheapest possible workload --- \textbf{and it broke.}
\end{warnbox}

\subsubsection*{Why nothing had ever seen it}

A census of every probe log on the bench: \textbf{no boot has ever run more than five
GPU-touching processes}, and every boot before \code{w361} ran \emph{one or two}. The pattern was
always \emph{boot $\to$ one CUDA program $\to$ grade $\to$ tear down}. The defect lives at $N=5$;
every instrument stopped at $N=2$. ⊘ \textbf{This is therefore not a regression} --- it is a limit
that was always there and was never probed, and the reason it was never probed is that
\textbf{the project's own workflow documentation told everyone to reboot between runs.} The line
\emph{"the emulated GSP's WPR2 state only resets on a full QEMU restart, so each clean run needs
a fresh boot"} has been in this tree's contributor guide for months, written as a
\emph{convention}. It was a defect report the whole time.

\subsubsection*{The guest kernel's own words, and a decode error worth recording}

No boot in this series had ever captured guest \code{dmesg} \emph{during} a workload: the harness
took it at boot time, so all twelve boots wrote a \textbf{5\,379-byte, byte-identical} file whose
last line is at 28.99\,s uptime. That absence had already been reported upward as
\emph{"the guest kernel logged nothing"} --- a measured-sounding claim that had never been
measured. With the capture moved after the workload, the guest says:

\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
NVRM: GspMsgQueueReceiveStatus: Bad sequence number.  Expected 0 got 161.\\
NVRM: \ldots NV\_ERR\_INVALID\_DATA (0x25) from rpcRecvPoll(\ldots, GSP\_INIT\_DONE, 0)\\
NVRM: \_kgspBootGspRm: unexpected WPR2 already up, cannot proceed with booting GSP\\
NVRM: RmInitAdapter failed! (0x62:0x40:2028)
\end{quote}

⊘ \textbf{The triple is \code{initStatus:rmStatus:line}, and reading it as a status cost an
hour.} \code{0x62} decodes as \code{NV\_ERR\_RESET\_REQUIRED} if you assume it is an
\code{NV\_STATUS} --- plausible, thematically perfect, and wrong: it is an \code{RM\_INIT\_*} enum
and the actual status is the \emph{second} field, \code{0x40}. ★ The same class as this paper's
\code{\_IOC\_SIZE} finding: \textbf{a field parsed as the wrong type still yields a believable
name}. And the \code{0x40} is not even the first domino --- it is the \emph{retry's} failure at
the WPR2 gate. The poll returned \code{0x25}.

\subsubsection*{PC-D7: the comment that described this exactly, and filed it}

The root cause is that we post \code{GSP\_INIT\_DONE} carrying a message-queue sequence carried
out of the \emph{previous} driver life --- 161 --- into a queue the new life has just constructed
at \code{rxSeqNum = 0}. The guest recovers only from \code{seqNum < rxSeqNum}; for \code{>} there
is \textbf{no branch}, so the stream never resynchronises and every later open dies instantly at
the WPR2 gate.

★★★ \textbf{The code predicted this failure verbatim, symptom for symptom, and marked it
\emph{"a named trade-off, not a defect to be fixed"}.} The instance identity was the shared
region's \emph{guest-physical address}; the comment observes that the allocator recycles pages,
that a recycled page therefore reads as "same instance", and that the guest then sees
\code{seqNum > rxSeqNum} \emph{"permanently"}. It judged the collision rare because it modelled
the guest's queue as living from module load to module unload. \textbf{At 580 that queue lives and
dies with the \emph{adapter}}: every \code{nvidia-smi} destroys and reconstructs it, so
\emph{every} open is a coin flip on allocator reuse. That nondeterminism is why the failing
cycle wandered between 3 and 5 across boots and read like a counter --- and why a day went into
looking for a resource with four units.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{The lesson is not "write the hazard down".} It was written down, in the right place, in
detail, with the exact symptom. \textbf{The load-bearing claim was the \emph{frequency estimate}
attached to it} --- and that estimate, not the mechanism, was what made it a trade-off instead of
a bug. A hazard note without a measured rate is a hypothesis wearing a decision's clothes.
\end{claimbox}

\subsubsection*{The fix, and the two rejected fixes recorded beside it}

\measured{} \code{w374}, real GA106, \code{147694ff}. The discriminator is now the guest's own
command sequence rather than an address: the guest stamps its private \code{txSeqNum} into every
command element it submits and zeroes that counter only at queue construction, so the command
stream carries the instance's identity \textbf{in-band, in memory we already read}. The
address survives as a logged cross-check, and a publish where the two disagree is exactly this
bug happening --- now a counted line. ★ It fired on hardware:

\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
GSP-PUBLISH gpa=0x23200000 gpa\_same=true seq\_last=Some(1) cmd\_seq=162 $\Rightarrow$ same\_instance=false\\
\ \ ★ PC-D7 COLLISION: address and sequence disagree --- the address was lying
\end{quote}

\code{Bad sequence number} went \textbf{3 $\to$ 0}; cycles 1--4 are now completely clean.
⊘ \textbf{And the device still wedges at cycle 5}, of a \emph{different} cause --- a scrub-channel
event-notification failure --- which an independent analysis had pre-registered as a separate bug
that this fix would not touch. \textbf{The wall moved; it did not fall.}

★★ \textbf{Two rejected fixes are recorded at the source site so they are not retried.} The
obvious one --- releasing the instance identity at the driver-life boundary --- fails an existing
test that encodes the C artifact's own expensive lesson, because that boundary serves both a real
teardown and an idle release, which need opposite answers. Its "safe" repair, scoping to the
Booter-Unload transition, is \textbf{worse}: measurement of the 580 teardown path shows that
transition \textbf{never fires} in this configuration, because our own emulation takes WPR2 down
first and the guest's own gate then skips it. That variant would have been \textbf{inert on the
bench with every test green} --- the exact shape of a change that reads as done.

\subsubsection*{What this says about the amplifier}

★ \textbf{The "WPR2 already up" wedge is faithful to real hardware and is not the defect.} Any
single failed adapter init leaves the device unusable until the guest reboots; a real GPU behaves
the same way. So this was never one bug --- it is $N$ triggers behind one amplifier, and each
trigger has to be killed separately. \textbf{The amplifier is also why the symptom is so much
larger than any individual cause}, and why "the GPU stops working after the fifth
\code{nvidia-smi}" is a fair description of a defect whose actual mechanism is a
sequence-number comparison.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{What contact with hardware has and has not bought}{What contact with hardware has and has not bought}}
\label{sec:contact}

Stated once more, in its corrected form, because it is the thing most likely to be
half-remembered between here and the end of the paper.

\textbf{What is no longer true, and every one of these was printed by a previous revision of this
paper as a settled fact.} \emph{There is no binary} — false. \emph{\code{RmBackend} and
\code{IsolateFactory} have only mocks} — false. \emph{No isolate process has ever been spawned} —
false. \emph{No guest driver has ever posted to the faked GSP} — false. \emph{\code{Arch} has zero
production implementations} — false, there are three. \emph{The boot does not complete} — false;
the guest reaches a working \code{nvidia-smi}. \emph{No compute exists in Mode 2} — false;
\code{cup3} returns 43 and \code{cup8} is byte-exact at 2048² and 3072². \emph{Every boot reports
\code{doorbells: 0 arrived}} — false; the \code{cup3} boot reports \code{480 arrived, 480 served, 0 REFUSED}. \emph{The control plane and the execution plane have not met} — false.
\textbf{★ And one added 2026-08-20, hours after this list was written:} \emph{"a second CUDA
context sequentially in one boot still hangs"} — \textbf{false}; three sequential processes pass
(\code{w367}, \S\ref{sec:seqfix}). \emph{"The next wall is our own object graph"} —
\textbf{false}; every refusal in it is more numerous in the boot that passes
(\S\ref{sec:llmwall}).

\textbf{What is still true, and it is now a different list rather than a shorter one.}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{No LLM has ever produced a token in this guest.} The \code{cudart}
  \emph{initialisation} wall is broken (\S\ref{sec:llmwall}); torch's full CUDA init raises
  \emph{"CUDA unknown error"}. ⚠ \code{Xid} 0 — \emph{a refusal we issue, not a fault.}
  ⊘ \textbf{This bullet used to name the cause} — \emph{"the failure moved one rung, into our own
  object graph: \code{PromoteFault::UnknownContextObject} and a handle resolving to the wrong kind
  on the context-promotion path"} — \textbf{and that was refuted three minutes after this
  document's pin} (\code{129dd316}): every one of those refusals is \emph{more} numerous in the
  boot that passes. \textbf{The cause is unidentified.} ⚠ And the workload has not been re-run
  since \code{3887be37}, so its status across the \S\ref{sec:seqfix} fixes is \unver{}.
\item \textbf{Multi-process is demonstrated for \emph{concurrent} and for \emph{sequential}
  processes, and bounded at three.} ⊘ \textbf{This bullet has moved out of the "still true" list
  it was written for.} It read: \emph{"a second context after the first's teardown in the same
  boot still hangs at \code{cuCtxCreate} (w311) \dots{} the bench's 'fresh boot per clean run'
  discipline is still standing in for a fix."} \textbf{That is false as of \code{56dc01f3}}: three
  sequential processes pass in one boot, 8/8 completions each, identical doorbell counts
  (\code{w367}, \S\ref{sec:seqfix}). ⚠ \textbf{What is still true is narrower and is a different
  sentence}: a \emph{fourth} process fails, above the \code{ioctl} boundary, on $n = 1$ boot,
  cause unknown --- and the C artifact's own \emph{"exactly one CUDA process works per QEMU
  lifetime"} was \textbf{not} the same defect after all.
\item \textbf{There is no tenant axis.} \S\ref{sec:multitenant}. No two-VM run has been attempted
  by anyone.
\item \textbf{Every green is a relaxed green} with named relaxations still armed
  (\S\ref{sec:ladder}), and no rung has yet removed them one at a time to see which the 43
  survives.
\item \textbf{Performance is not close.} \S\ref{sec:perf}: 22--81$\times$ off native on large
  kernels, our own doorbell handler dominating small ones, and operands in host system memory at
  \textbf{2.51\,GB/s} against VRAM's 313.5.
\item \textbf{Reclamation is modelled on an event that does not occur.} \measured{} 2026-08-19:
  CUDA's suballocator \emph{does not unmap on \code{cuMemFree}}, so the shipped release trigger
  fires \textbf{zero times} on this workload and is behaviourally identical to \code{off}.
\item \textbf{The trace vocabulary still does not emit.} A grep for the emit call over
  \code{crates/*/src} hits only the crate that defines it.
\item \textbf{One part, one driver, one guest.} GA106, 580.159.04 open. The second architecture
  (\code{Ad10xArch}) has never been booted, and its \code{mmu()} still delegates to
  \code{MockArch}'s \emph{invented} GMMU format.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{★★★ What this means for the claims in this document, and the shape of it changed.}
Everything about \emph{structure} — the DAG, the purity, the type-level isolation, the lock
discipline — is verifiable from the tree and this paper verified it. Everything about
\emph{behaviour against NVIDIA} was inherited from the C artifact and re-expressed, and the
re-expression is now checked at the strongest point available: \textbf{a stock guest driver ran a
CUDA program whose numerical answer only a real GR engine could have produced.} That retires the
whole class of doubt about whether the protocol model is \emph{right}.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{It retires nothing about whether it is \emph{complete}}, and the remaining walls are all
of that kind. ⊘ \textbf{CORRECTED 2026-08-20 (\code{56dc01f3}), and both halves of this sentence
had moved by the time it was read.} It named \emph{"\code{cudart} fails on a control we refuse"}
--- already superseded by \S\ref{sec:llmwall} at this very pin, which reports that wall broken
--- and \emph{"the multi-process property fails or holds on publication paths no boot has
exercised"}, which \code{w361}--\code{w367} then exercised, found a defect in, and fixed
(\S\ref{sec:seqfix}). \textbf{What stands is the shape rather than the instances}: \emph{the
project has stopped being blocked on "does this work at all" and started being blocked on "what
else does the driver want" — which is a better problem and a much longer tail.}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★ And the sharpest single lesson of getting this far is about testing, not about NVIDIA.}
An R1 invariant was violated on the production path for an unknown number of commits while
\textbf{1\,975 tests were green} (\S\ref{sec:r1}). The assert existed, was correct, and was old.
It was simply unreachable from any path CI can execute, because the suite spawns isolates through
a mock that cannot block. \emph{Where the mock and the real implementation differ in whether they
can violate a rule, the assert belongs at the seam they share} — and until it does, the suite's
green is a statement about the mock.
\end{claimbox}

\subsection{The quadratic control plane --- an open, measured DoS}

The invariant catalogue's I4 says hostile input is \emph{contained}: no wedge, no OOM, no
bystander corruption, every hostile event earning only its own refusal. All of that is
property-proven. It says nothing about \emph{cost}, and the project measured the cost and
recorded the result against itself:

\begin{warnbox}\small
The control plane is $O(\mathrm{live\ objects})$ per event, so $N$ events cost $O(N^2)$. Measured on
a debug build: \textbf{1\,000 events 0.85\,s; 8\,000 events 54.8\,s}. The capacity cap is
$2^{18}$ live handles, so a guest can drive that curve deliberately — and \textbf{PyTorch startup
reaches it benignly}. Deleting the per-event graph clone saves \textasciitilde24\% and leaves the
curve quadratic, so the clone is neither the cause nor the fix.
\end{warnbox}

The stated statement in \file{ARCHITECTURE.md} — "no $O(n^2)$ path on hostile floods" — is marked
\textbf{false} in the same document. Two candidate fixes are named and neither is small:
incremental derivation, which is a redesign of the "derived state is a pure function of the
graph, never accreted" decision that the entire determinism differential rests on; or an undo
journal in the most safety-critical file in the repository. A sibling of the same species
\emph{was} fixed (a condemned-list carry-forward, $O(n^2 \log n)$, 55\,s $\to$ 3.8\,s, by
union-find), which is evidence the class is tractable and also evidence that it recurs.

\subsection{Reclamation is the least finished plane}

Figure~\ref{fig:lifecycle} marks two triggers red. Restating the sharper form:

\textbf{"Retire eager, reap deferred" is currently half a law.} The deferral has no bound and
nothing arms it. In a run that never quiesces, reap-deferred and reap-never are indistinguishable.

\textbf{The one genuine cross-process reference has a disposition that no ledger will like.} On a
kernel/UVM dup of a user address space, refcount zero and per-object \code{Free} are not the same
event: the last reference retires and reaps the owner \emph{without issuing a single \code{Free}
verb}, and the disposition of record is the isolate process's death. GPA is conserved
independently and asserted. But any future reclamation ledger, quota or accounting hook will be
wrong on that path first — which the design says, in advance, in its own words.

\textbf{★★ And a third disposition arrived on 2026-08-20, unplanned, as the fix for the
sequential multi-process wall} (\S\ref{sec:seqfix}). A framebuffer join can outlive \emph{every}
table row that named it: the store is per-device and keyed by physical address, the tables are
per-address-space, and a process's rows are unbound in the same tick as the reap --- so by the
time anything looks, the corpse's table is empty while the store still holds the join.
\measured{} \code{w365}: three GR context frames, 48 refusals each, from two independent later
processes, all censused \code{live=0 retired=0}. \textbf{An orphan is provably unowned, so
reclaiming it needs no cross-process trust argument at all} --- and \code{live > 0} stays refused
\emph{by name}, 132 times in the passing boot. ⊘ \textbf{The disposition is a deliberate,
logged leak}: with no table row there is no host virtual address and no memory to hand to the
revoke path, so the host object is dropped rather than freed. Bounded at one per orphaned frame,
and a frame can be orphaned only once. \textbf{The follow-up is named and not done}: plumb the
backing that \code{FbStore::release\_join} already returns out through
\code{RegPlane::release\_fb\_join}, which discards it. ⇒ \emph{This is the second reclamation
path whose disposition of record is "the owner died", and any future ledger or quota will be wrong
on it first --- for the same reason as the one above.}

\textbf{And the most leak-prone moment has no vocabulary at all until L1-M2 lands.} A worker that
dies \emph{mid-chain} cannot run its own unwind, so everything allocated before the failure is in
no \code{Orphans} and in no core state. It is unrecoverable and no current test can see it. The
design's answer — escalate to killing the isolate, because killing the process is how you reclaim
state you cannot enumerate — is correct and is not yet built.

\subsection{arm64 is measured for the core and unmeasured for the shell}

The full suite \emph{ran} on a GB10 Grace-Blackwell aarch64 host: \textbf{372 passed, 0 failed},
identical to x86\_64. That is a real upgrade over a cross-compile check, and it covers the
concurrency suites, whose memory-ordering behaviour is exactly what a \code{cargo check} cannot
see.

What it does not establish, stated by the project so nobody over-reads it: \code{getconf PAGESIZE}
was 4096 on that host, so the 16/64\,KiB page-size rule — the one real pressure point — is still
unexercised. The box was a container with no \file{/dev/kvm} and no \file{/dev/userfaultfd}, so
\textbf{no L1/L2 OS-shell behaviour was exercised on ARM at all}. And there are zero
VM-capable arm64 machines on the bench provider, so settling the remaining question by experiment
requires hardware from somewhere else.

The honest summary is the project's own: \emph{the pure core is arm64-clean by measurement; the OS
shell on arm64 is entirely unmeasured}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{★★★ R1 was broken on the one path the suite could not see}{R1 was broken on the one path the suite could not see}}
\label{sec:r1}

Invariant \textbf{R1} is the one the whole concurrency design is built on: \textbf{no blocking
call under any lock, ever}. Blocking calls take \emph{zero} ranked locks. It is asserted, not
documented — a thread-local lock-depth counter checked at every blocking-verb entry.

\textbf{It was violated on the production path, and only a real GPU could say so.} With the real
isolate plane enabled, QEMU \textbf{aborted} on the guest's first register write that reached an
object allocation: \emph{"R1 no-blocking-under-lock violation: spawning a sandboxed child process
while holding rank(s) [0]"}, in a non-unwinding panic, with \code{kayfabe\_shim\_regs\_write}
$\leftarrow$ \code{nvkvm\_trap\_write} below it — an ordinary guest register write.

\textbf{The defect in one sentence.} Materialising an isolate is a \code{clone} into six
namespaces, an \code{execveat} of a sealed memfd, a blocking handshake and a chain of host RM
ioctls — and it had been placed on the \code{Ok} arm of the core's apply, which runs under the
device write lock. \textbf{R1 admits no exception for that, and the design already said so.}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ Why the suite was blind, which is the more important half.} The assert that fired is
real, correct and old — it lives at the syscall, in the raw-syscall crate. \textbf{It is
reachable only on the host plane.} The whole suite spawns through \code{MockIsolateFactory},
whose spawn blocks on nothing and asserts nothing, so on every path CI can execute the violation
was invisible: \textbf{1\,975 green tests over a live R1 breach.}

\smallskip
\emph{An invariant asserted only in the production adapter is an invariant the test suite does not
hold.} The repair was not only to move the spawn — it was to move \emph{the assert}, to
\code{IsolateBox::new}, which is the one way an isolate enters core state whichever plane made
it. \textbf{That turns the entire mock-driven suite into a live guard for it.}

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{And when it broke is \unver{}.} Earlier increments' live real-isolate measurements no
longer reproduce, no bisection was run, and no claim is made about which commit did it.
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{The fix is a phase-shape change, not an edit}, and it is the same latch-and-discharge
two-step the codebase already used twice elsewhere: \emph{decide} which isolates are needed under
rank 0, \emph{spawn} with zero locks held, \emph{install} under rank 0 with R5 re-validation. A
new deferred state, \code{IsolatePending}, resolves in the device loop. ★ \textbf{Six doors needed
the discrimination, not two}, and \emph{four of the six were found by the test and by nothing
else}.

\textbf{The acceptance, and it is a properly-controlled one.} Three boots on an RTX 3060: two
with the real plane, both exit 0, \textbf{zero} R1 violations, one isolate child each at
\code{t+42\,s} and \code{t+43\,s} against a device open at \code{t+37\,s} — so the spawn still
\emph{follows} the guest's action, re-measured rather than assumed. ★ And the pre-fix control was
taken \textbf{on a second physical machine the fix's author never touched}, at a master revision,
where the abort reproduces — so the pair is a genuine control rather than a comparison across two
variables at once.

\textbf{⊘ And the honest headline is that the wall did not move.} The guest dmesg is byte-identical
across all three arms — 26 lines, 22 \code{NVRM}, 3 adapter lines. \emph{Why unchanged is the
expected answer and not a disappointment}: the isolate plane serves \emph{verbs}, and the boot
dies long before a channel is scheduled, so there is no guest submission for it to serve. What
the run buys is that \textbf{the plane can be switched on at all}, which is the prerequisite for
everything after it.

\paragraph{★ Five bite-checks, four bit, and the fifth found the test.} A
\code{\#[should\_panic(expected = "R1 no-blocking-under-lock")]} was \emph{vacuous}: the assert
under test shares its panic message with the isolate's own \code{Drop}, so the expectation was
being satisfied by the wrong door. The general form, worth carrying: \textbf{a
\code{\#[should\_panic]} whose expected string is produced by more than one site in the code under
test is not a test of either of them.}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Multi-tenancy: two shapes contained, one that bites, and no tenant axis at all}{Multi-tenancy: two shapes contained, one that bites, and no tenant axis at all}}
\label{sec:multitenant}

Multi-tenancy is the thesis (\S\ref{sec:provenance}), so it is the claim most worth attacking.
Three denial shapes were measured on a real GA106, and they did not come back the same way.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{3.9cm}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Shape} & \textbf{Verdict} & \textbf{What was measured} \\
\midrule
Infinite compute kernel & \textbf{contained} &
  229\,376 threads against a ${\sim}43\,008$-thread resident capacity, so the device is genuinely
  oversubscribed. A second process: \textbf{12/12} runs over 60\,s, \code{bad=0} every time,
  \textbf{zero Xid}, no reset. It costs its neighbour roughly $2\times$ throughput — \emph{a
  fairness problem, which is the honest name for it}. ⊘ The \emph{mechanism} is \inferred{};
  the run observed only that the victim survived. \\
\addlinespace
Guest-reachable MMU fault & \textbf{contained} &
  A genuine \code{Xid 31 FAULT\_PDE}. The attacker's context dies sticky; ★ a victim
  \textbf{holding a live context across the fault} completed \textbf{2\,682\,576 iterations,
  2\,682\,576 correct, 0 wrong}. \textbf{Stronger than the row above, because the escalation path
  actually ran} rather than merely not being needed. \\
\addlinespace
\textbf{VRAM exhaustion} & ★ \textbf{NOT contained} &
  An \emph{unprivileged} hog took \textbf{11\,776 of 12\,288\,MiB (95.8\%)}. A second tenant
  \textbf{cannot even create a context} — out-of-memory in \textbf{0.29\,s}. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\textbf{★ Why only the third bites, and it is a one-line argument.} The first two rest on
\emph{the GPU's scheduler}. The third rests on nothing — it is ordinary allocation.
\textbf{Denial is total}: not "the victim's allocation fails" but "the victim cannot get onto the
device at all", which is strictly worse, because a tenant that cannot create a context cannot even
begin to degrade gracefully. ★ \textbf{But it is honest denial} — immediate, named, and fully
reversible, with no Xid and no reset. \emph{That is a resource problem, not a recovery problem},
and the mitigation is ordinary and buildable: a per-tenant VRAM quota, enforced where we already
broker allocation. It is the one multi-tenant exposure so far that is squarely inside our reach.

\textbf{★★ And the instrument was wrong first, in the way this paper keeps recording.} The first
version of the wedge probe span \textbf{1 block of 32 threads on a 28-SM GPU}. The victim survived
\emph{because 27 SMs were free}. It reported total containment and \textbf{could not have shown
otherwise} — and the one number that looked like a saturation check, GPU utilisation, reads 100\%
in both cases, so it could not discriminate either.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ The confidentiality answer is NO, and the reason is structural rather than a missing
control.} Asked whether the security model is sufficient for aggressive multi-tenancy, the
assessment answers: \textbf{no — because there is no tenant axis in this system at all.} Every
isolation property this tree proves is \emph{intra-VM, between guest processes}. There is no
\code{VmId}, \code{TenantId} or \code{GuestId} anywhere in \code{crates/}. Cross-tenant separation
is the incidental consequence of two VMs being two host processes.

\smallskip
\textbf{And at the one place two tenants genuinely meet — the host NVIDIA driver — the driver's
own cross-client check is defeated by construction.} RM compares client tokens with an
\textbf{OR}: a matching euid alone passes. Every isolate presents the same euid, so two tenants'
isolates pass RM's cross-client check against each other. ★ And the handle is \emph{not}
guessable-in-theory-only: it is a fixed base OR'd with a monotonically increasing index, so
concurrent isolates receive adjacent values. \textbf{There is nothing to guess.}

\smallskip
\textbf{★ Two narrowings that are genuinely reassuring, and are not the same as a fix.} RM's
default cross-client \emph{dup} policy is PID-scoped, not euid-scoped, so the dup path across
tenants \emph{is} refused even at matching euid — the exposure is the direct-naming path only.
And the expected break — residual tenant data on GPU memory reuse — \textbf{does not exist on the
paths we drive}: the host driver scrubs VRAM on free and makes the frame un-allocatable until the
scrub retires.

\smallskip
