⊘ \textbf{Every claim in this box is \inferred{} from driver source. No GPU was switched on for
it, both source documents say so in their own banners, and the single experiment that would settle
it — two VMs on one box — has never been attempted.} The highest-value single change is named and
is one line of deployment policy: \textbf{run each isolate at a distinct non-zero uid}, which
moves the boundary from our code to the kernel's.
\end{warnbox}

\paragraph{So where does that leave "red-teamed at the logic layer only"?} That sentence, which
this paper printed at the previous revision, is now \textbf{half discharged and half still true},
and the split is worth stating precisely. \textbf{Discharged at the host-GPU boundary}: a
system-level property now exists — \emph{every effect a hostile guest can produce on the host GPU
is an effect a local unprivileged process could already produce} — with a fourteen-finding audit
of the actual host-facing surface behind it, the three denial shapes above measured on hardware,
and a \textbf{measured host-filesystem escape found and closed} (the isolate's own \file{/dev}
descriptor opened \file{../etc/shadow} on a real host). \textbf{Still open at the cross-tenant
boundary}: no tenant axis, \textbf{no \code{seccomp} anywhere in the tree} (absent, and named as
absent rather than stubbed), no two-VM run, and the security review of the new FFI surface is the
declared exit gate of the L2 work and \textbf{has not been done}.

\subsection{The QEMU $\geq$ 10.2 floor buys less than the decision that took it assumed}
\label{sec:qemu}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★ This section is no longer a forecast.} At the previous pin the L2 adapter had not been
started and this section priced it. It is now built through stage Q5, compiled into \emph{two}
real hypervisor binaries (9.2.0 and 10.2.4, the same overlay unchanged), and booted against
repeatedly on a real GA106 — and on 9.2.0 the identical binary path is \textbf{refused by name}
at the runtime floor, which was watched rather than assumed. \textbf{Every cost itemised below
was paid, and none of the estimates below turned out to be wrong.} The exit gate, Q7 — the
security review of the FFI surface and the written R1/R3/R5 re-audit — is \textbf{not done}.
\end{claimbox}

The design requires \code{memory\_region\_enable\_lockless\_io()}, which lands in QEMU 10.2. That
buys a genuinely important thing — MMIO dispatch without the big QEMU lock, which is what makes
the doorbell path viable — and upstream's helper also sets the reentrancy guard in the same body,
so a discipline the design planned to maintain is no longer ours.

The floor decision's second claim was economic: requiring a version rather than patching one
deletes \emph{"the whole 'which QEMU are we built into' ambiguity"}. \textbf{The L2-Q adapter
design (\code{b31487a}) checked that claim against QEMU's source and it does not hold.}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{QEMU v10.2.0 has no supported out-of-tree device mechanism, so our device must be
compiled inside a QEMU source tree.} Two mechanisms, both closed:
\code{module\_load\_qom()} resolves a QOM type name against \code{module\_info}, a table
\emph{generated at QEMU build time}, so a \code{.so} that is not in that table can never be
discovered by type name — and \code{QEMU\_MODULE\_DIR} changes only the directory searched, not
the table. \code{module\_load\_dso()} additionally requires the module to export a per-build
\code{CONFIG\_STAMP} symbol; upstream's own failure hint is verbatim \emph{"Only modules from the
same build can be loaded."}
\hfill \src{v10.2.0 util/module.c, include/qemu/module.h}
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{State it exactly, without overstating it.} The floor \emph{does} delete a patch we would
otherwise have had to carry: the cancelled 9.2 backport modified upstream semantics inside
\code{system/physmem.c}, had to be re-derived every release, and a mis-rebase would silently
change how \emph{every} device in the machine dispatched. What replaces it is an additive
overlay — a directory dropped into a stock 10.2.x checkout plus two hunks (one \code{meson.build}
line, one \code{Kconfig} stanza) — whose only conflict surface is a build failure, never a
behaviour change. That is a real improvement. \textbf{What it does not buy is "install your
distro's QEMU and run".} The user builds QEMU once. Three of the four costs the floor decision
itemised against the backport — a rebase every release, a build script that must reproduce it,
and a supply-chain claim we make to every user — still apply.

\textbf{There is exactly one path to a genuinely stock QEMU, it is named, and it is not taken for
v1.} \code{vfio-user} would make our device a separate process speaking a documented socket
protocol to an unmodified distro-packaged QEMU. It is present at v10.2.0
(\file{hw/vfio-user/}, two documentation files, a vendored \code{libvfio-user}) and
\file{MAINTAINERS} lists it \code{S: Supported}. It would delete the QEMU tree, the overlay, and
the second \code{unsafe} crate the in-tree route needs. It is rejected for v1 on one source read:
nothing in \file{hw/vfio-user/} calls \code{memory\_region\_enable\_lockless\_io}, so a trapped BAR
access becomes a socket round trip \emph{taken with the BQL held} — reintroducing and amplifying
the exact serialisation the floor was taken to remove. The design records the small experiment
that would change the verdict (measure the round trip both ways, and ask whether marking the
client's regions lockless is a patch upstream would accept) rather than closing the question.

The facility inventory that priced the floor also \textbf{reversed two of its own design
assumptions}, which is worth knowing before trusting the rest of it: the read-only-memslot
region-lock fallback, priced in the design at 1.49\,µs as "a flags-only flip", is
\emph{unreachable through QEMU} — QEMU's flatten pass turns a readonly change into a
delete-plus-add. And checkpoint-restart (\code{cpr-transfer}) is a lifecycle event the design had
never considered.

\textbf{One more cost the adapter design surfaced, did not argue away, and then paid.} A QEMU
adapter needs \code{extern "C"} entry points, raw \code{MemoryRegion *} handles and adoption of a
host pointer QEMU owns — all \code{unsafe}. The workspace's CI gate exists to keep the audit
surface at \emph{one} crate readable in one sitting, and its own failure text says adding a
second is "a design decision, not a manifest edit". \textbf{That decision was taken and the
price was paid in full}: \code{kayfabe-qemu-raw} is the second audited \code{unsafe} crate at
\textbf{49} relaxations, the ratchet is now a named two-element list, and the acceptance
criterion the gate protects \emph{is} "read two crates". ★ Its bar started at \textbf{0}
deliberately, so the first relaxation had to be a reviewed commit rather than a manifest edit —
and the crate's first act as an FFI consumer was to prove the ratchet's own counting pattern
wrong (\S\ref{sec:crates}).

\textbf{And the packaging verdict is unchanged and now testable.} What the floor buys is an
additive overlay — a directory dropped into a stock 10.2.x checkout plus two build-file hunks —
and that is exactly what shipped: \file{qemu/hw/misc/nvkvm/}, 2\,676 lines of C, built by
\file{scripts/build\_qom\_shim.sh}. \textbf{What it does not buy is still "install your distro's
QEMU and run".} The user builds QEMU once. The \code{vfio-user} route that would delete that cost
remains named, priced and not taken for v1.

\subsection{The bench --- rented, serialised, and now more than one box}
\label{sec:bench}

All hardware work happens on rented GPU boxes, strictly serially: concurrent or mid-ioctl
activity wedges the GPU. Two cargo builds on one box corrupted a mutation run through the shared
\file{\textasciitilde/.cargo}, so the rule is one cargo per box.

\textbf{★ The previous revision said "there is no second machine, no second GPU generation, and no
continuous hardware testing." Two thirds of that is now false and the correction matters,
because $n=1$ was this paper's standing caveat on every hardware number.} Work since has run on
\textbf{four machines and three distinct physical GPUs}: a GPU-less four-core development box
(where, notably, \emph{every} guest boot in the boot log ran); a rented 38-core box with an
\textbf{RTX 3090 (GA102)}; and \textbf{two different RTX 3060s (GA106)} on separate rented
instances. Three cross-machine reproductions are load-bearing rather than decorative:

\begin{itemize}
\item A wall reproduced \textbf{line for line, 36 of 36 dmesg lines}, between the GPU-less box and
  a real-GPU box — which is what establishes it is a property of the port and not of a machine.
\item The bring-up ladder run on \textbf{two different physical GA106s} differs by \textbf{one
  line of thirty-three}, and that line is a handle. A committed transcript is therefore a real
  cross-machine regression test.
\item The R1 abort (\S\ref{sec:r1}) was reproduced at a master revision on \textbf{a second
  machine the fix's author never touched}, which is what makes its before/after a control rather
  than a comparison across two variables.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{⊘ What has \emph{not} changed, and the reader should hold onto it.} Every \emph{boot}
result is GA106, one driver family (580.159.04 open), one guest OS, and one guest at a time. The
GA102 appears only in ladder and suite runs, never in a boot. There is still \textbf{no continuous
hardware testing} — GitHub CI has no GPU and never will. And the project's own standing caveat is
repeated verbatim in every boot section for a reason: \textbf{one Mode-2 symptom was 1/3 one day
and 9/9 the next on a bit-identical binary}, so a single green boot is weak evidence and the
documents treat it that way.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★ And the bench has lied about its own provenance, more than once, which is why every
claim here carries a revision.} A binary served for weeks was built from a commit nobody had
noticed. A later one \textbf{could not name its own revision at all}: it contained exactly one
40-hex string, which was a build-id and not a commit in either repository, and its only
provenance was a worktree \textbf{19 commits behind master}. The instrument that was supposed to
discriminate — \code{strings} for feature markers — scored \textbf{0 for every marker in both
binaries}, because a release build strips enum names and inlines constants, and that was only
discovered by checking it against a known-good symbol. ⇒ \textbf{\code{strings} cannot answer
"what revision is this"; embed the SHA} — which the archive now does.
\end{warnbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The recurring failure mode: claims that were true \emph{once}}{The recurring failure mode: claims that were true once}}
\label{sec:drift}

This is the pattern the project keeps hitting, it has a name in the repository, and it is the
single most useful thing for a reviewer to internalise.

\begin{claimbox}\small
Across three independent passes, roughly \textbf{twenty} instances were found of documentation
asserting more than the code did — \textbf{several stating the literal opposite of their own
code}. \textbf{Every one of them was true when written.} That is the point: this is not
carelessness, it is entropy.
\hfill \src{docs/design/testing\_doctrine.md \S6}
\end{claimbox}

★★★★★ \textbf{The 2026-08-21 instance is the most expensive one yet, and it is a variant this
list did not previously contain.} Every entry below is a claim that \emph{became} false. The
\code{PC-D7} comment (\S\ref{sec:pcd7}) was \textbf{never true}: it described a real hazard in
correct detail, predicted the exact failure symptom, and attached a \emph{frequency estimate} ---
"rare" --- that was wrong by orders of magnitude because it modelled the guest's message queue as
living for the module's lifetime rather than the adapter's. On that estimate the item was filed
as \emph{"a named trade-off, not a defect to be fixed"}, and it then made the GPU unusable after
five invocations of \code{nvidia-smi}. ⇒ \textbf{A documented hazard carries two claims --- the
mechanism and its rate --- and only the first is usually checked. The second is what decides
whether it gets fixed.}

\smallskip
The instances are specific and they are not small:

\begin{itemize}
\item A design document named \code{memory\_region\_clear\_global\_locking()} as the mechanism for
  a decision. \textbf{That QEMU function had been deleted five years earlier.} The reference
  document written to price the floor now carries a \code{file:line} on every row for exactly this
  reason — and found two \emph{more} live instances of the same decay while being written.
\item A refactor plan cited a userfaultfd proof-of-concept file \textbf{that does not exist}. The
  mechanism was planned four times and never built; its demand-fault path is dead code that calls
  \code{stub\_exit(139)} and never returns, so the fault address has been permanently zero since a
  commit early in the project's life. Nobody noticed because "planned" and "presumably done" read
  the same in a plan.
\item \textbf{Two sections of one design document contradicted each other}, found only when the
  code between them was written.
\item Two claims about the C's behaviour, cited from the C's own source comments, turned out to be
  \textbf{false when measured on the bench}. The rule extracted: citing a comment is citing a
  belief.
\item A rustdoc described a default-to-GPU-0 guess \emph{inside the one resolver whose entire
  discipline is "never guess"}. Another claimed "MISS=FAULT is preserved — never a silent skip"
  while its own body correctly skipped on both arms; the body was right and the sentence was wrong
  about the most-cited exception in the codebase.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{The project's response is the right one: prefer a mechanism to a sentence.} A rule stated
only in prose has no reader. \code{HostHandle} carrying its \code{IsolateId} is the same rule with
a compiler behind it. The house style for corrections is \emph{strike visibly} — keep the original,
struck, with the correction and its evidence — so a reader who remembers the old claim learns it
was wrong rather than doubting their memory.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★★★ A FURTHER INSTANCE, ADDED 2026-08-20 AT \code{56dc01f3}, AND IT IS THE SHORTEST
INTERVAL YET MEASURED: FOUR HOURS AND FIFTY-FIVE MINUTES.} This paragraph is folded in above the
tally below because the tally is what it corrects.

\smallskip
This revision was pinned at \code{5c367a38} and committed at \textbf{15:36}. Its
\textbf{headline blocker} --- \emph{"a second CUDA context sequentially in one boot still
hangs"} --- was \textbf{fixed at 18:31} (\code{ecb67fae} onwards) and \textbf{measured passing
at 20:20} (\code{cca2eb4b}, three sequential processes, \S\ref{sec:seqfix}). A \emph{second}
headline --- \emph{"the next wall is a different subsystem: the object graph"} --- was refuted at
\textbf{15:39}, \textbf{three minutes} after the pin, by a negative control that had been sitting
on the bench as two log files the whole time (\S\ref{sec:llmwall}).

\smallskip
⇒ \textbf{That makes four consecutive revisions in which the falsified claim was the one the
paper led with}, and the interval has gone from \emph{days} to \emph{minutes} --- this time
without a new revision being written at all. ⊘ \textbf{And the two
instances are different species, which is the useful part.} The multi-process claim
\emph{went} stale --- true when written, overtaken by work done afterwards, and no discipline
available to a writer would have caught it. The object-graph claim was \textbf{false when
written}, and the control that refutes it \emph{already existed}: it was a census of a failing
run read as a wall, by an author who had written down the rule \emph{"the failing run is not the
experiment; the DIFFERENCE is"} eleven messages earlier. \emph{Only one of those two is a drift
problem. The other is a method problem wearing a drift problem's clothes.}
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{And the pattern is live right now, in this document's subject, and in this document.}
The first revision found fourteen instances (\S\ref{sec:corrections} rows 1--14). The second
found six more, two of them in the first revision's own text. The third found six more again,
\emph{three} in this paper including its headline. \textbf{This revision found eight more, and
\emph{five} of them are in this paper} — including, again, the sentence it led with, which has
now been the falsified claim in \textbf{three consecutive revisions} — \textbf{four} counting
the post-pin correction pass recorded in the box above.

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{★★ The direction of the drift inverted at this revision, and that is a new thing to say
about it.} Every previous instance was a document claiming \emph{more} than the code did. Five of
this revision's eight are the opposite: the paper claimed \emph{less} than the code did — no
binary, no isolate, no \code{Arch} implementation, no walk function, one adapter. \textbf{An
understatement decays exactly as fast as an overstatement and is harder to notice}, because
nobody audits a document for being too modest, and a reader who acts on it makes a
\emph{different} wrong decision rather than an obviously wrong one. ⊘ It is the same defect: a
description that used to be true of a thing.
\end{claimbox}

\textbf{Every claim that \emph{does} have a gate is mechanically true today} — the boundary greps,
the generation-name grep, the two-crate unsafe ratchet, the seven reached-count floors, the
"exactly one GPA call site" assertion, the E0639 compile-fail row that makes the ring gate
structural, the bridge-exclusivity grep \emph{which fired on its first run}, the ogkm-version-tag
gate, and the claim ledger. Four revisions of this paper have now found roughly thirty-four
ungated claims to be stale and \textbf{zero} gated ones. That is either a devastating footnote or
the strongest possible evidence for the project's own thesis, and it is the latter.

\textbf{★ With one qualification this revision earned the hard way, and it cuts against the
sentence above.} A gated claim is mechanically true \emph{of what the gate quantifies over}, and
three separate instruments were found this cycle to be quantifying over less than anyone thought:
the mutation gate scored 100\% over a universe that a full disk had eaten
(\S\ref{sec:mutgate}); the unsafe ratchet's own regex could not match the dominant form in the
crate it was newly guarding, and \textbf{counted 23 of 31 while calling itself a complete audit}
(\S\ref{sec:crates}); and a citation gate was satisfied by a source that said nothing
(\S\ref{sec:oraclelimits}). \emph{"Zero gated claims were stale" is a true statement about a set
whose size is itself a measurement} — and all three of those measurements were wrong upward.

\textbf{★ The sharpest form of it at this revision is not in the prose at all — it is in the
instruments} (\S\ref{sec:instruments}). A configuration file that was never read, a baseline that
omitted the suite, a timeout scored as a kill and a hang that printed nothing are all the same
species as a stale sentence: a description that used to be true of a thing. The difference is that
those four described \emph{the evidence}, which makes them the more expensive kind.

\textbf{The strongest single instance is in \S\ref{sec:gsp} and it is worth restating here as a
pattern rather than a fact about NVIDIA.} A specification was cited as if it were the
specification, when it was one version of a specification; 3\,550 lines were built against it; and
one question had been closed with an answer that is exactly backwards for the machine the code will
first run on. The claim that survived that audit was the one marked \code{[inferred]} — because it
knew it was second-hand. The claim that did not survive was marked \textbf{RESOLVED}.

\subsection{Risks this document adds}
\label{sec:risks}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{★★★★★ Nothing anyone would actually run works.} \S\ref{sec:llmwall}. The
  \code{cudart} initialisation wall is broken ($n = 2$ workloads) and \textbf{the model still
  produces zero tokens}. ⊘ \textbf{This bullet used to continue} \emph{"the failure moved one rung
  into our object graph"} \textbf{--- refuted at \code{129dd316}}, three minutes after this paper
  was committed (\S\ref{sec:corrections} row 44): \textbf{the cause is unidentified}, which is
  worse news than a named wall, not better. ⚠ \textbf{And the workload has not been re-run since
  \code{3887be37}}, so "zero tokens" is \measured{} at that revision and \unver{} at HEAD.
  ⊘ \textbf{And the
  milestone before it was graded on a proxy} — \code{is\_available()} went \code{True}, was
  reported as the wall breaking, and the actual model disagreed within the hour. \emph{This
  project has now made that exact mistake twice}, the first time declaring "first compute" on a
  copy-engine round trip.
\item \textbf{★★★ Every green result is a relaxed green, and the shipping default is not among the
  relaxations.} \S\ref{sec:ladder}. Eleven arms are forced on by the bench runner and five are
  measured load-bearing; \code{KAYFABE\_GR\_ROUTE} — one of the five — still defaults to
  \code{refuse} in code. \textbf{The configuration that computes is not the configuration that
  ships}, and no rung has yet removed the relaxations one at a time to see which the \code{43}
  survives.
\item \textbf{★★ Performance is not close, and the two problems bind at different sizes.}
  \S\ref{sec:perf}. Operands sit in host system memory read at \textbf{2.51\,GB/s} against VRAM's
  313.5; compute is \textbf{22--81$\times$} off native at large kernel sizes, while at small ones
  our own doorbell handler is the launch. \textbf{No tokens/second figure exists for this port at
  all} ($n = 0$), and the one in circulation is arithmetic over a floor that has since moved by
  ${\sim}20\times$.
\item \textbf{The mocks are still the only implementation of \code{Device}, and a mock is where
  an invariant hid for an unknown number of commits.} \S\ref{sec:r1} is the measured
  demonstration that this is not a theoretical concern: 1\,975 green tests over a live R1 breach,
  because the double could not violate the rule. \textbf{If a belief about RM semantics is wrong,
  the suite will confirm it consistently} — and if a rule is only assertable in production, the
  suite does not hold it at all.
\item \textbf{The project moves faster than its own documentation, and it has not slowed.}
  \textbf{974 commits since the previous pin}, and the whitepaper's own headline claim was
  falsified \emph{twice while this revision was being written} — once by a commit that landed
  mid-session (\S\ref{sec:llmwall}). Note also that the committed trace README describing that
  work is, at this pin, \textbf{one rung stale relative to the commit beside it}: it still names a
  suspect that the next commit retired. \file{crate\_maturity\_map.md} — a document whose entire job is
  to say what is built — is now wholly stale, and this paper stopped citing it rather than
  laundering numbers through it. \textbf{No gate covers prose}, which is why
  \S\ref{sec:corrections} keeps growing.
\item \textbf{★ Multi-tenancy has no tenant axis, and that is the thesis.} \S\ref{sec:multitenant}.
  Two denial shapes are contained and one is not; the confidentiality answer is \emph{no}; the
  host driver's own cross-client check is defeated by a shared euid; and \textbf{the single
  experiment that would settle any of it — two VMs on one box — has never been attempted}. The
  highest-value fix is one line of deployment policy, which is encouraging and is not the same as
  having done it.
\item \textbf{★ Multi-process holds for three processes, sequential or concurrent, and fails at
  the fourth.} ⊘ \textbf{This bullet was the paper's headline blocker and it was fixed at 18:31 on
  the day the paper was pinned.} It read: \emph{"fails for sequential ones \dots{} a second
  context after the first's teardown in the same boot still hangs at \code{cuCtxCreate}, which is
  the C artifact's own limitation inherited \dots{} the bench's 'fresh boot per clean run'
  discipline is still standing in for a fix."} \textbf{False at \code{56dc01f3}}
  (\S\ref{sec:seqfix}) --- and the C's limitation was not inherited: the port's cause was its own
  orphaned framebuffer join. \textbf{What remains a risk is smaller and sharper}: a
  \emph{fourth} process still fails above the \code{ioctl} boundary with the cause unknown, the
  result is $n = 1$ boot, and \textbf{a host object is leaked per reclaimed orphan frame} ---
  bounded at one per frame, logged, with the plumbing fix named and not done. The concurrent run
  still scopes itself as before: two identical short workloads with emulated copy work, bounded by
  the hypervisor's big lock — \emph{a bound that weakens exactly when the forwarded work gets
  longer}.
\item \textbf{★★★ Reclamation is built on an event that does not occur, and the fix is capped by
  a constant nobody has justified.} CUDA's suballocator does not unmap on \code{cuMemFree}, so the
  nominated release trigger fires \textbf{zero times}; the working mechanism is a takeover, capped
  because without a cap the settlement oscillates forever. ⊘ \textbf{The cap's key changed on
  2026-08-20 and the old key was itself a defect}: keyed by \emph{frame} with no reset, a
  device-lifetime budget spent by early actors left later leaves unbacked, and \textbf{our own
  bound raised a host \code{Xid} 31 \code{FAULT\_PDE}} (\code{cca2eb4b}, \S\ref{sec:seqfix}).
  It is now keyed by \code{(phys, taking va)}. \textbf{Which of two live aliases should win is
  still unproven}, and failure in this area is a function of allocation \emph{history} rather than
  size — which is why it took a campaign to find and why \code{cup8}
  cannot close it (its buffers never move).
\item \textbf{★★ Every mutation number this project has published has been wrong upward, five
  times, by five different mechanisms} (\S\ref{sec:mutgate}). The most recent scored
  \textbf{exactly 100\%} while a full disk deleted 78\% of its universe and the harness reported
  \code{ok}. \textbf{The bar has since been lowered from 91 to 80}, and no clean campaign has
  completed under either, so there is still no defensible score to quote.
\item \textbf{The C oracle is positively wrong about a whole class of rows, and a third class is
  unmeasured} (\S\ref{sec:oraclelimits}). 16 truncated rows — more than the 11 empty ones — have
  never been checked, and a truncated row passes the test built to catch the empty ones.
\item \textbf{★ The security review of the new FFI surface is the declared exit gate of the L2
  work and has not been done}, while the surface itself is now 49 audited \code{unsafe}
  relaxations in a crate a hypervisor calls directly. There is no \code{seccomp} anywhere in the
  tree.
\item \textbf{⊘ The item this list used to end with is RESOLVED, and it had been resolved for two
  revisions before this paper stopped printing it.} \S11-O7a was closed on 2026-08-06
  (\S\ref{sec:gsp}). What replaces it in this slot is a \emph{measurement} rather than a question:
  \textbf{no teardown / re-acquire boot has ever been run.} The 2026-08-05 recommendation was to
  spend the first boot that survives \code{RmInitAdapter} on one, \emph{"because nothing will
  surface this incidentally."} \code{RmInitAdapter} has since been survived many dozens of times,
  and a search of the log, the committed bench evidence and every design document finds no such
  boot. Combined with the core having \textbf{no reset entry point of any kind}, a guest
  \code{modprobe -r nvidia; modprobe nvidia} is an untested path against a core that cannot
  reclaim.
\end{itemize}

  \code{Spine::apply} \\

  \code{route\_doorbell}, then \code{plan\_doorbell}; the ring gate itself now lives one crate

\subsection{Conventions}

\begin{itemize}
\item \code{*\_unsafe.rs} — any file using the \code{unsafe} keyword is named this way, and the
  gate greps whole words so writing \emph{about} the rule (\code{unsafe\_code}, \code{\_unsafe.rs})
  never trips it.
\item \code{plan\_* / commit\_*} — the two halves of the verb seam. \code{plan\_} runs under locks
  and issues nothing; \code{commit\_} runs after re-acquisition and re-validates.
\item \code{route\_* / exec\_*} — the route/act split (structural rule R4): resolve identity
  against device-wide state, then act on one process's state.
\item Test names carry their provenance: \code{t12\_}, \code{t13\_}, \code{t14\_} are C-bug
  regressions; \code{cb*} are the regression matrix rows; \code{b1\_}--\code{b6\_} are security
  boundary cases; \code{i1}--\code{i4} are the isolation properties; the marker \emph{Mutation-gate kill}
  in a doc comment names the mutant a test exists to kill.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Which design documents to read, in order}
\label{sec:reading}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \file{docs/design/testing\_doctrine.md} (321 lines) — read this \emph{first}, even before
  the architecture. It is the shortest path to understanding what the project believes evidence is.
\item \file{docs/design/core\_state\_and\_consolidation.md} — the reviewed per-crate state and the
  L1 hand-off contract.
\item \file{docs/design/core\_security\_threat\_model.md} — the attacker model and the I1--I4
  properties.
\item \file{docs/design/l1\_concurrency.md} \S12 and \file{docs/design/l1\_os\_shell.md} \S14 —
  the contact logs (the two documents run to 10\,265 lines between them). \textbf{Read these rather than the summaries};
  they are where the design was found to be wrong, which is the part a summary cannot carry.
\item \file{docs/design/RESUME\_HERE\_2026\_08\_15.md} — \textbf{read this before any other
  status document.} It is the ordering rather than the record, it opens with its own supersession
  banner naming what a later rung invalidated in it, and its \S6 (\emph{"how to read anything
  written before today"}) lists three campaign-wide corrections that invalidate earlier phrasings
  across the whole tree — including that \textbf{four different things in this project are called
  "drain"}, a misattribution that propagated through three rungs.
\item \file{traces/cudart\_ga106/README.md} — the live blocker's evidence.
  ⚠ \textbf{And it is one rung stale at this pin} (\S\ref{sec:corrections} row 41): read the
  commit messages after it, not it alone. ⊘ \textbf{As of \code{56dc01f3} it is several rungs
  stale}: the wall it documents was refuted the same afternoon (row 44). The commit messages
  \code{dbc6c953}\,$\to$\,\code{56dc01f3} are the current record and they carry their evidence
  verbatim.
\item \file{docs/design/execution\_plane\_increments.md} and
  \file{docs/design/boot\_measured\_2026\_08\_01.md} — the dated, revision-stamped boot logs.
  ⚠ \textbf{Both predate first compute} (last touched 2026-08-11 and 2026-08-01) and describe
  walls that have since been crossed. They remain the best record of \emph{how} a wall gets
  characterised and of at least three sections marked \textbf{REFUTED} that were kept rather than
  deleted — \emph{read them for method, not for status.}
\item \file{docs/design/guest\_blast\_radius.md} and
  \file{docs/design/multi\_tenant\_isolation\_assessment.md} — the security position on the
  thesis. Read the banners first: neither switched on a GPU for its reasoning.
\item \file{docs/reference/} — measured ground truth, kept out of the design documents so a wrong
  fact is corrected in one place. Note the version caveat in \file{rm\_semantics\_measured.md}
  \S0 before quoting any number from it.
\item ⊘ \textbf{Do not read \file{docs/design/crate\_maturity\_map.md}} — \S\ref{sec:corrections}
  row 29.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★ One rule that applies to every document in that list, and the project states it about
itself.} Design documents here open with a dated \code{STATUS} block — and the convention's own
limit is written down: \textbf{a \code{STATUS: LIVE} header certifies that the document has not
been \emph{retired}, never that its body is \emph{current}.} Several headline documents carry
\code{LIVE} and have not been touched since 2026-07-31, while the campaign that produced every
result in \S\ref{sec:ladder} through \S\ref{sec:llmwall} ran from 2026-08-11 onward. ⇒ \emph{Treat
"open" in an untouched document as \textbf{open-and-unrevisited}, not open-and-rechecked} — this
paper found one item that had been resolved for two weeks and was still printed as unresolvable in
three places (\S\ref{sec:corrections} row 36).
\end{warnbox}

% ===========================================================================
\section{Claims corrected while writing this paper}
\label{sec:corrections}

Each of these is a claim that this paper checked against the tree and found stale or wrong. They
are listed because the list is more useful than any individual correction: it is a measurement of
the drift rate described in \S\ref{sec:drift}. None is a defect in the code; all are defects in
descriptions of the code.

Rows 1--14 are from the first revision, at \code{0b78102}; rows 15--20 from the second, at
\code{6ce8599}; rows 21--26 from the third, at \code{b612a91}; rows 27--34 from the fourth, at
\code{f33e1c8}; \textbf{rows 35--42 (and 41b) are new at \code{5c367a38}}; \textbf{rows 43--45
were added after this document was pinned, at \code{56dc01f3}, without a new revision being
written} --- they correct claims in the text above rather than in a predecessor, and rows 43 and
44 are this paper's \emph{headline} claims. \textbf{Eleven of the first
fourteen were fixed or superseded within four days}, and each row says which — that is the loop
working, and it is the single most encouraging measurement in this paper. Row 9 is the
instructive exception: it was fixed \emph{in one document}, which then recorded four further
documents still citing the retracted number and declared them out of scope.

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★★★ This revision's rows are a different species, and the difference is the most
important thing in this section.} Every previous row was a claim that \emph{went} stale — true
when written, overtaken later. \textbf{Rows 35, 36 and 37 were false at the moment the previous
revision was published}, by five days, eight days and zero days respectively. The device
enumerated on a stock guest on 2026-08-08; that revision is dated 2026-08-03 and its wall analysis
described a boot that had already been passed by work in the same repository.

\smallskip
⇒ \textbf{The failure was not update latency. It was scope.} The previous revision's own method
section states the rule that would have caught it — \emph{"every claim carried over from the
previous revision was re-measured rather than trusted"} — and applied it to line counts, crate
inventories and two figures supplied in its brief, \textbf{but not to the sentence the document
was built around.} The headline is exactly the claim nobody re-derives, because it is the one
everybody already believes. \emph{This revision's headline should be read with that in mind, and
so should this sentence.}
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{Six of this revision's seven rows are defects in this paper's own previous revision}, and
one of them is — for the \emph{fourth} consecutive time — the sentence that revision was built
around. That is the point rather than an embarrassment to be minimised: a whitepaper is a
description of code and decays at the same rate as any other description of code. \textbf{The
claims that decay fastest are the ones stated most confidently}, because confidence is what stops
the next reader re-checking them.
