\subsection{What this paper could not verify}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{★ What changed at this revision: some things \emph{were} run, and the list of which
  is short.} \measured{} here, on this box, at \code{5c367a38}: all line and crate counts; the
  \code{\#[test]} census; the \code{unsafe} relaxation counts for both audited crates; the entire
  dependency edge list (re-derived from the manifests by script, which is how row 40 was found);
  \code{scripts/claim\_ledger.py --gate} (\textbf{exit 1}); \code{cargo fmt --all --check}
  (\textbf{red, 40 files}); and the PDF build itself. \textbf{Nothing else was executed} — no
  \code{cargo test}, no \code{cargo clippy}, no mutation campaign, no boot.
\item \textbf{Every hardware result in this paper.} \S\ref{sec:ladder}, \S\ref{sec:perf},
  \S\ref{sec:llmwall}, \S\ref{sec:r1} and \S\ref{sec:multitenant} rest on runs this paper did not
  perform and could not perform — the box has no GPU. What was checked here is that each claim
  names a run, a revision, a machine and a committed artifact. \textbf{The artifacts were sampled,
  not audited line by line}; the \code{cup3} doorbell census and the \code{cup8} result lines were
  read from the committed logs directly, the rest were not.
\item \textbf{The current test pass count, and whether the standing red set is still 7.} The 2\,926
  figure is a recorded measurement at \code{9871d315} (2026-08-14), six days before this pin, and
  the red-set count is one that \emph{"moved and was mis-stated twice"} by the project's own
  account. \unver{} here.
\item \textbf{★★★ Anything in \S\ref{sec:llmwall}, which moved five commits in one afternoon
  and twice \emph{after} this paper had described it.} The \code{cudart} green is $n = 1$ against
  a measured 1-in-5 false-negative rate; the fix is capture-derived and pinned to one part and one
  driver; and the object-graph refusals that replaced it have had exactly one boot.
  \emph{This is the section most likely to be wrong by the time it is read}, and it is the reason
  the closing note says what it says.
  \newline ★★★★★ \textbf{THIS PREDICTION WAS CORRECT WITHIN THREE MINUTES.} The object-graph
  refusals were refuted at \code{129dd316} (\S\ref{sec:corrections} row 44) --- \emph{by the
  one-boot control this bullet asked for}, which found them \emph{more} numerous in the boot that
  passes. ⊘ \textbf{Kept as written, because a correct pre-registered doubt that nobody acted on
  is worth more on this list than a corrected sentence.}
\item \textbf{★ Whether the QEMU/KVM backend differential passes.} \S\ref{sec:qemu} reports that
  \file{crates/\allowbreak kayfabe-vmm-qemu/\allowbreak tests/\allowbreak kvm\_differential.rs}
  exists and is the experiment the
  previous revision said only a second backend could run. \textbf{It was not executed here}, so
  whether the "zero trait changes" half of the agnosticism claim held is \unver{}.
\item \textbf{★ The current mutation score, deliberately and by the project's own rule.} The most
  recent campaign to complete is void (\S\ref{sec:mutgate}, Lie 5) and the one before it ran
  against a tree modified underneath it. A bar of 91 is in force; \textbf{no clean campaign has
  completed under it}. There is consequently no defensible score to state, and this document
  states none.
\item \textbf{Anything about NVIDIA's 580 driver that this paper did not read itself.} The
  \S\ref{sec:gsp} claims about 580-vs-610 are taken from
  \file{docs/design/gsp\_580\_correction\_brief.md} and \file{mode2\_gsp\_port\_plan.md} \S14,
  which state their own limit: only 580.159.04 and 610.43.02 are vendored and were read directly.
  Seven further driver tags used to narrow the element-layout break interval are \textbf{relayed
  from another agent's probe and were read by nobody in this chain} \unver{}. The predicate the
  project derived (\code{major >= 610}) is safe under either reading because the 610 boundary is
  the directly-verified one; the sentence "580, 590 and 595 are all on the 48-byte side" is not.
\item \textbf{★ Whether any teardown / re-acquire boot has ever been run.} No commit, log or
  design document records one, and this paper searched. \textbf{That is absence of evidence and is
  reported as such}, not as evidence it was never done. (The \emph{other} question this bullet
  used to hold — the 580 resume handoff — is resolved; see \S\ref{sec:gsp}.)
\item \textbf{"TSan, 0 races."} Not re-run; only the target list and the test count in those
  targets were checked.
\item \textbf{The relationship between the C's 2026-06-16 bare-metal result and the 2026-07-25
  one-process-per-lifetime finding.} The memory note flags this itself: the finding was not
  cross-checked against the bare-metal box, and warns against assuming the C regressed.
\item \textbf{Whether Mode 2 ever produced any display output.} No positive evidence was found,
  only the plan. Absence of evidence.
\item \textbf{★ Anything about the demand ladder or the CPU-RM recorder beyond its own documents}
  (\S\ref{sec:recorder}). The classification is a static reading of the driver source, stated as
  one by its authors; the two numbers this paper prints were re-derived from the committed TSV,
  but the \emph{arguments} behind each row were not re-checked. And ⊘ \textbf{the recorder's
  observational neutrality is not proven by anybody} — its author says so.
\item \textbf{★★ The entire multi-tenancy confidentiality analysis} (\S\ref{sec:multitenant}).
  Every claim in it is \inferred{} from driver source; both source documents open by saying no
  GPU was switched on for them; and the experiment that would settle the central finding — two
  VMs on one box — has never been attempted by anyone.
\item \textbf{Whether \code{memmgrTestCeUtils} is really the next wall.} It is \inferred{} from
  source and \textbf{no boot has ever reached it}. The escape-hatch analysis that makes it
  un-fakeable is a reading, and this paper checked that the readings are cited, not that they are
  right.
\end{itemize}

% ===========================================================================
% ===========================================================================
\section{Open questions --- posed for a reader with no access to this tree}
\label{sec:questions}

\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{Who this section is for.} Someone who knows Linux, kernel internals, NVIDIA's RM object
model and GSP firmware architecture, and systems engineering — and who has never seen this
project. Each question below is written to be \textbf{answerable without our source}: it states
what was measured, on what hardware, what has been \emph{ruled out} and by which experiment, and
what observation would settle it. Where the decisive evidence is a file in this repository, the
question says so and says what is in it.

\smallskip
\textbf{What would be most useful.} Not "have you tried X" — most of the obvious X's are in the
ruled-out rows, which exist for that reason. The valuable answers are of the form \emph{"that
mechanism does not work the way you have assumed, and here is the source that says so"}, or
\emph{"there is a fourth possibility your experiment cannot distinguish"}. \textbf{This project's
single most expensive recurring failure is an instrument that cannot see the thing it is pointed
at}, and every question below is stated with its instrument's limits attached for that reason.
\end{claimbox}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q1 --- Why does \code{torch.\_C.\_cuda\_init()} fail with "CUDA unknown error" when every RM control on the path is served?}

\textbf{Where this stands, because it moved three times in one afternoon.} The CUDA \emph{runtime}
would not initialise in our guest at all: \code{libcuda} answered every call correctly while
\code{libcudart} returned \code{3} from its first. \textbf{That is fixed}
(\S\ref{sec:llmwall}) — \code{cudaGetDeviceCount}, \code{cudaSetDevice}, \code{cudaMalloc} and
\code{cudaDeviceSynchronize} all return 0, and \code{torch.cuda.is\_available()} is \code{True}.
\textbf{And the model still does not run}: \code{torch.\_C.\_cuda\_init()} raises
\code{RuntimeError: CUDA unknown error}, \code{LLM\_TOKENS = 0}.

\textbf{What the failing boot reports.} Host \code{Xid} \textbf{0}; guest \code{NVRM} line count
at the green baseline — \textbf{so this is a refusal we issue, not a hardware fault.} The
control-serving path refused \textbf{nothing}.

\begin{warnbox}\small
⊘⊘⊘ \textbf{CORRECTED 2026-08-20 (\code{129dd316}, \code{dbc6c953}), and the correction removes
this question's most attractive answer.} This paragraph used to continue: \emph{"what did refuse,
and it is a different subsystem entirely, is our object graph
--- \code{PromoteFault::UnknownContextObject} $\times 3$, \code{RmGraphError::FreeUnknown}
$\times 10$, an unmapped allocation class $\times 4$ (\code{0x70}, \code{0x208f}) --- and the
sharpest single line is \code{tsg = mid-miss(h0xa, wrong-kind(Device))}."}
\textbf{Every one of those is baseline.} Run against the boot that \emph{passes}, at the same
revision and the same binary, they count \textbf{4, 14, 6 and 2} respectively --- all
\emph{higher} than in the failing boot. \code{wrong-kind(Device)} is not a defect at all: a
channel's parent is legitimately a TSG \emph{or} a Device, and that exact line appears
byte-identical in a boot eleven days earlier. \textbf{⇒ Do not answer sub-question 3 below about
\code{0x208f}: it is measured innocent by negative control.} What \code{\_cuda\_init()} fails on
is \textbf{unidentified}, and that is the honest state of this question.
\end{warnbox}

★ \textbf{And one datum arrived after this question was written that may or may not belong to it.}
\measured{} \code{w367}, 2026-08-20: a \emph{fourth} sequential CUDA process in one boot fails
with the \textbf{verbatim same string} --- \code{avail=False}, \emph{"CUDA unknown error"} ---
while the first three now pass completely (\S\ref{sec:seqfix}). ⊘ \textbf{Deliberately not
welded to the LLM failure}: the LLM boots died \emph{earlier}, with no completions watched at all,
so the two may share only an error string. \emph{If they are the same defect, this question is
about process ordinality and not about torch at all} --- which is exactly the kind of "there is a
fourth possibility your experiment cannot distinguish" answer this section asks for.

\textbf{The questions.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.3ex]
\item \textbf{What is \emph{"CUDA unknown error"} actually reporting?} It is CUDA's fallback for a
  driver-API status it has no runtime mapping for. \emph{Which} driver-API failures reach the
  caller as that string, and is there a way to recover the underlying \code{CUresult} from
  outside the process?
\item \textbf{What does torch's full CUDA init do that \code{is\_available()} does not?} It walks
  the primary-context path. \textbf{Which RM objects and controls does primary-context creation
  require beyond what \code{cuDevicePrimaryCtxRetain} alone touches} — given that plain
  \code{cuDevicePrimaryCtxRetain} \emph{succeeds} in the same guest?
\item \textbf{Is \code{0x208f} what we think it is?} It is \code{NV20\_SUBDEVICE\_DIAG}. ⚠ It was
  mis-identified once here as the shared-user-data class (\code{0xDE}) by a substring match
  across two adjacent log lines — \emph{a substring match in a log is not an identity.} Does
  \code{cudart} or torch require a diagnostic subdevice, and if refused, does that surface as an
  init failure?
\item \textbf{Is \code{cudart} the whole interface?} Our differential covers \file{/dev/nvidia*}
  \code{ioctl} traffic and nothing else. \textbf{\code{mmap} results, \file{/proc}, \file{/sys}
  and all UVM behaviour are outside it} — the largest unexamined surface we know of.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ And the two transferable findings from getting this far, which we would most like
checked, because if they generalise they apply to any differential oracle built against a
reference system.}

\smallskip
\textbf{(1) The minimal fatal set was a PAIR, and no one-at-a-time experiment could find it.}
Three controls, each measured \emph{innocent alone} on a working host — and refusing two of them
together reproduces the failure exactly. A per-id bisection reported all six candidates innocent
and \textbf{was right about every one}. Only the full subset lattice found it. \emph{Is
"individually necessary, jointly sufficient" a recognised shape here, and is there a cheaper
search than the lattice?}

\smallskip
\textbf{(2) One member of the ladder is invisible to any experiment driven by a healthy
reference.} \code{0x2080a001} occurs \textbf{zero times} in an unmodified host trace — it is the
\emph{fallback} the guest reaches only after two earlier controls are refused. So the reference
capture, which is the whole instrument, cannot contain it. \textbf{The countermeasure we found is
to drive the reference INTO the failure and re-capture}, which then also produced a zero-boot
reproduction (refuse the fatal pair on bare metal, get the guest's exact failure in seconds).
\emph{Is that a named failure mode of differential debugging with a better countermeasure?}
\end{warnbox}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q2 --- Where does a slow host RM verb run, when every entry point is a vCPU trap under the big lock?}

\textbf{The constraint, which we believe is structural rather than a wiring gap.} Every guest MMIO
write into our emulated device arrives on a vCPU thread holding QEMU's Big QEMU Lock. Blocking
there freezes \emph{every vCPU and QEMU's main loop} — so the binding constraint is not any
NVIDIA timeout, it is the guest kernel's own soft-lockup and RCU-stall detectors, which see the
whole VM stop. Three further facts close the obvious escapes:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0.2ex]
\item The reply must be produced \textbf{synchronously by type}: the policy hook returns
  \code{Option<Reply>} to the ring FSM, with no deferred-reply channel. A deferred queue was
  considered and rejected in source, on the argument that \emph{a deferred reply is state that can
  be lost, reordered or double-sent}.
\item Posting into the GSP ring needs \code{\&mut} access to state living inside the rank-0 lock,
  so a second thread posting a reply is a lock-order inversion \emph{by construction}.
\item Our own invariant R1 forbids issuing a blocking call while any ranked lock is held, and it
  is asserted, always-on, with a panic.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{This is not hypothetical.} \measured{} 2026-08-20, on the first boot that wired a host
forward end to end for a real control: the process aborted with \emph{"R1
no-blocking-under-lock violation: issuing a host RM verb while holding rank(s) [0]"}.
\textbf{The wiring was correct; the architecture refused it.}

\textbf{Ruled out.} Budgeting the work — which \emph{did} fix an analogous case, an unbounded
teardown loop, by splitting it into 40\,ms slices — \textbf{does not generalise}: that work was
\emph{ours and divisible}, whereas a forwarded host RM verb is one call with one latency and no
budget can split it. Using the one existing off-vCPU thread is also out: it is handed a
\textbf{read-only closure by type} and structurally cannot issue a verb.

\textbf{The question.} For a device model whose guest-visible protocol is request/reply over a
memory ring, and whose replies are generated inside a BQL-held MMIO trap, \textbf{what is the
correct architecture for a bounded-but-slow host operation?} Specific sub-questions we cannot
answer from inside the problem: does QEMU's lockless-MMIO path (\code{memory\_region\_enable\_lockless\_io()},
10.2) actually change this, or only remove the BQL for the \emph{dispatch} while any state we
touch re-serialises anyway? Is there prior art for \textbf{returning "retry" to a guest driver
that is polling a ring}, without desynchronising its sequence numbers? And is the guest driver's
own RPC receive path tolerant of a reply arriving on a later poll than the one that would first
have observed it?

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q3 --- Does host RM fence the engine before \code{NV01\_FREE} of a channel returns?}

\textbf{Why it matters.} We refuse the guest's channel-stop verbs (Q4 context: they name host
hardware state we cannot produce). UVM's own source says the stop exists to \emph{"prevent
spurious MMU faults during teardown"} — and then discards the status and unmaps anyway. So the
question is whether the free path itself provides the fence.

\textbf{What is established from open source.} CPU-RM's channel destructor was read in full and
contains \textbf{no stop, no preempt, no idle-wait and no channel-halt} — it issues a synchronous
free RPC to the GSP and returns. \textbf{GSP's half is closed firmware.}

\textbf{The experiment, fully pre-registered, needs no VM and no part of this project.} On bare
metal: run a channel writing a monotonically increasing counter into a mapped buffer for
$\geq 2$\,s; issue \code{NV01\_FREE} on the channel with \emph{no} prior stop; sample the counter
at $t_0$, $+50$\,ms, $+500$\,ms and $+2$\,s. \textbf{Three outcomes, not two}: frozen at $t_0$
(RM fences); still advancing at $+2$\,s (it does not, and the exposure is real); or it stops
somewhere in between (the question becomes \emph{what the bound is}).

\textbf{Two controls the experiment must carry.} A positive control with an explicit stop first.
And an \textbf{authorship} control proving the GPU, not the CPU, wrote the counter — via a report
timestamp tracking the GPU's own clock, \textbf{not} a hardware watchpoint, because \emph{a DMA
write is invisible to x86 debug registers}. (We learned that the expensive way: a watchpoint is a
negative control only.)

\textbf{If you know the answer from documentation or experience, it saves a bench day and it is
severity-informative for every Mode-2-style project, not only this one.}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q4 --- Two unprivileged host processes at the same euid, each holding \file{/dev/nvidiactl}: what actually separates them?}

\textbf{The setting.} Our design gives every guest process its own sandboxed, unprivileged host
worker holding its own RM client. Two \emph{different guests} therefore become two host processes
— \textbf{at the same euid}, because nothing in the system currently assigns them different ones.

\textbf{What we read from the open driver.} RM's cross-client validation compares
\code{\{euid, pid\}} with an \textbf{OR}, so a matching euid alone passes. And client handles are
not guessing-hard: the encoding is a fixed base OR'd with a monotonically increasing index, so
concurrently-created clients receive \emph{adjacent} values.

\textbf{Two narrowings we found, which are reassuring and are not a fix.} RM's default
cross-client \emph{dup} policy is PID-scoped, not euid-scoped, so the dup path across tenants is
refused even at matching euid — leaving the direct-naming path. And residual-data-on-VRAM-reuse
does not exist on the paths we drive: the driver scrubs on free and a frame with a pending scrub
is not allocatable.

\textbf{The questions.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.3ex]
\item \textbf{Is the same-euid direct-naming path exploitable end to end?} We have never run the
  experiment — two unprivileged processes at one uid, one naming the other's client handle. If you
  have, we would like the result more than almost anything else in this section.
\item \textbf{Does one-uid-per-isolate actually close it?} Our proposed mitigation is a deployment
  policy — run each worker at a distinct non-zero uid — which moves the boundary from our code
  into the kernel's. \textbf{What else in RM keys on euid} such that distinct uids would break
  something legitimate, or such that it would \emph{not} close the hole?
\item \textbf{What is the right threat model for an unprivileged process holding
  \file{/dev/nvidiactl}?} We assert that every effect a hostile guest can produce is an effect a
  local unprivileged process could already produce. Is that assertion defensible?
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{⊘ Stated plainly so nobody over-reads the rest of this paper}: every claim in this area is
\inferred{} from driver source. \textbf{No GPU was switched on for any of it, and the single
experiment that would settle it — two VMs on one box — has never been attempted by anyone.} There
is also no \code{seccomp} anywhere in the tree; it is absent and named as absent.

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q5 --- What is the observable event that says a GPU virtual address range's backing may be released?}

\textbf{The design assumed the guest's own unmap.} \textbf{It does not occur.} \measured{}
2026-08-15 and again 2026-08-19: \textbf{CUDA's suballocator does not unmap on \code{cuMemFree}}.
A workload that allocates and frees repeatedly ends with the guest VAS holding \emph{one} 140\,MiB
run covering both buffers; the release trigger the design nominated fires \textbf{zero times} on
every workload tested, and the shipped default was therefore \emph{behaviourally identical to
disabling it}.

\textbf{The consequence, and it is the sharpest failure this project has diagnosed.} A freed
allocation's framebuffer frames stay bound forever. The guest recycles them; our machinery
correctly refuses a second binding for a range already bound; the mapping goes stale; the next
write into it kills the channel — \textbf{with no \code{Xid} and no driver log line, because
nothing ever reaches hardware}. Failure is therefore a function of \textbf{allocation history, not
allocation size}: the same 64\,MiB allocation succeeds alone and fails when a 28\,MiB allocation
preceded it. \emph{We spent a campaign looking for a size cliff that does not exist.}

\textbf{What works now, and why it is not an answer.} A \emph{takeover} — when the guest re-points
a range we already hold, we release and re-install at the same key inside one operation. It works
(measured 7 rows versus 0, zero stale refusals versus 32, zero \code{Xid} versus one). But it is
capped, and \textbf{that cap is load-bearing}: without it the settlement re-proposes the
superseded row and ping-pongs host operations forever. \textbf{Which of two live aliases should
win is unproven.}

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{UPDATED 2026-08-20 (\code{cca2eb4b}), and the update is part of the question.} The cap
used to be \textbf{4 per frame, reset by nothing}, and that key was wrong in a way that produced a
\emph{hardware fault of our own making}: a device-lifetime budget spent by early actors left a
later process's leaves fabricated with no host backing, and the copy engine wrote into one ---
\code{Xid 31 FAULT\_PDE} at \code{0x7e59\_c6000000}, traced to a frame our own log had marked
\code{CAPPED} (\S\ref{sec:seqfix}). It is now keyed by \code{(phys, taking va)}: a ping-pong
between one pair of aliases is still bounded at $2\times$\,CAP, while a genuinely new owner gets
its own budget. ⚠ \textbf{A separate defect of the same shape was found in the same campaign} ---
a join left behind by an \emph{exited} process, named by no address-table row at all, which the
per-address-space takeover could not reach by construction. That one is now reclaimed when nothing
names the frame. \emph{Both are workarounds for the missing event this question asks about.}

\textbf{The question.} \emph{Is there any protocol-visible event, on a GSP-client part, that means
"this GPU VA range's backing is no longer referenced"?} We have measured that TLB invalidates do
not appear on this path at all — both transports counted zero — and that \code{MAP\_MEMORY\_DMA}
is stubbed out through the entire HAL chain on GSP clients. If the honest answer is \emph{"no such
event exists and you must reference-count what you observe"}, that is a useful answer and we will
take it. \textbf{What we want to avoid is building a reclamation model on a second event that also
never fires.}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q6 --- Is a guest-memory region lock implementable on arm64 at all?}

\textbf{The mechanism on x86.} To read a guest page table safely we want to make a guest-physical
range temporarily unwritable and have the resulting fault be \emph{decodable} by the VMM, so that
a guest write during a walk is stalled rather than racing.

\textbf{The reading that says it cannot work on arm64.} On a stage-2 permission fault caused by
the guest's own \emph{page-table walker}, ESR's ISV bit is 0 — the syndrome carries no instruction
information — so KVM cannot hand the VMM a decodable MMIO exit and instead injects an abort into
the guest. \textbf{If that reading is right, the region lock does not exist on arm64}, and our own
documents record the contradiction in writing: \emph{"'arm64 kept' and 'the capability is refused
on arm64' cannot both hold."} Three mutually exclusive standings were written the same day and
none was chosen.

\textbf{The questions.} Is the ISV=0 reading correct and complete for current kernels? Is there
\emph{any} arm64 mechanism that gives a hypervisor a decodable, resumable stall on a
guest-page-table write — stage-2 permission plus a software walker, hardware dirty-state
management, something in the SMMU, or an approach we have not considered? And if not:
\textbf{what do other hypervisors that need to read guest page tables safely do on arm64?}

\textbf{⊘ One honest note that may make this cheaper than it looks.} Our own analysis concluded
the region lock may have \textbf{no members at all} — that is, no call site actually needs it — in
which case the contradiction is moot and the right move is to build nothing. \textbf{We would
rather learn that the primitive is unnecessary than pick one of three standings.}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q7 --- What host-side state, fixed at boot and stable thereafter, varies performance by 9$\times$?}

\textbf{The measurement.} Same workload, same binary, same box, three consecutive boots: the
guest's submit-side cost was \textbf{85.32, 9.41 and 64.39\,ms} — \textbf{a 9.1$\times$ spread
with the middle boot fastest}. Across twelve boots that day it spanned \textbf{6.3 to 216\,ms}.
\textbf{Within any one boot the iterations agree to $\pm 2\%$}, so the value is decided at boot
and then holds. A different statistic on the same twelve boots held to $\pm 0.8\%$.

\textbf{Ruled out.} A session ramp from accumulating host state: the sequence is \emph{non-monotone},
and free memory and low-order page availability \emph{improved} after the slow boot while the cost
stayed high.

\textbf{What we suspect but have not shown.} The dominant cost in the slow case is pinning guest
RAM page by page, and the per-operation count tracks \emph{physical contiguity} of the backing —
so host page-allocator fragmentation at the moment the guest's memory backing is created is the
obvious candidate. \textbf{The question is what to measure to confirm or kill it}, and what a
device model can do about it: is there a way to obtain, or to ask the kernel to preserve,
physically contiguous backing for a large memfd on a long-uptime host, short of \code{hugetlbfs}
reservations at boot?

\textbf{⊘ One trap already paid for here.} \code{MADV\_HUGEPAGE} on a \textbf{memfd} returns
\textbf{0} and delivers \textbf{zero} huge pages: a memfd is shmem, and shmem transparent huge
pages are a \emph{separate} sysfs knob that commonly ships disabled. \emph{The syscall's return
value is not the measurement}; the mapped-huge-page counter is. A prescribed fix in one of our own
design documents was inert as written for exactly this reason.

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q8 --- We are the GSP. The guest kernel driver trusts the GSP completely. Is that a confused deputy?}

\textbf{The structure.} The open NVIDIA kernel driver applies essentially no validation to GSP RPC
replies — it is talking to firmware on a device it already trusts with full DMA. \textbf{In this
architecture, we occupy that position}, and the data we return is derived, in part, from what the
guest's own \emph{userspace} asked for.

\textbf{The question, which nothing in our audit set currently asks:} \textbf{can guest userspace
steer the values we hand the guest kernel such that the guest kernel violates an invariant its own
source assumes?} The interesting cases are not memory-safety-obvious ones; they are fields the
driver uses as sizes, counts, indices or capability bits without re-checking, where the value's
provenance is ultimately an unprivileged request.

\textbf{One instance of this class we found and closed, offered as the shape to look for.} At
driver 580 the guest reads an element-count field \emph{we choose} directly into a copy loop whose
staging area is a fixed small number of elements, with the live ring metadata as the very next
byte. A count one larger than the staging area overwrites metadata the guest is actively using; at
the ring's reachable maximum it writes 188\,416 bytes past a kernel allocation. \textbf{That is
guest-kernel heap corruption reachable from a value we pick.} It is now refused by a bound derived
from the layout rather than a literal, checked on both the send and receive sides.

\textbf{We do not believe that is the only one.} The general question for a reader who knows this
driver: \textbf{which fields in the GSP$\to$CPU direction does the kernel driver consume without
validation, and which of them can an emulated GSP choose freely?}

% --------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection*{Q9 --- Smaller, sharply-posed items}

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0.5ex]
\item \textbf{Driver-version drift.} Our supported cross-product is currently \textbf{one point}:
  one GPU (GA106), one guest driver version, one host driver version, one guest at a time.
  \textbf{The supported drift range is unmeasured}, and the discriminating experiment is cheap —
  pin the host, walk the guest driver back through earlier majors, and record \emph{what} refuses.
  Nobody has run it. \emph{Is there a documented compatibility contract between an NVIDIA guest
  driver and the GSP firmware it talks to, or is version-lockstep the only safe assumption?}
\item \textbf{The second GPU generation will fail before it starts.} Our second architecture's
  page-table format currently delegates to a \emph{test double with an invented encoding}, while
  the first is checked against NVIDIA's own generator. \textbf{Compile the second generation's
  format against the vendor source as an oracle \emph{before} booting it}, or the boot reports
  "it broke" without reporting where. \emph{Is the GMMU page-table format stable across Ampere and
  Ada, and where is the authoritative encoding in the open modules?}
\item \textbf{A negative fixture that has never been replayed.} The predecessor implementation
  parsed \textbf{378 GSP ring elements out of arbitrary guest RAM and answered success} — a
  guest-reachable defect, kept as a recorded trace. The rewrite has a named refusal vocabulary
  that \emph{should} reject every one of them. \textbf{No test replays the fixture against it}, so
  the immunity is argued from types and not measured. \emph{This is the cheapest open item in this
  section and we are listing it to be embarrassed into closing it.}
\item \textbf{No teardown or driver-reload boot has ever been run}, and our core has \textbf{no
  reset entry point of any kind}: on a guest driver reload the graph, every live process with its
  sandboxes, arenas and host handles, both routing maps and the guest-physical window all survive
  into the next driver's life with nothing reclaimed. The recommendation to spend the first
  successful boot on a teardown was made on 2026-08-05; many dozens of successful boots later, no
  such run is recorded. \emph{Nothing surfaces this incidentally, which is exactly why it is
  still open.}
\item \textbf{What kills the fourth CUDA process, when the first three now pass identically?}
  \measured{} \code{w367}, 2026-08-20 (\S\ref{sec:seqfix}): three sequential guest CUDA
  processes complete with \emph{identical} doorbell counts and 8/8 completions each, host
  \code{Xid} 0. The fourth reports \code{avail=False}, \emph{"CUDA unknown error"}. \textbf{Only
  three processes ever existed for four workloads}, so the fourth never reached the device; the
  guest kernel logged nothing while it ran. \textbf{It fails above the \code{ioctl} boundary},
  which is precisely where our instruments stop --- our differential covers \file{/dev/nvidia*}
  \code{ioctl} traffic and nothing else. \emph{What state does \code{libcuda} or the guest kernel
  driver keep that is per-boot rather than per-process, and exhausts or wedges after three
  contexts?} ⚠ $n = 1$ boot, and the run's logs are not committed.
\item \textbf{A quadratic control plane, measured.} Deriving process boundaries is
  $O(\text{live objects})$ per event, so $N$ events cost $O(N^2)$: \textbf{1\,000 events 0.85\,s,
  8\,000 events 54.8\,s} on a debug build, against a cap of $2^{18}$ live handles. Deleting the
  per-event clone saves ${\sim}24\%$ and leaves the curve quadratic. \emph{The unmeasured quantity
  that decides whether this is a denial-of-service or a real workload's ceiling is: how many RM
  objects does a CUDA or PyTorch process hold live simultaneously?} We have never measured it, and
  an earlier claim that PyTorch startup reaches the curve benignly was an inference and is
  withdrawn.
\end{itemize}

\begin{warnbox}\small
\textbf{★★★ And one meta-question, which is the one we would most like an outsider's view on.}
Every question above is stated with a named instrument and that instrument's known limits. This
project's central finding — measured roughly twenty-five times — is that \textbf{an instrument's
null and the system's success are the same reading}, and that the failures are
\emph{asymmetric}: a broken instrument almost always makes the system look \emph{better} than it
is, because a mutant that is not run, a capture that is empty, a gate quantified over the wrong
set, and a universal over an empty set all read as green.

\smallskip
Our working countermeasures are: \textbf{plant one reachable violation per class before trusting
any audit} (a scan that misses its own plant is a broken instrument, not a clean bill of health);
\textbf{discriminate on counts, never on a diff} (a vocabulary diff came back empty in both
directions while the explaining quantity sat in the log at 32 against a baseline of 16); and
\textbf{pre-register three-valued falsifiers}, because the third outcome — \emph{the arm never
fired at all} — is usually the likely one and a two-valued test cannot see it.

\smallskip
\textbf{Is there a better discipline than this?} We arrived at it by paying for each rule
individually and we do not know whether it is the state of the art or a reinvention of something
with a literature.
\end{warnbox}

\appendix
\section{Measurement method}
\label{sec:method}

Every number in this paper that is not attributed to a document was produced by one of the
following, run against the kayfabe tree at \code{5c367a38} and
\file{/workspace/nvidia-gpu-passthrough} at \code{c86b69d}. \textbf{No compilation, no test
execution, no GPU and no network access was involved}: the box has no GPU and 16\,GB of free
disk, so a \code{cargo build} was ruled out before writing began rather than skipped afterwards.
Two things that need \emph{no} compilation \emph{were} run and are marked \measured{} in the text:
\code{scripts/claim\_ledger.py --gate} and \code{cargo fmt --all --check}.

\smallskip
⚠ \textbf{And one hazard this revision hit that previous ones did not: the tree moved underneath
it.} A commit landed mid-session that changed the answer to \S\ref{sec:llmwall} — the pin was
\code{034154fd} when the front matter was drafted and \code{5c367a38} when it was finished, and
the LLM-wall paragraph was rewritten twice as a result. \textbf{Everything is re-measured at the
final pin}, but a reader should treat \emph{"pinned at a commit"} as a claim about a moving ref
unless the working tree was also clean — which the project itself learned when a rung reported
\emph{"byte-identical to master"} and \code{master} had advanced 30 files underneath it.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{AND IT KEPT MOVING AFTER THE DOCUMENT WAS COMMITTED, WHICH IS THE STRONGER FORM OF THE
SAME HAZARD.} Twelve further commits landed between the pin (\code{5c367a38}, 15:36) and this
paper's first reading (\code{56dc01f3}, 20:32), and two of them falsified headline claims. The
corrections are folded in above the text they correct rather than appended, and are listed as
\S\ref{sec:corrections} rows 43--45. ⚠ \textbf{No structural quantity in this paper was
re-measured at \code{56dc01f3}} --- the commands in the table below were run at \code{5c367a38}
and their outputs are pinned there. ⇒ \emph{"Re-measured at the final pin" is a claim with a
timestamp, and the timestamp expires.}

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.6cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Command} \\
\midrule
implementation lines & \code{find crates -path '*/src/*' -name '*.rs' | xargs wc -l} \\
test lines & the same over \code{tests}, \code{crates/*/tests}, \code{fuzz} \\
per-crate lines & the same, per \code{crates/<name>} \\
\code{\#[test]} sites & \code{grep -rc '\#[test]'} over workspace members, excluding the
  detached \code{crates/kayfabe-abi/gen} and the \code{trybuild} ui fixtures \\
\code{unsafe} relaxations & \code{grep -rhoE 'unsafe (\{|fn |impl |trait )'
  crates/kayfabe-linux-raw/src/*\_unsafe.rs | wc -l} — the same expression the CI ratchet uses \\
dependency edges & every \code{Cargo.toml} read in full, cross-checked against \code{Cargo.lock} \\
commit counts and dates & \code{git rev-list --count HEAD}, \code{git log --format=\%ci} \\
document staleness & \code{git log -1 --format=\%ci} per file, compared against the commit that
  invalidated the claim \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

The five figures are TikZ, drawn from the measurements above and from a function-by-function read
of the call chains they depict; they are vector, not traced from an image. Figure~\ref{fig:deps}
in particular is generated from the manifest edge list rather than from any design document, which
is why it disagrees with the hexagon picture about which crates are "ports".

\textbf{Two method rules carried forward, both prompted by the figures being where the drift was
worst.} Figure~\ref{fig:datapath} names \emph{symbols} rather than \code{file:line} pins, as the
repository itself did to its own 105 pins in \code{3fd1455}. And Figure~\ref{fig:deps}'s edge list
is re-derived from scratch every revision rather than edited — it was re-derived by script this
time, which is how the \code{mocks}\,$\rightarrow$\,\code{abi} edge and the new topological
ordering were picked up without being looked for.

\textbf{One method rule carried forward.} Every claim carried over from the previous revision was
re-measured rather than trusted, \emph{including the ones this paper itself originated}. That is
the only reason rows 27, 28, 30 and 33 exist — all four are claims this document asserted with no
hedge, and all four had gone stale in the \emph{understating} direction, which nobody audits for.
\textbf{Re-derive, do not restate}, because a restated number and a measured number are
indistinguishable on the page.

\textbf{★ One method rule added at this revision, and it earned two corrections immediately.}
\emph{The brief is a source like any other and is re-derived, not obeyed.} Two figures supplied
to this revision as settled facts were checked against the tree and found wrong
(\S\ref{sec:corrections} rows 31--32); one was a number that appears in no document, and one was
a blanket where the exceptions carried the actual argument. Neither is a criticism of whoever
wrote the brief — both were close to right, and the second was a fair summary of an earlier
document that a later measurement had superseded. \textbf{The rule is that an instruction and a
citation decay the same way.}

\paragraph{★★★ The claim ledger was run against this tree and it is RED.}
\code{scripts/claim\_ledger.py --gate} exits \textbf{1} at \code{5c367a38} with \textbf{1\,166
unattributed / 90 conflated / 36 bare-hardware} against bars of 381 / 66 / 17
(\S\ref{sec:ledger}). The previous revision reported this gate green with all three bars holding;
that is no longer the case, and the bars have not moved since 2026-07-31. ⊘ \textbf{And the result
is weaker than it looks in both directions}: the ledger's universe is \code{*.rs} and \code{*.md},
so \textbf{this file is not in it} — the gate says nothing whatever about the whitepaper — and its
universe now includes the committed bench-trace READMEs, so part of the growth is denominator and
not regression. \emph{Which part, this paper did not measure.}

\vfill
\begin{claimbox}\small
\textbf{One closing note on how to use this paper.} If you are here to find what is wrong with
kayfabe, start at \textbf{\S\ref{sec:questions}} — the open questions are written to be attacked
by someone with no access to this tree, and each carries the evidence needed to attack it. Then,
in order: \S\ref{sec:seqfix} (the paper's headline blocker, fixed and measured passing five hours
after this document was committed), \S\ref{sec:llmwall} (the LLM wall --- ⊘ \textbf{this line
used to read} \emph{"the CUDA runtime does not initialise, and the fix is unbooted"}, which
contradicted that very section \emph{at this pin}: the wall broke, the model still produces no
token, and the cause named there was refuted, \S\ref{sec:corrections} rows 44--45),
\S\ref{sec:perf} (the performance picture, and why one statistic grades two flags backwards),
\S\ref{sec:ladder} (what "first compute" does and does not mean — every green is a relaxed green
and the shipping default is not among the relaxations), \S\ref{sec:contact} (what contact with
hardware bought, as a conjunction rather than either half), \S\ref{sec:oraclelimits} (the oracle
is positively wrong about a class of rows and a larger class is unmeasured), \S\ref{sec:r1} (an
invariant broken on the production path under a green suite), \S\ref{sec:multitenant} (there is
no tenant axis, and the thesis is multi-tenancy), \S\ref{sec:ledger} and \S\ref{sec:mutgate} (the
evidence machinery, wrong five times and currently red), and \S\ref{sec:oracle} (an independent
oracle that was independently written and identically wrong). Then the code — starting with
\code{VerbPlan::gated\_doorbell}, \code{Spine::apply}, \code{rmrpc::translate} and
\code{kayfabe\_fwd::plan\_ce}. Do not start with \file{README.md}, and do not use
\file{crate\_maturity\_map.md} for anything (\S\ref{sec:corrections} row 29).

\smallskip
\textbf{And one note on how to read its confidence, which this revision has the sharpest version
of.} Each of the five revisions of this document has found its predecessor wrong in several
places, and \textbf{for four consecutive revisions the falsified claim was the one the predecessor
led with}: a disconnected crate, then the bridge, then the guest reaching the bridge, then the
execution plane that \emph{"cannot be faked"}. All four were crossed. \textbf{Worse, this
revision found that three of its predecessor's headline claims were false \emph{on the day it was
published}}, not merely overtaken later (\S\ref{sec:corrections}, rows 35--37) — so the failure
mode is not update latency, it is that \textbf{nobody re-derives the sentence everybody already
believes.}

\smallskip
This revision's lead claim was that the remaining wall is \emph{no longer} a question about what
NVIDIA's protocol wants — it is our own object model, reached through closed CUDA userspace. It is
the fifth answer to "what is the critical path", \textbf{and it changed twice in the hours this
document took to write}, the second time because a milestone had been graded on a proxy.

\smallskip
⊘ \textbf{AND IT CHANGED A THIRD TIME, THREE MINUTES AFTER THIS DOCUMENT WAS COMMITTED, AND A
FOURTH FIVE HOURS AFTER THAT.} \emph{"Our own object model"} is refuted --- every refusal in it is
more numerous in the boot that passes (\S\ref{sec:corrections} row 44). \emph{"A second CUDA
context sequentially in one boot still hangs"} is fixed --- three sequential processes pass
(row 43, \S\ref{sec:seqfix}). \textbf{The fifth answer lasted three minutes and there is no
sixth}: what \code{torch.\_C.\_cuda\_init()} fails on, and what kills a \emph{fourth} CUDA
process, are both \textbf{unidentified}. ⇒ \textbf{The base rate did not merely hold; it
accelerated.} \textbf{Treat every unhedged sentence here exactly the way the previous four
deserved to be treated} — including this one, and including that one.
\end{claimbox}
